License: CC BY 4.0 arXiv:2608.15645v1 [cs.LG] 16 Aug 2026
Yorgos Felekis, Paris Giampouras, Fabio Massimo Zennaro and Theodoros Damoulas
Generalised Transportability via Causal Abstractions
Yorgos Felekis yorgos.felekis@warwick.ac.uk Affiliation: Department of Computer Science Affiliation: University of Warwick Affiliation: Coventry, CV4 7AL, UK Paris Giampouras paris.giampouras@warwick.ac.uk Affiliation: Department of Computer Science Affiliation: University of Warwick Affiliation: Coventry, CV4 7AL, UK Fabio Massimo Zennaro fabio.zennaro@uib.no Affiliation: Department of Informatics Affiliation: University of Bergen Affiliation: Bergen, N-5020, NO Theodoros Damoulas t.damoulas@warwick.ac.uk Affiliation: Departments of Computer Science & Statistics Affiliation: University of Warwick Affiliation: Coventry, CV4 7AL, UK
Abstract
Transporting a causal conclusion from a source study population to a target one is a fundamental problem in causal inference. The theory of transportability provides a criterion for when this is possible: given experimental data from the source, observational data from the target, and a diagram showing where the two populations differ, it determines whether a target query is identifiable and does so completely; i.e., if the query can be transported, the criterion finds the exact formula. However, it works one query at a time and answers only whether transport is possible, without providing the query value. It is also silent in two practically important regimes: when the query is not transportable, even given target data, and when no target data exist at all. To tackle both, we take a model-level perspective on the transportability problem grounded in Causal Abstraction theory. Source and target share variables, graph, and interventions, differing only at a known set of mechanisms, which makes transportability a special case of same-level abstraction. Thus, instead of asking whether one query transports, we ask whether a single map aligns the source and target across their interventional behaviour. We characterise when such a map exists in both the Markovian and semi-Markovian settings; when it does, every target query transports at once. Our main contribution lies in the approximate case. When no exact map exists, the best approximate one still yields certified query intervals , recasting abstraction error as a quantitative notion of approximate transportability . We formulate model-level transport as distributionally robust optimisation over mechanism and environment perturbations of the unseen target and derive certificates that cover both challenging regimes: bounds for non-transportable queries and guarantees under target-agnostic settings. We evaluate the framework on synthetic Markovian and semi-Markovian benchmarks and a real ecological dataset, and we show that the certified intervals bracket the true interventional query.

1 Introduction

Because τ \tau is only approximate, the transported value Q τ ​ # ​ s = 𝔼 τ # ​ ℙ s ​ [ Y ∣ d ​ o ​ ( X ) ] Q_{\tau\#s}=\mathbb{E}_{\tau_{\#}\mathbb{P}_{s}}[Y\mid do(X)] need not coincide with the true target value. Instead, it anchors the certified target interval [ ℓ , L ] [\ell,L] , shown in red, which contains Q t ⋆ Q_{t}^{\star} for every admissible target.
A clinical study in a metropolitan hospital network estimates the effect of a new respiratory therapy on patient recovery. Before deploying it in a rural region with a different age distribution and air quality characteristics, a natural question is whether the estimated population effect still applies, and if not, how it should be adjusted. This is the classical question of external validity : whether a causal conclusion established under the conditions in which it was studied continues to hold under different ones. In the experimental-design literature ( Campbell and Stanley, 1966 ; Shadish et al., 2001 ) , external validity is contrasted with internal validity : generalisation beyond the study conditions versus correct identification within them. Two regimes of external validity are usually distinguished ( Degtiar and Rose, 2021 ) : generalisability , which involves transferring a finding from a sample to the broader source population from which it was drawn, and transportability , which involves transferring it to a distinct target population that differs from the source. The theory of causal transportability ( Pearl and Bareinboim, 2011 ; Bareinboim and Pearl, 2016 ) gives this second regime its modern graphical formalisation, as a form of formal criterion to transfer causal effects learned from experiments in a source population to a target where only observational data can be collected. Source and target domains are taken to share the same causal graph but differ through a known subset of mechanisms, marked by a selection diagram. The do-calculus then provides a complete query-level criterion ( Bareinboim and Pearl, 2013 ) : for a given target query, it either derives an identification formula expressing it in terms of source experimental and target observational data or proves that no such formula exists. The theory has been extended to multiple source domains ( Bareinboim and Pearl, 2016 ) and to finite-sample statistical questions ( Correa and Bareinboim, 2019 ) . It has also been generalised to related data-fusion regimes in which experiments are available only on a restricted set of controllable variables, including z z -transportability ( Lee and Honavar, 2013 ) . More broadly, it connects to the problem of generalisation across environments ( Pearl and Bareinboim, 2014 ; Rojas-Carulla et al., 2018 ; Magliacane et al., 2018 ) .
This query-level view is precise and powerful within its scope, but it is often uninformative in two practically important regimes. The first ( ℛ 1 \mathcal{R}_{1} ) is when a target query is not identifiable. In the metropolitan study, suppose an unmeasured factor such as socioeconomic status influences both who receives the therapy and how well they recover. The do-calculus may then return “not transportable” for the effect of interest, not because nothing can be said about the target effect, but because there is no exact identifying formula to return. The second regime ( ℛ 2 \mathcal{R}_{2} ) is when no target data are available at all: the rural region has not yet been sampled, or one wishes to commit to a deployment policy that remains valid across a whole family of plausible target regions. In that case, even queries that are classically transportable cannot be evaluated because their identifying formulas reference target-side quantities that have not been observed. In both regimes, a method that returns a single binary verdict per query leaves the practitioner with little actionable information.
We argue that in these regimes, the natural object of study is not the individual query but the relation between the source and target models. The transportability setting is highly structured: source and target share variables, graph, and intervention semantics and differ only through a known subset of mechanisms. Structure-preserving relations between causal models are exactly the object of Causal Abstraction (CA) theory ( Rubenstein et al., 2017 ; Beckers and Halpern, 2019 ) , which formalises maps between causal models that describe the same system at different resolutions. Thus, the transportability setting is a special, same-resolution instance of it. Causal Abstraction Learning (CAL) ( Zennaro et al., 2022 ) aims to recover abstraction maps directly from data, enabling cross-scale representation learning and the integration of heterogeneous evidence. Early CAL methods established distinct computational frameworks for learning approximate abstractions from data: joint differentiable programming ( Zennaro et al., 2023 ) and multi-marginal optimal transport ( Felekis et al., 2024 ) , with subsequent approaches broadening the class of abstractions and objectives ( Kekić et al., 2024 ; Xia and Bareinboim, 2024 ; D’Acunto et al., 2025 ) . Causal abstractions have been applied across domains, from climate modelling ( Chalupka et al., 2017 ) and neural-network interpretability ( Geiger et al., 2021 ) to bandits ( Zennaro et al., 2024 ) , surrogate modelling ( Dyer et al., 2024 ) , and causal discovery ( Massidda et al., 2024 ) .
Our setting is a constrained, same-resolution instance of the general CA picture: the relevant map is automorphic , acting as the identity on the invariant parts of the model and only locally on the shifted mechanisms. There is no aggregation of variables, no change of granularity, and no nontrivial translation between interventions. Concretely, in our example, it leaves the recovery mechanism untouched and re-expresses only the shifted demographic and environmental mechanisms, so that a single map carries every interventional query from the metropolitan source to the rural target at once. We therefore ask whether the target model is an automorphic abstraction of the source. When such a map exists, it transports the full source interventional family to the target and hence transports all causal queries simultaneously. The converse need not hold: several individual queries may be classically transportable while no single common map explains them all. We call this the compatibility gap . When no exact map exists, a best approximate one always does, and its residual error is what we turn into certified bounds: the question then shifts from whether an automorphic abstraction exists to how reliably the best one transports and what its approximation error certifies about the target queries.
The value of this perspective depends on the setting. In the Markovian case with target data, every query is already transportable by direct adjustment, whereas in the semi-Markovian case, some queries admit no identifying formula at all ( ℛ 1 \mathcal{R}_{1} ). Precisely for these non-identifiable queries, the existence of an abstraction provides a mechanism-level route to transport, where the query-level criterion supplies none. However, the main payoff of the model-level perspective appears in the approximate case. When no exact map exists, the best approximate one still carries structural information; its residual error induces certified bounds on target queries. This places our contribution alongside the literature on partial identification ( Zhang and Bareinboim, 2021 ; Duarte et al., 2024 ; Bellot, 2024 ) and partial transportability ( Jalaldoust et al., 2024 ) , which seeks bounds when point identification fails. Our route differs on three axes. First, rather than optimising over the full class of SCMs compatible with the graph and observed distributions, re-solving per query, we learn a single transport map τ \tau that carries the entire source interventional family to the target at once. Second, our ambiguity set is metric rather than combinatorial: a structured neighbourhood around the source SCM, parameterised by bounded mechanism and environment perturbations, allows us to certify how far a mechanism may shift rather than only which mechanisms may differ. Third, we derive query intervals directly from the map’s residual error through structural stability constants, rather than by sampling or optimising over admissible models. Together, these yield what the classical query-level theory cannot: a certified interval, not a binary verdict, in both regimes. In ℛ 1 \mathcal{R}_{1} , the interval needs no identifying formula, so it is informative exactly where the do-calculus is silent; in ℛ 2 \mathcal{R}_{2} , the map minimises worst-case transport error over the ambiguity set, so the interval holds uniformly over every admissible target. Close to our setting is also ( Kostin et al., 2024 ) , which studies distributional robustness under partial identifiability in the same reduced form linear Gaussian model, distinguishing shift directions seen during training from unseen ones, a distinction that parallels our ℛ 1 \mathcal{R}_{1} / ℛ 2 \mathcal{R}_{2} regimes. Their shifts, however, are covariate shifts with an invariant mechanism, and their central object is a robust predictor minimising prediction risk; we instead admit mechanism perturbations and learn a transport map that certifies interventional queries, yielding query intervals rather than a risk bound.
Our robust formulation leverages Distributionally Robust Optimisation (DRO) ( Kuhn et al., 2019 ) , of a specific kind. In standard DRO, and in its use for domain generalisation ( Wang et al., 2025 ) , the ambiguity set models uncertainty about the data-generating distribution itself. Here, by contrast, the source SCM is treated as a trusted causal reference, and ambiguity is placed on the unseen target through perturbations of the shifted mechanisms and the target environment. The decision variable is a causal transport map, and robustness is over causal models rather than predictive distributions. The ambiguity set can further encode a directional prior, a declared sign and magnitude for an anticipated mechanism shift, which sharpens both the transport map and its certificate when the realised shift is consistent with it. Our formulation builds directly on recent work introducing distributional robustness into CAs ( Felekis et al., 2026 ) . The result is a formalism in which identifiability is no longer a binary gate but a special, best-case point in a continuum of certified approximate transport: transportability is the existence of a structural relation between two causal models, and where that relation fails to hold exactly, its best approximation still certifies what can be transported. We summarise our contributions, and with them the structure of the paper, as follows.
• Transportability as automorphic causal abstraction (Section 3 ). We show that the transportability setting, where the source and target share variables, graph, and intervention semantics, differing only through a known subset of mechanisms, is a same-resolution instance of causal abstraction. This connection has not been drawn before, and it is what allows per-query verdicts to be replaced by a single model-level object relating the two domains.
• A hierarchy of model-level consistency notions (Section 3 ). We define four source–target relations, from observational matching to a single constructive map transporting the full interventional family, each implying the one before, together with an orthogonal query-restricted relaxation. This hierarchy places classical τ \tau - ω \omega abstraction in the transportability setting and identifies the compatibility gap between query-level and model-level transport.
• Exact existence characterisations (Section 4 ). We provide characterisations for the existence of a constructive abstraction for both Markovian and semi-Markovian cases. In finite state spaces, these reduce to linear feasibility conditions, with accompanying uniqueness criteria.
• A target-agnostic robust formulation of approximate transport (Section 7 ). We introduce TraCA, a new framework that formulates approximate model-level transport as a distributionally robust optimisation problem over target-side mechanism and environment perturbations around a trusted source SCM.
• An optimisation algorithm (Sections 6 and 8 ). For linear additive-noise models, we derive stability bounds for mechanism perturbations and instantiate TraCA with Gaussian and empirical objectives, yielding a practical alternating min-max learning algorithm.
• Provable query-level certificates (Section 9 ). We show how model-level transport error induces certified intervals for downstream target queries, including the target-agnostic robust regime where the bounds hold uniformly over the ambiguity set. The certificate comes in a general form, valid for any target in the ambiguity set, and a sharper directional form that exploits a declared prior.
• Empirical validation in the two motivating regimes (Section 10 ). We validate the framework on synthetic Markovian and semi-Markovian benchmarks and on a real-world ecological dataset, focusing on certified transport for a query that is not classically transportable and on robust target-agnostic transport. Across all benchmarks, the certified intervals bracket the ground-truth query at the radius the true shift requires; the directional certificate is several times tighter than the general one where both are informative, and it remains the only informative bound where the general one is vacuous.
• Practical guidance (Section 11 ). We provide concrete guidance to practitioners on choosing the ambiguity geometry, encoding a directional prior, sizing the operating radius, and reading the two certificates together.
2 Background
2.1 Structural causal models
We briefly recall the Structural Causal Model (SCM) framework of Pearl (2009) .
Definition 1 .
A d d -dimensional Structural Causal Model (SCM) is a pair ℳ ≔ ( 𝒮 , ρ ) \mathcal{M}\coloneq({\cal S},\rho) , where 𝒮 = ⟨ 𝒳 , 𝒰 , ℱ ⟩ {\cal S}=\langle\mathcal{X},\mathcal{U},\mathcal{F}\rangle defines the deterministic causal basis , consisting of a set of d d endogenous variables 𝒳 \mathcal{X} , a set of exogenous variables 𝒰 \mathcal{U} , and a set of d d structural functions ℱ \mathcal{F} , each defining the value of an endogenous variable as
X i = f i ​ ( PA ⁡ ( X i ) , U i ) ∀ i ∈ [ d ] , \displaystyle X_{i}=f_{i}(\operatorname{PA}(X_{i}),U_{i})\quad\forall\;i\in[d], (1)
where PA ⁡ ( X i ) ⊆ 𝒳 ∖ X i \operatorname{PA}(X_{i})\subseteq\mathcal{X}\setminus{X_{i}} denotes the direct causes (parents) of X i X_{i} . For any variable or index set V V , we write dom ⁡ [ V ] \operatorname{dom}[V] for its domain (state space), the set of values it may take; the environment ρ \rho is a joint probability distribution over 𝒰 \mathcal{U} .
The structural assignments induce a directed graph 𝒢 ℳ \mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{M}} , called the causal graph on nodes X 1 , … , X d X_{1},\dots,X_{d} by placing an edge X k → X i X_{k}\to X_{i} whenever X k X_{k} appears as an argument of f i f_{i} . Throughout the paper, we assume acyclic SCMs, so 𝒢 ℳ \mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{M}} is a directed acyclic graph (DAG). This allows us to recursively compose the structural functions into a single map 𝐠 : dom ⁡ [ 𝒰 ] → dom ⁡ [ 𝒳 ] \mathbf{g}:\operatorname{dom}[\mathcal{U}]\to\operatorname{dom}[\mathcal{X}] , referred to as the mixing function . This defines the SCM’s reduced form 𝒳 = 𝐠 ⁡ ( 𝒰 ) \mathcal{X}=\mathbf{g}(\mathcal{U}) , where endogenous variables are expressed purely in terms of exogenous noise. Consequently, the induced distribution is the pushforward ℙ ℳ ​ ( 𝒳 ) = 𝐠 # ​ ( ρ ) \mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{M}}(\mathcal{X})=\mathbf{g}_{\#}(\rho) , making explicit the generative process by which exogenous uncertainty propagates through the model.
2.2 Interventions and causal queries
SCMs facilitate reasoning about interventions . An exact intervention ι = do ​ ( 𝐀 = a ) \iota=\text{do}(\mathbf{A}=\textbf{a}) fixes variables 𝐀 ⊆ 𝒳 \mathbf{A}\subseteq\mathcal{X} to values a , while allowing the rest of the system to evolve as usual. Graphically, this mutilates 𝒢 ℳ \mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{M}} by removing incoming edges to 𝐀 \mathbf{A} . This yields a post-interventional SCM ℳ ι \mathcal{M}_{\iota} with joint distribution ℙ ℳ ι ​ ( 𝒳 ) \mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{M}_{\iota}}(\mathcal{X}) . Whenever clear from the context, we write do ⁡ ( 𝐚 ) \operatorname{do}(\mathbf{a}) as shorthand for do ⁡ ( 𝐀 = 𝐚 ) \operatorname{do}(\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{a}) . For the purpose of our paper, we consider a set of relevant interventions ℐ = { ι 1 , … ​ ι k } {\cal I}=\{\iota_{1},...\iota_{k}\} which restricts attention to interventions that are scientifically meaningful or practically feasible. To distinguish between the intervention itself and the variables whose post-intervention behaviour one ultimately cares about, we introduce the following object.
Definition 2 (Query specification family) .
Let ℐ {\cal I} be a set of relevant interventions. A query specification family is an index set
𝒬 = { ( ι , O ι ) : ι ∈ ℐ , O ι ⊆ [ d ] ∖ J ι } , \displaystyle\mathcal{Q}=\{(\iota,O_{\iota}):\iota\in{\cal I},\;O_{\iota}\subseteq[d]\setminus J_{\iota}\}, (2)
where J ι ⊆ [ d ] J_{\iota}\subseteq[d] denotes the set of variables directly intervened upon by ι \iota , and O ι O_{\iota} specifies the post-intervention variables of interest under ι \iota . Throughout the paper, we assume 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} is finite.
When O ι = [ d ] ∖ J ι O_{\iota}=[d]\setminus J_{\iota} , this reduces to the full post-interventional case. The role of 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} is to specify which interventional marginals are relevant. It does not yet specify which numerical functional of those marginals will be evaluated. For each ( ι , O ι ) ∈ 𝒬 (\iota,O_{\iota})\in\mathcal{Q} , the corresponding query-relevant interventional distribution is the marginal
π O ι ​ # ​ ℙ ℳ ι ​ ( 𝒳 ) , \displaystyle\pi_{O_{\iota}\#}\,\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{M}_{\iota}}(\mathcal{X}), (3)
where π O ι \pi_{O_{\iota}} denotes the projection onto the coordinates indexed by O ι O_{\iota} .
Definition 3 (Causal query) .
A causal query is any measurable functional of a query-relevant interventional marginal. Concretely, if Φ \Phi is a measurable map on probability measures over dom ⁡ [ 𝒳 O ι ] \operatorname{dom}[\mathcal{X}^{O_{\iota}}] , then the causal query associated with ( ι , O ι ) ∈ 𝒬 (\iota,O_{\iota})\in\mathcal{Q} is
Q ℳ ( ι , O ι ) := Φ ⁡ ( π O ι ​ # ​ ℙ ℳ ι ​ ( 𝒳 ) ) . \displaystyle Q_{\mathcal{M}}^{(\iota,O_{\iota})}:=\Phi\!\left(\pi_{O_{\iota}\#}\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{M}_{\iota}}(\mathcal{X})\right). (4)
For instance, take ι = d ​ o ​ ( X = x ) \iota=do(X{=}x) and O ι = { Y } O_{\iota}=\{Y\} . Then ℙ ℳ ι ​ ( 𝒳 ) \mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{M}_{\iota}}(\mathcal{X}) is the joint post-interventional law over all variables, π O ι ​ # \pi_{O_{\iota}\#} marginalises it to Y Y alone, and choosing Φ \Phi to be the mean gives Q ℳ ( ι , O ι ) = 𝔼 ⁡ [ Y ∣ d ​ o ​ ( X = x ) ] Q_{\mathcal{M}}^{(\iota,O_{\iota})}=\mathbb{E}[Y\mid do(X{=}x)] . A different Φ \Phi on the same marginal, e.g. the median, the variance, gives a different query from the same interventional distribution, which is why the two are kept separate. This separation between 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} and the functional Φ \Phi is important in the present work. The object we later optimise is not a single scalar query but the discrepancy between source and target interventional marginals over a designated family 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} . Query-level guarantees are then obtained by applying appropriate functionals Φ ( ι , O ι ) \Phi_{(\iota,O_{\iota})} to these marginals. Thus 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} specifies the interventional distributions that matter, while the query functional specifies how they are ultimately evaluated. Given a causal query, a central question in causal inference is identifiability: whether the quantity of interest can be re-expressed in terms of the distributions actually available. Specifically,
Definition 4 (Identifiability) .
Given a graph 𝒢 \mathcal{G} and a class 𝒫 \mathcal{P} of available distributions, a causal query Q ℳ ( ι , O ι ) Q_{\mathcal{M}}^{(\iota,O_{\iota})} is identifiable from 𝒫 \mathcal{P} if there is a functional Φ \Phi with Q ℳ ( ι , O ι ) = Φ ⁡ ( 𝒫 ) Q_{\mathcal{M}}^{(\iota,O_{\iota})}=\Phi(\mathcal{P}) for every SCM consistent with 𝒢 \mathcal{G} that induces the given distributions in 𝒫 \mathcal{P} .
Here, consistent with 𝒢 \mathcal{G} means the SCM has 𝒢 \mathcal{G} as its causal graph and induces exactly the distributions in 𝒫 \mathcal{P} . Many such SCMs typically exist, and they may disagree on the interventional query; identifiability demands a single functional that returns the correct value for all of them, so the answer does not depend on which one generated the data. Thus, identifiability is a statement about whether the value of an interventional quantity of interest can be determined by the observational and interventional information available, together with the structural assumptions defining the model class. In the SCM framework, identifiability questions are typically addressed through do-calculus , a collection of graphical rules that allows expressions involving interventional distributions to be reduced to functionals of observational or simpler interventional quantities when such a reduction is valid. A central result is that do-calculus is complete for identifiability in nonparametric SCMs: whenever a causal query is identifiable from the available assumptions and data, there exists a derivation using the rules of do-calculus together with standard probability manipulations that recovers it. In this sense, do-calculus provides the canonical query-level language for deciding and deriving identifiability results ( Shpitser and Pearl, 2008 ) .
2.3 Markov kernels
Since our framework studies relations between interventional distributions, we need a general notion of transformation acting on probability measures. In general, such a transformation need not be deterministic. This is captured by the notion of a Markov kernel .
Definition 5 (Markov kernel) .
Let ( 𝒳 , 𝒜 ) (\mathcal{X},\mathcal{A}) and ( 𝒴 , ℬ ) (\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{B}) be measurable spaces (each a set equipped with a σ \sigma -algebra). A Markov kernel from 𝒳 \mathcal{X} to 𝒴 \mathcal{Y} is a map K : 𝒳 × ℬ → [ 0 , 1 ] K:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{B}\to[0,1] , such that:
(i) for every x ∈ 𝒳 x\in\mathcal{X} , the map B ↦ K ⁡ ( x , B ) B\mapsto K(x,B) is a probability measure on ( 𝒴 , ℬ ) (\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{B}) ;
(ii) for every B ∈ ℬ B\in\mathcal{B} , the map x ↦ K ⁡ ( x , B ) x\mapsto K(x,B) is 𝒜 \mathcal{A} -measurable.
Equivalently, for each input x ∈ 𝒳 x\in\mathcal{X} , the kernel K ( ⋅ ∣ x ) K(\cdot\mid x) specifies a probability distribution over outputs in 𝒴 \mathcal{Y} .
A deterministic measurable map τ : 𝒳 → 𝒴 \tau:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y} is a special case of a Markov kernel given by
K ( ⋅ ∣ x ) = δ τ ⁡ ( x ) , \displaystyle K(\cdot\mid x)=\delta_{\tau(x)}, (5)
where δ τ ⁡ ( x ) \delta_{\tau(x)} denotes the Dirac measure at τ ⁡ ( x ) \tau(x) . Thus, Markov kernels strictly generalise deterministic maps by allowing the output associated with a given input to be random rather than fixed. If μ \mu is a probability measure on 𝒳 \mathcal{X} , the image of μ \mu under K K is the probability measure μ ​ K \mu K on 𝒴 \mathcal{Y} defined by
( μ ​ K ) ​ ( B ) = ∫ 𝒳 K ⁡ ( x , B ) ​ μ ​ ( 𝑑 x ) , B ∈ ℬ . \displaystyle(\mu K)(B)=\int_{\mathcal{X}}K(x,B)\,\mu(dx),\qquad B\in\mathcal{B}. (6)
When K ( ⋅ ∣ x ) = δ τ ⁡ ( x ) K(\cdot\mid x)=\delta_{\tau(x)} , this reduces to the usual pushforward τ # ​ μ \tau_{\#}\mu .
These preliminaries provide the language for the rest of the paper: SCMs specify the source and target domains, interventions generate the relevant post-interventional families, and Markov kernels provide the general transport operators relating them. We now use this language to reformulate transportability as a structural relation between models, rather than as a collection of query-specific formulas.
3 Transportability as a structural relation between models
3.1 Standard transportability setting
X X Y Y σ Y \sigma_{Y} (a)
Z Z X X Y Y σ Z \sigma_{Z} σ Y \sigma_{Y} (b)
Figure 2 : Selection diagrams . Each panel shows one source-target pair: the two SCMs ℳ s \mathcal{M}_{s} and ℳ t \mathcal{M}_{t} share endogenous variables, causal graph, and intervention semantics, and differ only at the shifted nodes (orange), where the mechanism, the exogenous noise, or both may vary across domains. A square selection node σ ( ⋅ ) \sigma_{(\cdot)} marks each. (a) A semi-Markovian example: the bidirected arc X ↔ Y X\leftrightarrow Y encodes an unobserved confounder, and the shifted effect node Y Y varies in its mechanism f Y f_{Y} (the coefficient on its parent X X ) and/or its exogenous noise, while the mechanism of X X is invariant. (b) A Markovian example with two shifted nodes: the outcome Y Y shifts through its mechanism f Y f_{Y} , the coefficients on its parents Z Z and X X and/or its exogenous noise, while the root Z Z , having no parents, shifts only through its exogenous law f Z ​ ( U Z ) f_{Z}(U_{Z}) ; the mechanism of X X is invariant.
The standard setting of transportability imposes a precise shared structure on the two populations/domains: source and target are governed by SCMs ℳ s \mathcal{M}_{s} and ℳ t \mathcal{M}_{t} on the same set of endogenous variables 𝒳 = ( X 1 , … , X d ) \mathcal{X}=(X_{1},\dots,X_{d}) , the same DAG 𝒢 ℳ s = 𝒢 ℳ t = : 𝒢 \mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{M}_{s}}=\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{M}_{t}}=:\mathcal{G} , and the same intervention semantics. They differ only in the mechanisms or exogenous distributions of a known subset of endogenous nodes 𝒦 ⊆ [ d ] \mathcal{K}\subseteq[d] , which we will call the shifted nodes . Their local conditionals may therefore change across domains:
ℙ ℳ s ​ ( X i ∣ PA ⁡ ( X i ) ) ≠ ℙ ℳ t ​ ( X i ∣ PA ⁡ ( X i ) ) for ​ i ∈ 𝒦 , \displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{M}_{s}}\!\bigl(X_{i}\mid\operatorname{PA}(X_{i})\bigr)\neq\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{M}_{t}}\!\bigl(X_{i}\mid\operatorname{PA}(X_{i})\bigr)\quad\text{for }i\in\mathcal{K}, (7)
while for every invariant node i ∉ 𝒦 i\notin\mathcal{K} , the mechanisms coincide:
ℙ ℳ s ​ ( X i ∣ PA ⁡ ( X i ) ) = ℙ ℳ t ​ ( X i ∣ PA ⁡ ( X i ) ) . \displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{M}_{s}}\!\bigl(X_{i}\mid\operatorname{PA}(X_{i})\bigr)=\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{M}_{t}}\!\bigl(X_{i}\mid\operatorname{PA}(X_{i})\bigr). (8)
The shifted nodes are encoded graphically by a selection diagram 𝒢 σ \mathcal{G}^{\sigma} (Figure 2 ), which augments 𝒢 \mathcal{G} with a selection variable σ i \sigma_{i} pointing into each X i X_{i} with i ∈ 𝒦 i\in\mathcal{K} .
In the standard setting, the available information consists of source observational data 𝒟 s o \mathcal{D}_{s}^{o} , source interventional data 𝒟 s i \mathcal{D}_{s}^{i} , and target observational data 𝒟 t o \mathcal{D}_{t}^{o} . The transportability problem then asks whether a target causal query can be identified as a functional of the available information ( 𝒟 s o , 𝒟 s i , 𝒟 t o ) (\mathcal{D}_{s}^{o},\mathcal{D}_{s}^{i},\mathcal{D}_{t}^{o}) , together with the structural assumptions encoded by the selection diagram. For notational convenience, for each intervention ι ∈ ℐ \iota\in{\cal I} , we write
P s ( ι ) := ℙ ( ℳ s ) ι ​ ( 𝒳 ) , P t ( ι ) := ℙ ( ℳ t ) ι ​ ( 𝒳 ) . \displaystyle P_{s}^{(\iota)}:=\mathbb{P}_{(\mathcal{M}_{s})_{\iota}}(\mathcal{X}),\qquad P_{t}^{(\iota)}:=\mathbb{P}_{(\mathcal{M}_{t})_{\iota}}(\mathcal{X}). (9)
In the classical full-joint formulation, a target causal query is typically written as:
Q t ( ι ) := Φ ⁡ ( P t ( ι ) ) , \displaystyle Q_{t}^{(\iota)}:=\Phi\!\left(P_{t}^{(\iota)}\right), (10)
that is, as a functional of the full post-interventional distribution (or equivalently, of the distribution on the non-intervened variables). More generally, in the notation of Section 2.2 , one may regard this as the special case O ι = [ d ] ∖ J ι O_{\iota}=[d]\setminus J_{\iota} of a query Q t ( ι , O ι ) Q_{t}^{(\iota,O_{\iota})} based on a query-relevant marginal. The answer is given one query at a time: for a specific functional Φ \Phi , intervention ι ∈ ℐ \iota\in{\cal I} , and output set O ι O_{\iota} , either the target quantity can be written as an explicit expression in the available distributions ( 𝒟 s o , 𝒟 s i , 𝒟 t o ) (\mathcal{D}_{s}^{o},\mathcal{D}_{s}^{i},\mathcal{D}_{t}^{o}) , a transport formula such as a back-door adjustment reweighted by the target covariate distribution, or no such expression exists. When such a formula exists, the query is said to be transportable . The do-calculus on the selection diagram provides a complete graphical criterion for deciding transportability and deriving the formula when it exists ( Pearl and Bareinboim, 2014 ) .
3.2 A hierarchy of consistency notions
Observational (O) Per-intervention (PI)
P s ​ K = P t P_{s}K=P_{t} P s ( ι ) ​ K ( ι ) = P t ( ι ) ​ ∀ ι P_{s}^{(\iota)}K^{(\iota)}=P_{t}^{(\iota)}\ \forall\iota
Interventional (IC) P s ( ι ) ​ K = P t ( ι ) ​ ∀ ι P_{s}^{(\iota)}K=P_{t}^{(\iota)}\ \forall\iota Constructive IC (CICk/CICm) K = ∏ i K i K=\prod_{i}K_{i} require single K K require component-wise 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -Restricted Relaxation π O ι ​ # ​ ( P s ( ι ) ​ K ) = π O ι ​ # ​ P t ( ι ) \pi_{O_{\iota}\#}(P_{s}^{(\iota)}K)=\pi_{O_{\iota}\#}P_{t}^{(\iota)}
Figure 3 : The hierarchy of source-target consistency notions. Constraint strength increases downward along the spine (the narrower the box, the more constraining the notion) and, orthogonally, from the 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -restricted relaxation toward the full-family notions on the left. (O) and (PI) hold for any pair of models; the real constraint begins at interventional consistency (IC). Constructive interventional consistency (CICk/CICm) is the strongest and the central object of Section 4 . In an orthogonal dimension, 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -restricted consistency (right) relaxes any of the spine notions to hold only on designated query marginals; it is informative precisely at IC and Constructive IC, since O and PI already hold for every model pair and their relaxations are therefore vacuous.
This query-level viewpoint is natural from the perspective of identification, but it leaves open a more structural question. In the transportability setting, the source and target domains are not arbitrary unrelated SCMs. They already share the same endogenous variables, the same graph, and the same intervention semantics, differing only through the mechanisms indexed by the selection nodes, as illustrated in Figure 2 . This means that the two models come equipped with a canonical variable-by-variable and intervention-by-intervention correspondence. Once this shared scaffold is fixed, it becomes natural to ask not only whether a given query on the target can be transported from the source, but also whether the two models themselves are related by a common structural relation that explains such transport as a consequence. This leads to a shift in perspective from query transport to model-level transport . Instead of asking for a separate transport formula for each target quantity, we ask whether there exists a single relation between ℳ s \mathcal{M}_{s} and ℳ t \mathcal{M}_{t} that aligns them in a meaningful way. The central question of the paper is therefore the following:
When is transportability supported by a common, model-level, structural relation between the source and target models themselves?
Equivalently, can the transport of target queries be understood as a consequence of a single model-level object rather than a collection of unrelated query-specific formulas? In the strongest form considered in this paper, this means asking whether there exists one common map sending the full source interventional family { P s ( ι ) } ι ∈ ℐ \{P_{s}^{(\iota)}\}_{\iota\in{\cal I}} to the target interventional family { P t ( ι ) } ι ∈ ℐ \{P_{t}^{(\iota)}\}_{\iota\in{\cal I}} . This question is strictly stronger than the classical one: even if many individual queries are transportable, there may still be no single common structural transformation explaining them simultaneously.
Understanding when such a common relation exists, when it fails, and what it means for the source and target to be aligned at the model level is the starting point for the hierarchy developed next. We therefore define four increasingly structured notions of relation between the two models, together with an orthogonal relaxation. Along the main chain, each step imposes an additional structural requirement on how the source and target are allowed to be related. At the weakest level, one may ask only whether some operator relates the observational distributions, with no causal or mechanistic structure imposed.
Definition 6 (Observational consistency) .
ℳ s \mathcal{M}_{s} is observationally consistent with ℳ t \mathcal{M}_{t} if there exists a Markov kernel K : dom ⁡ [ 𝒳 ] × ℬ ⁡ ( dom ⁡ [ 𝒳 ] ) → [ 0 , 1 ] K:\operatorname{dom}[\mathcal{X}]\times\mathcal{B}(\operatorname{dom}[\mathcal{X}])\to[0,1] such that
P s ​ K = P t . \displaystyle P_{s}K=P_{t}. (11)
A stronger notion requires consistency not only at the observational level but also separately for each intervention in the family ℐ {\cal I} .
Definition 7 (Per-intervention consistency) .
ℳ s \mathcal{M}_{s} is per-intervention consistent with ℳ t \mathcal{M}_{t} over ℐ {\cal I} if, for every ι ∈ ℐ \iota\in{\cal I} , there exists a Markov kernel K ( ι ) : dom ⁡ [ 𝒳 ] × ℬ ⁡ ( dom ⁡ [ 𝒳 ] ) → [ 0 , 1 ] K^{(\iota)}:\operatorname{dom}[\mathcal{X}]\times\mathcal{B}(\operatorname{dom}[\mathcal{X}])\to[0,1] such that
P s ( ι ) ​ K ( ι ) = P t ( ι ) . \displaystyle P_{s}^{(\iota)}K^{(\iota)}=P_{t}^{(\iota)}. (12)
Here, each intervention is treated independently, and the operator may vary with ι \iota .
Lemma 1 .
For any two probability measures μ \mu and ν \nu on dom ⁡ [ 𝒳 ] \operatorname{dom}[\mathcal{X}] , there exists a Markov kernel K K with μ ​ K = ν \mu K=\nu . Consequently, observational and per-intervention consistency always hold between ℳ s \mathcal{M}_{s} and ℳ t \mathcal{M}_{t} over any ℐ {\cal I} .
Proof.
Define the constant kernel
K ⁡ ( x , B ) := ν ⁡ ( B ) , x ∈ dom ⁡ [ 𝒳 ] , B ∈ ℬ ⁡ ( dom ⁡ [ 𝒳 ] ) . \displaystyle K(x,B):=\nu(B),~\;x\in\operatorname{dom}[\mathcal{X}],~\;B\in\mathcal{B}(\operatorname{dom}[\mathcal{X}]). (13)
For each fixed x x , the map B ↦ K ⁡ ( x , B ) B\mapsto K(x,B) is a probability measure on dom ⁡ [ 𝒳 ] \operatorname{dom}[\mathcal{X}] , and for each fixed measurable set B B , the map x ↦ K ⁡ ( x , B ) x\mapsto K(x,B) is constant; hence, it is measurable. Thus, K K is a Markov kernel. Applying K K to μ \mu gives
( μ ​ K ) ​ ( B ) = ∫ dom ⁡ [ 𝒳 ] K ⁡ ( x , B ) ​ μ ​ ( 𝑑 x ) = ∫ dom ⁡ [ 𝒳 ] ν ⁡ ( B ) ​ μ ​ ( 𝑑 x ) = ν ⁡ ( B ) , \displaystyle(\mu K)(B)=\int_{\operatorname{dom}[\mathcal{X}]}K(x,B)\,\mu(dx)=\int_{\operatorname{dom}[\mathcal{X}]}\nu(B)\,\mu(dx)=\nu(B), (14)
for every B ∈ ℬ ⁡ ( dom ⁡ [ 𝒳 ] ) B\in\mathcal{B}(\operatorname{dom}[\mathcal{X}]) . Hence, μ ​ K = ν \mu K=\nu . Now it is straightforward to see that taking μ = P s \mu=P_{s} and ν = P t \nu=P_{t} proves observational consistency, and taking μ = P s ( ι ) \mu=P_{s}^{(\iota)} and ν = P t ( ι ) \nu=P_{t}^{(\iota)} for each ι ∈ ℐ \iota\in{\cal I} proves per-intervention consistency. ∎
Remark 2 (Deterministic maps) .
Deterministic pairwise transport τ # ​ μ = ν \tau_{\#}\mu=\nu is not automatic for arbitrary pairs ( μ , ν ) (\mu,\nu) : its existence requires additional structure on the source-target pair. On standard Borel spaces, nonatomicity of μ \mu is a sufficient condition: any atomless standard probability space is Borel-isomorphic to ( [ 0 , 1 ] , Leb ) ([0,1],\mathrm{Leb}) ( Kechris, 1995 , Thm. 17.41) , and any target law ν \nu on a standard Borel space is the pushforward of Lebesgue measure under some Borel map. Hence, there exists a measurable τ \tau with τ # ​ μ = ν \tau_{\#}\mu=\nu . In Euclidean settings, stronger results are available from optimal transport: under quadratic cost, if μ \mu is absolutely continuous with respect to Lebesgue measure, Brenier’s theorem yields an optimal deterministic transport map ( Brenier, 1991 ; Villani and others, 2009 ) . Thus, unlike a common kernel, which always exists, a deterministic map exists only under such regularity conditions, so it already reflects real structure in the pair ( μ , ν ) (\mu,\nu) .
That said, observational consistency and per-intervention consistency remain pairwise, distribution-level notions. They allow the transport operator to vary freely across interventions and therefore do not yet express a single relation between the two models. The first genuinely model-level notion requires the same operator to work simultaneously across the full interventional family.
Definition 8 (Interventional consistency) .
ℳ s \mathcal{M}_{s} is interventionally consistent with ℳ t \mathcal{M}_{t} over ℐ {\cal I} if there exists a single Markov kernel K : dom ⁡ [ 𝒳 ] × ℬ ⁡ ( dom ⁡ [ 𝒳 ] ) → [ 0 , 1 ] K:\operatorname{dom}[\mathcal{X}]\times\mathcal{B}(\operatorname{dom}[\mathcal{X}])\to[0,1] , such that
P s ( ι ) ​ K = P t ( ι ) ∀ ι ∈ ℐ . \displaystyle P_{s}^{(\iota)}K=P_{t}^{(\iota)}\qquad\forall\,\iota\in{\cal I}. (15)
The deterministic version restricts to kernels of the form
K ( ⋅ ∣ x ) = δ τ ⁡ ( x ) , \displaystyle K(\cdot\mid x)=\delta_{\tau(x)}, (16)
for some measurable map τ : dom ⁡ [ 𝒳 ] → dom ⁡ [ 𝒳 ] \tau:\operatorname{dom}[\mathcal{X}]\to\operatorname{dom}[\mathcal{X}] .
Interventional consistency upgrades per-intervention consistency by replacing the family { K ( ι ) } ι ∈ ℐ \{K^{(\iota)}\}_{\iota\in{\cal I}} with a single common operator. This is the first notion that is not automatically achievable: the existence of a common K K imposes a genuine compatibility constraint between the source and target interventional families and is precisely the central consistency requirement that appears in the Causal Abstraction (CA) literature ( Rubenstein et al., 2017 ; Beckers and Halpern, 2019 ) . CA theory studies when one SCM ℳ h {\cal M}^{h} can serve as a causally faithful higher-level representation of another SCM ℳ ℓ {\cal M}^{\ell} , typically at a different level of granularity. The key requirement is that interventions and abstraction commute, so that abstracting after intervening gives the same result as intervening after abstracting.
Definition 9 (Exact transformation) .
Let ℳ ℓ \mathcal{M}^{\ell} and ℳ h \mathcal{M}^{h} be SCMs with respective intervention sets ℐ ℓ \mathcal{I}^{\ell} and ℐ h \mathcal{I}^{h} , and let ω : ℐ ℓ → ℐ h \omega:\mathcal{I}^{\ell}\to\mathcal{I}^{h} be a surjective order-preserving map between interventions. A measurable map τ : dom ⁡ [ 𝒳 ℓ ] → dom ⁡ [ 𝒳 h ] \tau:\operatorname{dom}[\mathcal{X}^{\ell}]\to\operatorname{dom}[\mathcal{X}^{h}] is an exact transformation if
τ # ​ ( ℙ ℳ ι ℓ ​ ( 𝒳 ℓ ) ) = ℙ ℳ ω ⁡ ( ι ) h ​ ( 𝒳 h ) ∀ ι ∈ ℐ ℓ . \displaystyle\tau_{\#}\bigl(\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{M}^{\ell}_{\iota}}(\mathcal{X}^{\ell})\bigr)=\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{M}^{h}_{\omega(\iota)}}(\mathcal{X}^{h})\qquad\forall\,\iota\in\mathcal{I}^{\ell}. (17)
Definition 9 is the abstraction-theoretic version of interventional consistency: a single deterministic map τ \tau must align the entire family of interventional distributions, while the map ω \omega specifies which intervention at the lower level corresponds to which intervention at the higher level. In other words, the abstraction must preserve not just observational behaviour but the full interventional semantics of the model via a single deterministic map τ \tau .
Our transportability setting can now be understood as a special case of this general abstraction framework. Indeed, source and target SCMs are defined on the same endogenous variables, with the same graphical scaffold and the same intervention semantics. Hence, there is no change of granularity, and no nontrivial intervention translation is needed. Formally, this means:
dom ⁡ [ 𝒳 ℓ ] = dom ⁡ [ 𝒳 h ] , ℐ ℓ = ℐ h , ω = id ℐ \displaystyle\operatorname{dom}[\mathcal{X}^{\ell}]=\operatorname{dom}[\mathcal{X}^{h}],\qquad\mathcal{I}^{\ell}=\mathcal{I}^{h},\qquad\omega=\mathrm{id}_{{\cal I}} (18)
Under Equations 18 , the exact transformation condition (Equation 17 ) becomes τ # ​ P s ( ι ) = P t ( ι ) , ∀ ι ∈ ℐ \tau_{\#}P_{s}^{(\iota)}=P_{t}^{(\iota)},~\forall\iota\in{\cal I} , which is exactly the deterministic form of interventional consistency from Definition 8 . Thus, the transportability problem studied here is an automorphic or same-level instance of causal abstraction: rather than relating a fine-grained SCM to a coarser one, we relate two SCMs on the same variable space that differ only through the mechanisms indexed by the selection diagram. This shows that our model-level notion of transportability is not separate from causal abstraction theory, but rather a structurally specialised instance of it.
The correspondence above is stated for deterministic exact transformations. Our framework extends it by allowing a common Markov kernel in place of a single measurable map. This is also the level at which it connects to optimal-transport-based approaches to causal abstraction learning.
Remark 3 (Connection to Optimal Transport couplings) .
A Markov kernel may be viewed as the conditional form of a coupling. In particular, if π \pi is a coupling of probability measures μ \mu and ν \nu on standard Borel spaces, then π \pi admits a disintegration with respect to its first marginal:
π ⁡ ( d ​ x , d ​ y ) = μ ⁡ ( d ​ x ) ​ K ​ ( x , d ​ y ) , \displaystyle\pi(dx,dy)=\mu(dx)\,K(x,dy), (19)
for some Markov kernel K K , and therefore ν = μ ​ K \nu=\mu K . In the finite discrete case, this reduces to P ⁡ ( x , y ) = μ ⁡ ( x ) ​ K ​ ( y ∣ x ) P(x,y)=\mu(x)\,K(y\mid x) , so row-normalising a transport plan yields a stochastic map. Thus, the stochastic abstraction maps learned from Optimal Transport ( Villani and others, 2009 ) plans in Felekis et al. (2024) can be viewed as finite-sample instances of the kernel-valued transport operators considered here. The present paper uses such operators directly at the level of interventional families, rather than a single empirical coupling.
In the general CA literature, the map τ \tau may merge several low-level variables into one high-level variable, split one low-level variable across several high-level ones, or otherwise change the level of representation. An important subclass is the constructive one, in which each high-level variable depends only on a dedicated, non-overlapping subset of low-level variables.
Definition 10 (Constructive exact transformation) .
Let 𝒳 h = ( X 1 h , … , X m h ) \mathcal{X}^{h}=(X^{h}_{1},\dots,X^{h}_{m}) be the endogenous variables of the high-level model. An exact transformation ( τ , ω ) (\tau,\omega) from ℳ ℓ \mathcal{M}^{\ell} to ℳ h \mathcal{M}^{h} is called constructive if there exists a partition Π = { B 1 , … , B m , B 0 } \Pi=\{B_{1},\dots,B_{m},B_{0}\} of the low-level variable indices, with B 1 , … , B m B_{1},\dots,B_{m} nonempty, such that for each k = 1 , … , m k=1,\dots,m there exists an exact transformation
τ k : dom ⁡ [ X B k ℓ ] → dom ⁡ [ X k h ] , where ​ dom ⁡ [ X B k ℓ ] := ∏ j ∈ B k dom ⁡ [ X j ℓ ] , \displaystyle\tau_{k}:\operatorname{dom}[X_{B_{k}}^{\ell}]\to\operatorname{dom}[X_{k}^{h}],\qquad\text{where }\operatorname{dom}[X_{B_{k}}^{\ell}]:=\prod_{j\in B_{k}}\operatorname{dom}[X_{j}^{\ell}], (20)
satisfying
τ ⁡ ( x ) = ( τ 1 ​ ( x B 1 ) , … , τ m ​ ( x B m ) ) ∀ x ∈ dom ⁡ [ 𝒳 ℓ ] . \displaystyle\tau(x)=\bigl(\tau_{1}(x_{B_{1}}),\dots,\tau_{m}(x_{B_{m}})\bigr)\qquad\forall x\in\operatorname{dom}[\mathcal{X}^{\ell}]. (21)
The block B 0 B_{0} (possibly empty) collects low-level variables that are marginalised away.
Remark 4 .
One may additionally require the intervention map ω \omega to be constructive, in the sense that interventions on X k h X_{k}^{h} depend only on interventions acting on variables in B k B_{k} . This is automatic in our setting, where ω = id ℐ \omega=\mathrm{id}_{{\cal I}} .
Definition 10 captures the general many-to-one setting of causal abstraction, where a single high-level variable summarises an entire block of low-level variables. In the transportability setting, by contrast, constructiveness becomes the natural same-level specialisation of this idea: the partition reduces to singletons B i = { i } B_{i}=\{i\} for i ∈ [ d ] i\in[d] , with B 0 = ∅ B_{0}=\emptyset , and ω = id ℐ \omega=\mathrm{id}_{{\cal I}} , so constructiveness is automatic. Since source and target differ only through the mechanisms indexed by 𝒦 \mathcal{K} , the relevant transformation should act locally on the variables whose mechanisms may shift, while leaving invariant variables untouched. In this sense, constructiveness is not an external modelling choice but the canonical form that interventional consistency takes in the transportability setting.
Remark 5 .
Constructiveness is the form that the transportability setting motivates, not the only one possible. Interventional consistency asks for some common K K ; that does not necessarily factorise; thus, non-constructive solutions can also exist in this setting, and therefore, the two notions remain distinct.
Definition 11 (Constructive interventional consistency) .
ℳ s \mathcal{M}_{s} is constructively interventionally consistent with ℳ t \mathcal{M}_{t} over ℐ {\cal I} if it is interventionally consistent and the common kernel K K factorises component-wise:
K ⁡ ( d ​ 𝐱 ′ ∣ 𝐱 ) = ∏ i = 1 d K i ​ ( d ​ x i ′ ∣ x i ) , \displaystyle K(d\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\mid\mathbf{x})=\prod_{i=1}^{d}K_{i}(dx_{i}^{\prime}\mid x_{i}), (22)
with
K i ( ⋅ ∣ x i ) = δ x i for every invariant node i ∉ 𝒦 . \displaystyle K_{i}(\cdot\mid x_{i})=\delta_{x_{i}}\qquad\text{for every invariant node }i\notin\mathcal{K}. (23)
The deterministic version is the special case in which
K i ( ⋅ ∣ x i ) = δ τ i ​ ( x i ) \displaystyle K_{i}(\cdot\mid x_{i})=\delta_{\tau_{i}(x_{i})} (24)
for measurable maps τ i : dom ⁡ [ X i ] → dom ⁡ [ X i ] \tau_{i}:\operatorname{dom}[X_{i}]\to\operatorname{dom}[X_{i}] .
We call such a K K constructively interventionally consistent kernel (CICk) and the deterministic equivalent constructively interventionally consistent map (CICm). Alongside this main hierarchy, there is also a query-family-restricted relaxation of interventional consistency. Instead of requiring a common operator to align the full post-interventional joint for each intervention, one may require it to align only the query-relevant marginals specified by a query specification family 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} . This restriction is orthogonal to the constructive or not distinction: a common operator may be 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -restricted with or without being constructive.
Definition 12 ( 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -restricted interventional consistency) .
Let 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} be a query specification family over ℐ {\cal I} . We say that ℳ s \mathcal{M}_{s} is 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -restricted interventionally consistent with ℳ t \mathcal{M}_{t} if there exists a single Markov kernel K : dom ⁡ [ 𝒳 ] × ℬ ⁡ ( dom ⁡ [ 𝒳 ] ) → [ 0 , 1 ] K:\operatorname{dom}[\mathcal{X}]\times\mathcal{B}(\operatorname{dom}[\mathcal{X}])\to[0,1] such that
π O ι ​ # ​ ( P s ( ι ) ​ K ) = π O ι ​ # ​ P t ( ι ) ∀ ( ι , O ι ) ∈ 𝒬 . \displaystyle\pi_{O_{\iota}\#}\bigl(P_{s}^{(\iota)}K\bigr)=\pi_{O_{\iota}\#}P_{t}^{(\iota)}\qquad\forall\,(\iota,O_{\iota})\in\mathcal{Q}. (25)
The deterministic version restricts to kernels of the form
K ( ⋅ ∣ x ) = δ τ ⁡ ( x ) \displaystyle K(\cdot\mid x)=\delta_{\tau(x)} (26)
for some measurable map τ : dom ⁡ [ 𝒳 ] → dom ⁡ [ 𝒳 ] \tau:\operatorname{dom}[\mathcal{X}]\to\operatorname{dom}[\mathcal{X}] .
A constructive 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -restricted interventional consistency condition is obtained by adding to this definition the same componentwise factorisation requirement as in constructive interventional consistency. Thus, 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -restricted consistency weakens what must be matched, while constructiveness restricts how the common operator is allowed to act.
Remark 6 .
The two extremes of 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -restricted interventional consistency recover two endpoints of interest. Taking O ι = [ d ] ∖ J ι O_{\iota}=[d]\setminus J_{\iota} for every ι ∈ ℐ \iota\in{\cal I} induces the full post-interventional joint and recovers interventional consistency. At the other extreme, taking 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} to be a singleton isolates the single marginal on which a query of interest lives.
Constructive interventional consistency is the strongest notion in the main hierarchy considered here. Once such a common operator exists, it simultaneously aligns the full source interventional family with the target one; therefore, the transportability of all queries follows immediately.
Remark 7 .
If P s ( ι ) ​ K = P t ( ι ) , ∀ ι ∈ ℐ P_{s}^{(\iota)}K=P_{t}^{(\iota)},\forall\iota\in{\cal I} , then for every query family 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} , every measurable functional Φ \Phi , and every ( ι , O ι ) ∈ 𝒬 (\iota,O_{\iota})\in\mathcal{Q} , we have
Φ ⁡ ( π O ι ​ # ​ P t ( ι ) ) = Φ ⁡ ( π O ι ​ # ​ ( P s ( ι ) ​ K ) ) . \displaystyle\Phi\!\left(\pi_{O_{\iota}\#}P_{t}^{(\iota)}\right)=\Phi\!\left(\pi_{O_{\iota}\#}(P_{s}^{(\iota)}K)\right). (27)
Thus, full interventional consistency implies the transport of all query-restricted causal queries simultaneously. More generally, if K K is only 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -restricted interventionally consistent, then the same conclusion holds for all ( ι , O ι ) ∈ 𝒬 (\iota,O_{\iota})\in\mathcal{Q} , but not necessarily outside 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} . However, the converse fails in general: even if all queries in a given family are transportable, there may be no single common operator that aligns the full interventional family. This failure is what we call the compatibility gap : it separates query-level transportability from model-level transportability.
Constructive interventional consistency is thus the strongest member of the hierarchy. In the following section, we study when this holds and how it can be characterised.
4 Characterising Exact Model-level Transportability
Section 3 introduced the hierarchy of model-level transport notions and isolated constructive interventional consistency as the central exact notion. We now ask when such a common constructive transport operator exists, first for Markovian SCMs and then extending the analysis to the semi-Markovian case.
4.1 The Markovian Setting
We first specialise to the case where both the source and target SCMs, ℳ s = ( 𝒮 s , ρ s ) \mathcal{M}_{s}=({\cal S}_{s},\rho_{s}) and ℳ t = ( 𝒮 t , ρ t ) \mathcal{M}_{t}=({\cal S}_{t},\rho_{t}) , are Markovian . Thus, both models are acyclic, and their exogenous variables are jointly independent; i.e., ρ α = ⨂ i = 1 d ρ α , i , α ∈ { s , t } \rho_{\alpha}=\bigotimes_{i=1}^{d}\rho_{\alpha,i},~\alpha\in\{s,t\} . Joint independence implies causal sufficiency, so there are no latent common causes among the endogenous variables. We assume that ℳ s \mathcal{M}_{s} and ℳ t \mathcal{M}_{t} are defined on the same endogenous variable set 𝒳 \mathcal{X} and entail the same DAG 𝒢 \mathcal{G} . Throughout, we index the endogenous variables X 1 , … , X d X_{1},\dots,X_{d} in a fixed topological order of 𝒢 \mathcal{G} so that PA ⁡ ( X i ) ⊆ { X 1 , … , X i − 1 } \operatorname{PA}(X_{i})\subseteq\{X_{1},\dots,X_{i-1}\} . Under this numbering, “variable X i X_{i} ”, “coordinate i i ”, and “stage i i ” of the assembly all refer to the same index; we use the terms interchangeably. Markovianity yields the usual DAG factorisation:
P α ​ ( 𝐱 ) = ∏ i = 1 d ℙ ℳ α ​ ( x i ∣ PA ⁡ ( X i ) = x PA ⁡ ( X i ) ) , α ∈ { s , t } . P_{\alpha}(\mathbf{x})=\prod_{i=1}^{d}\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{M}_{\alpha}}\bigl(x_{i}\mid\operatorname{PA}(X_{i})=x_{\operatorname{PA}(X_{i})}\bigr),\qquad\alpha\in\{s,t\}. (28)
For every invariant node i ∉ 𝒦 i\notin\mathcal{K} , ℙ ℳ s ​ ( X i ∣ PA ⁡ ( X i ) ) = ℙ ℳ t ​ ( X i ∣ PA ⁡ ( X i ) ) \mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{M}_{s}}\!\bigl(X_{i}\mid\operatorname{PA}(X_{i})\bigr)=\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{M}_{t}}\!\bigl(X_{i}\mid\operatorname{PA}(X_{i})\bigr) . Now fix an intervention ι = do ⁡ ( 𝐀 = 𝐚 ) ∈ ℐ \iota=\operatorname{do}(\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{a})\in{\cal I} , and let J ι ⊆ [ d ] J_{\iota}\subseteq[d] denote the index set of the variables in 𝐀 \mathbf{A} . In the Markovian case, the interventional distribution admits the truncated factorisation ( Pearl, 2009 ) :
P α ( ι ) ​ ( 𝐱 ) = ∏ i ∉ J ι ℙ ℳ α ​ ( x i ∣ PA ⁡ ( X i ) = x PA ⁡ ( X i ) ) ⋅ ∏ i ∈ J ι δ a i ​ ( x i ) , α ∈ { s , t } , P_{\alpha}^{(\iota)}(\mathbf{x})=\prod_{i\notin J_{\iota}}\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{M}_{\alpha}}\bigl(x_{i}\mid\operatorname{PA}(X_{i})=x_{\operatorname{PA}(X_{i})}\bigr)\cdot\prod_{i\in J_{\iota}}\delta_{a_{i}}(x_{i}),\qquad\alpha\in\{s,t\}, (29)
where a i a_{i} is the intervention value assigned to X i X_{i} . Hence, under a given intervention, the only non-intervened factors that can differ between source and target are those associated with shifted nodes i ∈ 𝒦 ∖ J ι i\in\mathcal{K}\setminus J_{\iota} .
4.1.1 Relevant parent contexts
Classical transportability reasons at the level of target queries of a shifted node X i X_{i} rather than at the level of its mechanisms. Here we ask a different and stronger question: whether the mechanism of X i X_{i} , namely its conditional law ℙ ⁡ ( X i ∣ PA ⁡ ( X i ) ) \mathbb{P}(X_{i}\mid\operatorname{PA}(X_{i})) , can be aligned across domains by a single local transport component. Since a mechanism is indexed by parent values, this is not a statement about X i X_{i} in isolation: it must hold at every parent configuration on which the mechanism is actually evaluated. The intervention family determines exactly which parent values can actually occur while the mechanism remains active. An intervention on an ancestor of X i X_{i} can drive its parents into values never seen observationally, enlarging the set that must be matched; an intervention on X i X_{i} itself switches the mechanism off entirely. We therefore isolate exactly the parent configurations that matter.
Definition 13 (Relevant parent contexts) .
Fix a shifted node i ∈ 𝒦 i\in\mathcal{K} , and for each ι ∈ ℐ \iota\in{\cal I} , let J ι J_{\iota} be its intervened set. The set of relevant parent contexts for X i X_{i} is
𝒫 i rel := ⋃ ι ∈ ℐ i ∉ J ι ( supp ⁡ ( P s ( ι ) ​ ( PA ⁡ ( X i ) ) ) ∪ supp ⁡ ( P t ( ι ) ​ ( PA ⁡ ( X i ) ) ) ) . \displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{i}^{\mathrm{rel}}:=\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\iota\in{\cal I}\\ i\notin J_{\iota}\end{subarray}}\left(\mathrm{supp}\!\Bigl(P_{s}^{(\iota)}\bigl(\operatorname{PA}(X_{i})\bigr)\Bigr)\;\cup\;\mathrm{supp}\!\Bigl(P_{t}^{(\iota)}\bigl(\operatorname{PA}(X_{i})\bigr)\Bigr)\right). (30)
The set 𝒫 i rel \mathcal{P}_{i}^{\mathrm{rel}} collects all the parent configurations at which the native mechanism of X i X_{i} is queried under interventions in ℐ {\cal I} ; this is the domain on which local source-target alignment of that mechanism must be checked. If i ∈ J ι i\in J_{\iota} the intervention fixes X i X_{i} and bypasses its mechanism, replacing it with a point mass at the intervention value, so such regimes impose no condition on the local component K i K_{i} or τ i \tau_{i} .
As a concrete instance, take a chain X 1 → X 2 X_{1}\to X_{2} with X 2 X_{2} shifted. Comparing the observational conditionals ℙ ℳ s ​ ( X 2 ∣ X 1 ) \mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{M}_{s}}(X_{2}\mid X_{1}) and ℙ ℳ t ​ ( X 2 ∣ X 1 ) \mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{M}_{t}}(X_{2}\mid X_{1}) only on the naturally observed support of X 1 X_{1} can miss parent values that arise once one intervenes on X 1 X_{1} ; since constructive interventional consistency must hold over all of ℐ {\cal I} , the mechanism of X 2 X_{2} must be aligned on every such context while X 2 X_{2} remains active.
If every parent of a shifted node X i X_{i} is invariant, alignment is direct: one asks whether the source mechanism for X i X_{i} , composed with a local component K i K_{i} , matches the target mechanism on 𝒫 i rel \mathcal{P}_{i}^{\mathrm{rel}} . The difficulty lies when a parent of X i X_{i} is itself shifted. By the time the mechanism of X i X_{i} is reached, its parents no longer follow the source law, and they have been modified by the earlier components, so the stage- i i condition must be imposed against the partially transformed parent law rather than the original source conditionals. This is what makes the problem recursive. However, recursion alone is still not enough: a general componentwise kernel can destroy the Markov factorisation of the intermediate laws. Thus, besides recursive alignment, one must require that the partially transformed laws retain the relevant Markov structure.
4.1.2 Mechanism-level characterisation and recursive assembly
The idea now is simple: by stage i − 1 i-1 all parents of X i X_{i} have already been transported, so if K i K_{i} is chosen to give X i X_{i} the correct target conditional given those transported parents, the first i i variables match the target law. Iterating down the topological order assembles the full target joint, one mechanism at a time. Making this precise requires two pieces of bookkeeping: a notation for applying the local components one coordinate at a time, and a condition ensuring that each intermediate law still presents the next mechanism as a conditional on its parent tuple.
For each i ∈ [ d ] i\in[d] , let K ¯ i \bar{K}_{i} denote the lifted kernel on the full state space that acts as K i K_{i} on coordinate i i and as the identity on all other coordinates:
K ¯ i ​ ( d ​ 𝐱 ′ ∣ 𝐱 ) := K i ​ ( d ​ x i ′ ∣ x i ) ​ ∏ j ≠ i δ x j ​ ( d ​ x j ′ ) . \displaystyle\bar{K}_{i}(d\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\mid\mathbf{x}):=K_{i}(dx_{i}^{\prime}\mid x_{i})\prod_{j\neq i}\delta_{x_{j}}(dx_{j}^{\prime}). (31)
Since these lifted kernels act on disjoint coordinates, they commute, and their composition is exactly the componentwise kernel K ⁡ ( d ​ 𝐱 ′ ∣ 𝐱 ) = ∏ i = 1 d K i ​ ( d ​ x i ′ ∣ x i ) K(d\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\mid\mathbf{x})=\prod_{i=1}^{d}K_{i}(dx_{i}^{\prime}\mid x_{i}) .
Definition 14 .
A componentwise kernel K ⁡ ( d ​ 𝐱 ′ ∣ 𝐱 ) = ∏ i = 1 d K i ​ ( d ​ x i ′ ∣ x i ) K(d\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\mid\mathbf{x})=\prod_{i=1}^{d}K_{i}(dx_{i}^{\prime}\mid x_{i}) is called stagewise Markov-preserving over ℐ {\cal I} if, for every intervention ι ∈ ℐ \iota\in{\cal I} , with
R 0 ( ι ) := P s ( ι ) , R i ( ι ) := R i − 1 ( ι ) K ¯ i , i = 1 , … , d , \displaystyle R_{0}^{(\iota)}:=P_{s}^{(\iota)},\qquad R_{i}^{(\iota)}:=R_{i-1}^{(\iota)}\bar{K}_{i},\qquad i=1,\dots,d,
one has, for every node i ∉ J ι i\notin J_{\iota} ,
R i ( ι ) ​ ( X i ∣ X 1 , … , X i − 1 ) = R i ( ι ) ​ ( X i ∣ X PA ⁡ ( X i ) ) R i ( ι ) ​ -a.s. \displaystyle R_{i}^{(\iota)}\!\bigl(X_{i}\mid X_{1},\dots,X_{i-1}\bigr)=R_{i}^{(\iota)}\!\bigl(X_{i}\mid X_{\operatorname{PA}(X_{i})}\bigr)\qquad R_{i}^{(\iota)}\text{-a.s.} (32)
Theorem 8 (Recursive global assembly) .
Let K K be a stagewise Markov-preserving componentwise Markov kernel such that K i ( ⋅ ∣ x i ) = δ x i K_{i}(\cdot\mid x_{i})=\delta_{x_{i}} for every invariant node i ∉ 𝒦 i\notin\mathcal{K} . For each intervention ι ∈ ℐ \iota\in{\cal I} , set R 0 ( ι ) := P s ( ι ) R_{0}^{(\iota)}:=P_{s}^{(\iota)} and R i ( ι ) := R i − 1 ( ι ) ​ K ¯ i R_{i}^{(\iota)}:=R_{i-1}^{(\iota)}\bar{K}_{i} for i = 1 , … , d i=1,\dots,d . Then K K is a CICk from ℳ s \mathcal{M}_{s} to ℳ t \mathcal{M}_{t} over ℐ {\cal I} if and only if, for every intervention ι ∈ ℐ \iota\in{\cal I} and every node i ∈ [ d ] i\in[d] , the partially transformed law R i ( ι ) R_{i}^{(\iota)} satisfies:
(i) if i ∈ J ι i\in J_{\iota} , then R i ( ι ) ​ ( X i ) = δ a i R_{i}^{(\iota)}(X_{i})=\delta_{a_{i}} ;
(ii) if i ∉ J ι i\notin J_{\iota} , then R i ( ι ) ​ ( X i ∣ X PA ⁡ ( X i ) ) = ℙ ℳ t ​ ( X i ∣ X PA ⁡ ( X i ) ) ​ R i ( ι ) ​ -a.s. R_{i}^{(\iota)}\!\bigl(X_{i}\mid X_{\operatorname{PA}(X_{i})}\bigr)=\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{M}_{t}}\!\bigl(X_{i}\mid X_{\operatorname{PA}(X_{i})}\bigr)~~R_{i}^{(\iota)}\text{-a.s.}
The deterministic analogue holds with K i ( ⋅ ∣ x i ) = δ τ i ​ ( x i ) K_{i}(\cdot\mid x_{i})=\delta_{\tau_{i}(x_{i})} .
Stage 0 X 1 X_{1} X 2 X_{2} X 3 X_{3} R 0 ( ι ) = P s ( ι ) R_{0}^{(\iota)}=P_{s}^{(\iota)} K ¯ 1 \bar{K}_{1} Stage 1 X 1 X_{1} X 2 X_{2} X 3 X_{3} R 1 ( ι ) R_{1}^{(\iota)} K ¯ 2 \bar{K}_{2} Stage 2 X 1 X_{1} X 2 X_{2} X 3 X_{3} R 2 ( ι ) R_{2}^{(\iota)} K ¯ 3 \bar{K}_{3} Stage 3 X 1 X_{1} X 2 X_{2} X 3 X_{3} R 3 ( ι ) = P t ( ι ) R_{3}^{(\iota)}=P_{t}^{(\iota)}
Figure 4 : Recursive global assembly (Theorem 8 ). Schematic illustration of the induction behind the theorem on a chain X 1 → X 2 → X 3 X_{1}\to X_{2}\to X_{3} . At stage i i , the lifted kernel K ¯ i \bar{K}_{i} acts on coordinate i i while the others are held fixed. Nodes shaded in blue still carry the source-side law, nodes shaded in orange already agree with the target law, and the red outline marks the coordinate updated at the current stage. The partially transformed laws R i ( ι ) R_{i}^{(\iota)} satisfy the induction invariant that, after stage i i , the prefix ( X 1 , … , X i ) (X_{1},\dots,X_{i}) agrees with the target interventional law P t ( ι ) P_{t}^{(\iota)} ; by stage d d , the full target joint is recovered.
The proof formalises the mechanism-by-mechanism assembly described above, made visible in Figure 4 : one proceeds by induction along the topological order, showing that matching the stagewise conditional at node i i aligns the first i i variables with the target law. We defer the full argument to Appendix A.1 .
Remark 9 .
The stagewise Markov-preserving assumption excludes no genuine CICks. Indeed, if a componentwise kernel K K is a CICk, then in the forward direction above one has
R i ( ι ) ​ ( X 1 , … , X i ) = P t ( ι ) ​ ( X 1 , … , X i ) ∀ i , ι , \displaystyle R_{i}^{(\iota)}(X_{1},\dots,X_{i})=P_{t}^{(\iota)}(X_{1},\dots,X_{i})\qquad\forall\,i,\iota, (33)
and since P t ( ι ) P_{t}^{(\iota)} is Markov with respect to the DAG, R i ( ι ) R_{i}^{(\iota)} inherits the same node- i i conditional independence. Thus, a CICk exists in the full componentwise class if and only if it exists in the stagewise Markov-preserving componentwise class.
Remark 10 .
The stagewise Markov-preserving property is automatic in several regimes. First, if no shifted node is an ancestor of another shifted node, then the earlier kernels act as the identity on the parent tuples of later shifted nodes. Second, in the deterministic case, it suffices that the earlier maps be injective (e.g. relabelling or permutation) on the relevant parent supports, so that no conditioning information is lost. More generally, any kernel family that preserves the information carried by the relevant parent tuple yields the required stagewise Markov property.
Remark 11 .
When all parents of X i X_{i} are invariant, stagewise Markov-preservation is automatic and the stage- i i condition collapses to the direct local alignment
ℙ ℳ s ​ ( X i ∣ PA ⁡ ( X i ) = p ) ​ K i = ℙ ℳ t ​ ( X i ∣ PA ⁡ ( X i ) = p ) ∀ p ∈ 𝒫 i rel . \displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{M}_{s}}\!\bigl(X_{i}\mid\operatorname{PA}(X_{i})=p\bigr)\,K_{i}=\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{M}_{t}}\!\bigl(X_{i}\mid\operatorname{PA}(X_{i})=p\bigr)\qquad\forall\,p\in\mathcal{P}_{i}^{\mathrm{rel}}. (34)
Theorem 8 extends this picture by replacing the original parent law with its recursively transported counterpart.
Finite-state reduction.
When dom ⁡ [ X i ] \operatorname{dom}[X_{i}] is finite, the stage- i i compatibility condition is equivalent to the feasibility of a linear system for the row-stochastic kernel K i K_{i} , with additional row-fixing constraints enforcing intervention preservation. In this regime, existence becomes a linear feasibility question, while uniqueness can be studied via the rank of the transformed source conditional family. We record these finite-state results, together with the deterministic specialisation and related remarks, in Appendix A.2 .
4.2 Semi-Markovian extension
The Markovian case shows that exact model-level transport can be assembled recursively by aligning one active mechanism at a time along the DAG. We now ask how this picture changes when latent confounding destroys node-wise factorisation and the relevant transport objects are districts rather than individual variables. When latent confounding is present, the graph is an acyclic directed mixed graph (ADMG): a DAG augmented with bidirected edges ↔ \leftrightarrow marking pairs of variables sharing an unobserved common cause. Its districts (also called c-components) are the connected components under bidirected edges; i.e., the maximal sets of variables linked, directly or indirectly, by latent confounding. Let 𝒟 ⁡ ( 𝒢 ) {\cal D}(\mathcal{G}) denote the districts of the ADMG 𝒢 \mathcal{G} , and for D ∈ 𝒟 ⁡ ( 𝒢 ) D\in{\cal D}(\mathcal{G}) define
PA ⁡ ( D ) := ( ⋃ i ∈ D PA ⁡ ( X i ) ) ∖ D , dom ⁡ [ X D ] := ∏ i ∈ D dom ⁡ [ X i ] . \displaystyle\operatorname{PA}(D):=\Bigl(\bigcup_{i\in D}\operatorname{PA}(X_{i})\Bigr)\setminus D,\qquad\operatorname{dom}[X_{D}]:=\prod_{i\in D}\operatorname{dom}[X_{i}]. (35)
The corresponding district mechanism is
q D α ( ⋅ ∣ p ) := ℙ ℳ α ( X D ∣ do ( PA ( D ) = p ) ) , α ∈ { s , t } . \displaystyle q_{D}^{\alpha}(\cdot\mid p):=\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{M}_{\alpha}}\!\bigl(X_{D}\mid\operatorname{do}(\operatorname{PA}(D)=p)\bigr),\qquad\alpha\in\{s,t\}. (36)
In the Markovian special case, every district is a singleton and q D α q_{D}^{\alpha} reduces to the usual node-wise conditional. Let 𝒦 𝒟 := { D ∈ 𝒟 ⁡ ( 𝒢 ) : D ∩ 𝒦 ≠ ∅ } \mathcal{K}^{{\cal D}}:=\{D\in{\cal D}(\mathcal{G}):D\cap\mathcal{K}\neq\emptyset\} denote the shifted districts, i.e., those containing at least one shifted node; 𝒦 ⊆ [ d ] \mathcal{K}\subseteq[d] retains its meaning from Section 3.1 as the set of shifted nodes. We assume that for every invariant district D ∉ 𝒦 𝒟 D\notin\mathcal{K}^{{\cal D}} ,
q D s ( ⋅ ∣ p ) = q D t ( ⋅ ∣ p ) ∀ p ∈ dom [ PA ( D ) ] . q_{D}^{s}(\cdot\mid p)=q_{D}^{t}(\cdot\mid p)\qquad\forall\,p\in\operatorname{dom}[\operatorname{PA}(D)]. (37)
Constructive kernels factor over districts:
K ( d 𝐱 ′ ∣ 𝐱 ) = ∏ D ∈ 𝒟 ⁡ ( 𝒢 ) K D ( d 𝐱 D ′ ∣ 𝐱 D ) , K D ( ⋅ ∣ 𝐱 D ) = δ 𝐱 D for every D ∉ 𝒦 𝒟 . \displaystyle K(d\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\mid\mathbf{x})=\prod_{D\in{\cal D}(\mathcal{G})}K_{D}(d\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{D}\mid\mathbf{x}_{D}),\qquad K_{D}(\cdot\mid\mathbf{x}_{D})=\delta_{\mathbf{x}_{D}}\text{ for every }D\notin\mathcal{K}^{{\cal D}}. (38)
The following assumption restricts the intervention family so that each shifted district is either left intact or intervened upon, but never split. Since a district is the unit coupled by latent confounding, an intervention cutting across it would break the districtwise factorisation the assembly relies on; the cost is that interventions partially targeting a confounded set fall outside the present theory.
Assumption 1 (Shifted-district consistency) .
For every shifted district D ∈ 𝒦 𝒟 D\in\mathcal{K}^{{\cal D}} and every ι ∈ ℐ \iota\in{\cal I} with intervened set J ι J_{\iota} , D ⊆ J ι ​ or ​ D ∩ J ι = ∅ D\subseteq J_{\iota}~\text{or}~D\cap J_{\iota}=\emptyset .
Contracting each district to a single node yields a directed graph 𝒢 / 𝒟 \mathcal{G}_{/{\cal D}} on the districts, with directed edges lifted from 𝒢 \mathcal{G} (bidirected edges are internal to districts and are absorbed by the contraction). Contracting such multi-node blocks can create cycles even when 𝒢 \mathcal{G} is acyclic: with districts D X = { X 1 , X 2 } D_{X}=\{X_{1},X_{2}\} and D Y = { Y 1 , Y 2 } D_{Y}=\{Y_{1},Y_{2}\} (from X 1 ↔ X 2 X_{1}\!\leftrightarrow\!X_{2} , Y 1 ↔ Y 2 Y_{1}\!\leftrightarrow\!Y_{2} ), the directed edges X 1 → Y 1 X_{1}\!\to\!Y_{1} and Y 2 → X 2 Y_{2}\!\to\!X_{2} lift to D X → D Y D_{X}\!\to\!D_{Y} and D Y → D X D_{Y}\!\to\!D_{X} , a 2 2 -cycle. Following the standard restriction for Cluster DAGs ( Anand et al., 2023 ) , we focus on models whose quotient 𝒢 / 𝒟 \mathcal{G}_{/{\cal D}} is acyclic, which holds whenever no two districts are joined by directed edges in both directions, giving a topological order D 1 , … , D m D_{1},\dots,D_{m} for the assembly. This is a requirement of our constructive route, not of transportability as such: a cyclic quotient removes the order the stagewise assembly relies on, but such a model need not be non-transportable. Whether a common transport operator exists in the cyclic case, plausibly, by a non-constructive argument for models with a unique solution, we leave to future work. Using this order, for each r ∈ [ m ] r\in[m] , let K ¯ D r \bar{K}_{D_{r}} denote the lifted kernel that acts as K D r K_{D_{r}} on the block D r D_{r} and as the identity on all other coordinates, and define:
R 0 ( ι ) := P s ( ι ) , R r ( ι ) := R r − 1 ( ι ) K ¯ D r , r = 1 , … , m . \displaystyle R_{0}^{(\iota)}:=P_{s}^{(\iota)},\qquad R_{r}^{(\iota)}:=R_{r-1}^{(\iota)}\bar{K}_{D_{r}},\qquad r=1,\dots,m. (39)
Definition 15 (Stagewise district-preserving kernel) .
A districtwise product kernel K K is called stagewise district-preserving over ℐ {\cal I} if, for every intervention ι ∈ ℐ \iota\in{\cal I} and every district stage r r , one has, whenever D r ∩ J ι = ∅ D_{r}\cap J_{\iota}=\emptyset ,
R r ( ι ) ​ ( X D r ∣ X D 1 , … , X D r − 1 ) = R r ( ι ) ​ ( X D r ∣ X PA ⁡ ( D r ) ) R r ( ι ) ​ -a.s. \displaystyle R_{r}^{(\iota)}\!\bigl(X_{D_{r}}\mid X_{D_{1}},\dots,X_{D_{r-1}}\bigr)=R_{r}^{(\iota)}\!\bigl(X_{D_{r}}\mid X_{\operatorname{PA}(D_{r})}\bigr)\qquad R_{r}^{(\iota)}\text{-a.s.} (40)
Theorem 12 (Recursive district assembly) .
Under Assumption 1 , let K ⁡ ( d ​ 𝐱 ′ ∣ 𝐱 ) = ∏ D ∈ 𝒟 ⁡ ( 𝒢 ) K D ​ ( d ​ 𝐱 D ′ ∣ 𝐱 D ) K(d\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\mid\mathbf{x})=\prod_{D\in{\cal D}(\mathcal{G})}K_{D}(d\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{D}\mid\mathbf{x}_{D}) be a stagewise district-preserving districtwise product kernel such that K D ( ⋅ ∣ 𝐱 D ) = δ 𝐱 D K_{D}(\cdot\mid\mathbf{x}_{D})=\delta_{\mathbf{x}_{D}} for every invariant district D ∉ 𝒦 𝒟 D\notin\mathcal{K}^{{\cal D}} . Then K K is a CICk from ℳ s \mathcal{M}_{s} to ℳ t \mathcal{M}_{t} over ℐ {\cal I} if and only if, for every intervention ι ∈ ℐ \iota\in{\cal I} and every district D r ∈ 𝒟 ⁡ ( 𝒢 ) D_{r}\in{\cal D}(\mathcal{G}) , the partially transformed law R r ( ι ) R_{r}^{(\iota)} satisfies:
(i) if D r ⊆ J ι D_{r}\subseteq J_{\iota} , then R r ( ι ) ​ ( X D r ) = δ a D r R_{r}^{(\iota)}(X_{D_{r}})=\delta_{a_{D_{r}}} ;
(ii) if D r ∩ J ι = ∅ D_{r}\cap J_{\iota}=\emptyset , then R r ( ι ) ( X D r ∣ X PA ⁡ ( D r ) ) = q D r t ( ⋅ ∣ X PA ⁡ ( D r ) ) R r ( ι ) -a.s. R_{r}^{(\iota)}\!\bigl(X_{D_{r}}\mid X_{\operatorname{PA}(D_{r})}\bigr)=q_{D_{r}}^{t}\!\bigl(\cdot\mid X_{\operatorname{PA}(D_{r})}\bigr)~~R_{r}^{(\iota)}\text{-a.s.}
The deterministic analogue holds with K D ( ⋅ ∣ 𝐱 D ) = δ τ D ​ ( 𝐱 D ) K_{D}(\cdot\mid\mathbf{x}_{D})=\delta_{\tau_{D}(\mathbf{x}_{D})} .
Proof.
The proof uses the same inductive argument as in Theorem 8 , with nodes replaced by districts and Markov preservation replaced by district preservation. ∎
Remark 13 .
As in the Markovian case, the stagewise district-preserving assumption excludes no genuine CICks: any districtwise CICk automatically induces partially transformed laws whose district-prefix marginals agree with the target interventional law and hence inherit the required district-level conditional structure.
Finite-state district reduction.
When the district state space dom ⁡ [ X D r ] \operatorname{dom}[X_{D_{r}}] is finite, the stage- r r district compatibility problem again reduces to a linear feasibility problem for a row-stochastic district kernel K D r K_{D_{r}} , now with district state spaces replacing node state spaces. The corresponding finite-state existence, uniqueness, and deterministic-style refinements follow the same logic as in the Markovian case, with districts replacing nodes throughout. We defer these finite-discrete districtwise results, together with related computational and query-level remarks, to Appendix A.3 .
The exact characterisations above answer the question of when a common constructive transport operator exists. However, its real value is in the approximate case: when no exact map exists, the best approximate one still certifies query-level intervals ℓ ≤ Q t ≤ L \ell\leq Q_{t}\leq L , turning approximation error into a quantitative notion of approximate transportability, which is informative even for non-identifiable queries ( ℛ 1 \mathcal{R}_{1} ) and when learned from source data alone, with no target data ( ℛ 2 \mathcal{R}_{2} ). This motivates the approximate notion introduced next.
5 Approximate Model-level Transportability
The characterisation results of Section 4 are exact: they ask whether a single common kernel or map transports the relevant source interventional family to the target family exactly . In many practically important settings, however, such exact consistency is too strong to be expected. We therefore relax exact equality to a discrepancy between the two sides of the desired transport relation. This yields a quantitative notion of model-level transport error, analogous to abstraction error in causal abstraction, but specialised to the transportability setting. Let 𝖣 \mathsf{D} be any nonnegative discrepancy on probability measures, with 𝖣 ⁡ ( μ , ν ) = 0 \mathsf{D}(\mu,\nu)=0 iff μ = ν \mu=\nu .
Definition 16 (Intervention-query transport error) .
For a common Markov kernel K K , an intervention-query pair ( ι , O ι ) ∈ 𝒬 (\iota,O_{\iota})\in\mathcal{Q} , and discrepancy 𝖣 \mathsf{D} , define the intervention-query transport error
e K ( ι , O ι ) := 𝖣 ⁡ ( π O ι ​ # ​ ( P s ( ι ) ​ K ) , π O ι ​ # ​ P t ( ι ) ) . e_{K}^{(\iota,O_{\iota})}:=\mathsf{D}\!\left(\pi_{O_{\iota}\#}\bigl(P_{s}^{(\iota)}K\bigr),\;\pi_{O_{\iota}\#}P_{t}^{(\iota)}\right). (41)
Thus e K ( ι , O ι ) e_{K}^{(\iota,O_{\iota})} measures, for a fixed intervention and designated output set, how far the transported source interventional distribution is from the target one. Figure 5 depicts this comparison as a commuting diagram: the transported law P s ( ι ) ​ K P_{s}^{(\iota)}K and the true target P t ( ι ) P_{t}^{(\iota)} are compared in the target space, and the residual after projecting both onto the query coordinates O ι O_{\iota} is exactly e K ( ι , O ι ) e_{K}^{(\iota,O_{\iota})} . Aggregating these pointwise errors over the relevant query family yields the model-level transport error.
Definition 17 ( 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -transport error) .
Let K K be a common Markov kernel and 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} a query specification family. The 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -restricted transport error of K K is defined as:
ℰ 𝒬 ​ ( K ) := 1 | 𝒬 | ​ ∑ ( ι , O ι ) ∈ 𝒬 e K ( ι , O ι ) . \mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{Q}}(K):=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{Q}|}\sum_{(\iota,O_{\iota})\in\mathcal{Q}}e_{K}^{(\iota,O_{\iota})}. (42)
Remark 14 .
In the full post-interventional case O ι = [ d ] ∖ J ι O_{\iota}=[d]\setminus J_{\iota} for every ι ∈ ℐ \iota\in{\cal I} , ℰ 𝒬 ​ ( K ) \mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{Q}}(K) reduces to the model-level average abstraction error ( Felekis et al., 2024 ; Dyer et al., 2024 ; Kekić et al., 2024 ) .
Restricting the admissible kernel class to deterministic kernels recovers the approximate abstraction map problem, while restricting it to the constructive class recovers the approximate constructive problem. Vanishing transport error is equivalent to exact 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -restricted consistency, and the infimum is attained under standard compactness/lower-semicontinuity conditions; both facts are formalised in Appendix B .
In the present setting, ℰ 𝒬 ​ ( K ) \mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{Q}}(K) measures how far a single common transport operator is from transporting the relevant source interventional family to the target one. For this reason, it admits a structural interpretation as a model-level transport error . The definition above is abstract and applies to arbitrary SCM classes. To make it useful, we next specialise in the concrete parametric setting of linear additive noise models.
Unconstrained 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} ( O ι = [ d ] ∖ J ι O_{\iota}=[d]\setminus J_{\iota} ) Constrained 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} ( O ι ⊂ [ d ] ∖ J ι O_{\iota}\subset[d]\setminus J_{\iota} ) P s P_{s} P s ( ι ) P_{s}^{(\iota)} P t P_{t} P s ( ι ) ​ K P_{s}^{(\iota)}K P t ( ι ) P_{t}^{(\iota)} π O ι ​ # ​ ( P s ( ι ) ​ K ) \pi_{O_{\iota}\#}\bigl(P_{s}^{(\iota)}K\bigr) π O ι ​ # ​ P t ( ι ) \pi_{O_{\iota}\#}P_{t}^{(\iota)} ι \iota K K K K ι \iota e K ( ι ) e_{K}^{(\iota)} π O ι \pi_{O_{\iota}} π O ι \pi_{O_{\iota}} e K ( ι , O ι ) e_{K}^{(\iota,O_{\iota})}
Figure 5 : Computation of the intervention-query error.
