From the source observational law P s P_{s} , two pathways are compared: (a) intervene with ι \iota on the source, then map to the target via K K (yielding P s ( ι ) ​ K P_{s}^{(\iota)}K ); and (b) map via K K , then apply ι \iota (yielding P t ( ι ) P_{t}^{(\iota)} ). Their gap is the full-joint error e K ( ι ) e_{K}^{(\iota)} for the unconstrained regime O ι = [ d ] ∖ J ι O_{\iota}=[d]\setminus J_{\iota} . Projecting both laws onto the query coordinates via π O ι \pi_{O_{\iota}} ( O ι ⊂ [ d ] ∖ J ι O_{\iota}\subset[d]\setminus J_{\iota} ) instead gives the pointwise error e K ( ι , O ι ) e_{K}^{(\iota,O_{\iota})} ; averaging this over all pairs in 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} yields the 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -restricted transport error ℰ 𝒬 \mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{Q}} .
6 Modelling shifts in Linear Additive Noise models
6.1 Setting and Structural Forms
We specifically consider Linear Additive Noise Models (LANs), where structural assignments take the form
𝒳 = W ​ 𝒳 + 𝒰 , \mathcal{X}=W\mathcal{X}+\mathcal{U}, (43)
where 𝒳 ∈ ℝ d \mathcal{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{d} , 𝒰 ∈ ℝ d \mathcal{U}\in\mathbb{R}^{d} , and W ∈ ℝ d × d W\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d} is a weighted adjacency matrix strictly lower-triangular under a fixed topological ordering. Here W i ​ j W_{ij} is the coefficient of X j X_{j} in the structural equation for X i X_{i} , and it is nonzero only when X j → X i X_{j}\to X_{i} ; each row of W W therefore collects the incoming edges of one node, i.e., its mechanism. The reduced form of the SCM becomes 𝒳 = A ​ 𝒰 \mathcal{X}=A\mathcal{U} , with the mixing matrix A ≔ ( I − W ) − 1 A\coloneq(I-W)^{-1} , which is called the propagator . Since W W is strictly lower triangular, it is nilpotent: W d = 0 W^{d}=0 . Hence, ( I − W ) (I-W) is automatically invertible, and its inverse admits a finite polynomial expansion. Thus, the propagator can be written as:
A = ( I − W ) − 1 = I + W + W 2 + ⋯ + W d − 1 A=(I-W)^{-1}=I+W+W^{2}+\cdots+W^{d-1} (44)
Since W W is strictly lower triangular, each power W k W^{k} is lower triangular, and therefore A A is lower triangular as well. The matrix W W encodes direct causal mechanisms (local edge weights), whereas A = ( I − W ) − 1 A=(I-W)^{-1} encodes total causal effects (global propagation along all directed paths).
Let ι ∈ ℐ \iota\in\mathcal{I} denote a hard intervention acting on a set of nodes J ι ⊂ { 1 , … , d } J_{\iota}\subset\{1,\dots,d\} . As mentioned, a hard intervention disables the structural equations of the intervened nodes (equivalently, it removes all incoming edges into J ι J_{\iota} ). We represent this via a diagonal gating matrix R ι ∈ ℝ d × d R_{\iota}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d} :
( R ι ) j ​ j = { 0 , j ∈ J ι , 1 , otherwise . \displaystyle(R_{\iota})_{jj}=\begin{cases}0,&j\in J_{\iota},\\ 1,&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases} (45)
The interventional propagator is then A ι := ( I − R ι ​ W ) − 1 A_{\iota}:=(I-R_{\iota}W)^{-1} . Since R ι ​ W R_{\iota}W remains strictly lower triangular, A ι A_{\iota} also admits a finite polynomial expansion analogous to ( 44 ). The LAN reduced form 𝒳 = A ​ 𝒰 \mathcal{X}=A\mathcal{U} makes the two possible sources of domain shift clear: the environment 𝒰 \mathcal{U} may change, the mechanism A = ( I − W ) − 1 A=(I-W)^{-1} may change, or both may change jointly. In the LAN instantiations below, we therefore model target uncertainty with separate mechanism and environment perturbations.
6.2 Exact Perturbation Identities
Under a mechanism perturbation Δ ​ W \Delta W , necessarily strictly lower triangular under the same fixed ordering, so that W ′ = W + Δ ​ W W^{\prime}=W+\Delta W remains a valid LAN structural matrix, the perturbed propagator A ′ = ( I − W ′ ) − 1 A^{\prime}=(I-W^{\prime})^{-1} always exists and remains lower triangular. The resolvent identity B − 1 − C − 1 = B − 1 ​ ( C − B ) ​ C − 1 B^{-1}-C^{-1}=B^{-1}(C-B)C^{-1} , applied with B = I − W − Δ ​ W B=I-W-\Delta W and C = I − W C=I-W , gives
A ′ − A = A ′ ​ Δ ​ W ​ A . A^{\prime}-A=A^{\prime}\Delta WA. (46)
This is exact but implicit : A ′ A^{\prime} still appears on the right-hand side. To obtain an explicit expansion involving only A A and Δ ​ W \Delta W , we use the factorisation A ′ = ( I − A ​ Δ ​ W ) − 1 ​ A A^{\prime}=(I-A\Delta W)^{-1}A . Note that A ​ Δ ​ W A\Delta W is strictly lower triangular because A A is lower triangular with unit diagonal, whereas Δ ​ W \Delta W is strictly lower triangular. Hence ( A ​ Δ ​ W ) d = 0 (A\Delta W)^{d}=0 , so A ​ Δ ​ W A\Delta W is nilpotent, and we obtain the finite expansion:
A ′ = ( ∑ k = 0 d − 1 ( A ​ Δ ​ W ) k ) ​ A . A^{\prime}=\left(\sum_{k=0}^{d-1}(A\Delta W)^{k}\right)A. (47)
No convergence condition is required since the series terminates after d − 1 d-1 terms. Subtracting A A gives the exact identity
A ′ − A = ∑ k = 1 d − 1 ( A ​ Δ ​ W ) k ​ A . A^{\prime}-A=\sum_{k=1}^{d-1}(A\Delta W)^{k}A. (48)
Under intervention ι \iota , analogous identities hold with A ι A_{\iota} and R ι ​ Δ ​ W R_{\iota}\Delta W .
First-order structure.
The leading term in ( 48 ) is
A ′ = A + A ​ Δ ​ W ​ A + higher-order terms . A^{\prime}\;=\;A+A\Delta WA+\text{higher-order terms}. (49)
Even if Δ ​ W \Delta W is sparse, it appears in the sandwiched form A ​ Δ ​ W ​ A A\Delta WA . Since A A aggregates propagation along all directed paths in the DAG, a localised mechanism perturbation can induce a global change in the propagator.
6.3 Structured Stability Bounds
Throughout this subsection, let ∥ ⋅ ∥ \|\cdot\| denote any submultiplicative matrix norm. Let Δ ​ W \Delta W be an admissible mechanism perturbation preserving the fixed topological order, and for each intervention ι ∈ ℐ \iota\in\mathcal{I} , define the perturbed interventional propagator
A ι ′ := ( I − R ι ​ ( W + Δ ​ W ) ) − 1 . A^{\prime}_{\iota}:=(I-R_{\iota}(W+\Delta W))^{-1}. (50)
Since R ι ​ ( W + Δ ​ W ) R_{\iota}(W+\Delta W) remains strictly lower triangular, A ι ′ A^{\prime}_{\iota} is always well defined. Let 𝒜 W \mathcal{A}_{W} denote the admissible set of mechanism perturbations preserving the fixed acyclic structural class. To quantify the effect of mechanism perturbations after intervention, we introduce two intervention-specific quantities. The first is the amplification factor , which measures the stability of the nominal propagator A ι A_{\iota} under admissible mechanism perturbations:
γ ι := sup Δ ​ W ∈ 𝒜 W ‖ A ι ​ ( R ι ​ Δ ​ W ) ‖ , \gamma_{\iota}:=\sup_{\Delta W\in\mathcal{A}_{W}}\|A_{\iota}(R_{\iota}\Delta W)\|, (51)
The second is the corresponding propagator stability modulus , which controls the deviation between the perturbed and nominal propagators
α ι := sup Δ ​ W ∈ 𝒜 W ‖ A ι ′ − A ι ‖ , \alpha_{\iota}:=\sup_{\Delta W\in\mathcal{A}_{W}}\|A^{\prime}_{\iota}-A_{\iota}\|, (52)
The exact perturbation identity from Section 6.2 gives, for every admissible Δ ​ W \Delta W ,
A ι ′ − A ι = ∑ k = 1 d − 1 ( A ι ​ ( R ι ​ Δ ​ W ) ) k ​ A ι . A^{\prime}_{\iota}-A_{\iota}=\sum_{k=1}^{d-1}\bigl(A_{\iota}(R_{\iota}\Delta W)\bigr)^{k}A_{\iota}. (53)
This immediately yields the following bound.
Proposition 15 (Polynomial stability bound) .
For every intervention ι ∈ ℐ \iota\in\mathcal{I} ,
α ι ≤ ‖ A ι ‖ ​ ∑ k = 1 d − 1 γ ι k . \alpha_{\iota}\leq\|A_{\iota}\|\sum_{k=1}^{d-1}\gamma_{\iota}^{k}. (54)
Proof.
Fix Δ ​ W ∈ 𝒜 W \Delta W\in\mathcal{A}_{W} . By ( 53 ), subadditivity and submultiplicativity,
‖ A ι ′ − A ι ‖ ≤ ∑ k = 1 d − 1 ‖ A ι ​ ( R ι ​ Δ ​ W ) ‖ k ​ ‖ A ι ‖ . \displaystyle\|A^{\prime}_{\iota}-A_{\iota}\|\leq\sum_{k=1}^{d-1}\|A_{\iota}(R_{\iota}\Delta W)\|^{k}\,\|A_{\iota}\|. (55)
Since ‖ A ι ​ ( R ι ​ Δ ​ W ) ‖ ≤ γ ι \|A_{\iota}(R_{\iota}\Delta W)\|\leq\gamma_{\iota} by definition of γ ι \gamma_{\iota} , taking sup over Δ ​ W ∈ 𝒜 W \Delta W\in\mathcal{A}_{W} gives ( 54 ). ∎
The existence of A ι ′ A^{\prime}_{\iota} is already guaranteed by acyclicity, as noted above. Proposition 15 adds the quantitative half, controlling its sensitivity through γ ι \gamma_{\iota} . Thus, structural perturbations are amplified polynomially in γ ι \gamma_{\iota} . When γ ι \gamma_{\iota} is small, the dominant term is linear in ‖ Δ ​ W ‖ \|\Delta W\| , yielding a local Lipschitz regime. A sharper control is available in the small-perturbation regime γ ι < 1 \gamma_{\iota}<1 , using the Neumann-series bound: for any matrix T T with ‖ T ‖ < 1 \|T\|<1 ,
( I − T ) − 1 = ∑ m = 0 ∞ T m , ‖ ( I − T ) − 1 ‖ ≤ ∑ m = 0 ∞ ‖ T ‖ m = 1 1 − ‖ T ‖ . \displaystyle(I-T)^{-1}=\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}T^{m},\qquad\|(I-T)^{-1}\|\leq\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\|T\|^{m}=\frac{1}{1-\|T\|}. (56)
Applying this with T = A ι ​ ( R ι ​ Δ ​ W ) T=A_{\iota}(R_{\iota}\Delta W) yields the following sharper control.
Proposition 16 (Neumann-regime stability bound) .
If γ ι < 1 \gamma_{\iota}<1 , then
‖ A ι ′ ‖ ≤ ‖ A ι ‖ 1 − γ ι , and ​ α ι ≤ ‖ A ι ‖ 2 1 − γ ι ​ sup Δ ​ W ∈ 𝒜 W ‖ R ι ​ Δ ​ W ‖ . \displaystyle\|A^{\prime}_{\iota}\|\leq\frac{\|A_{\iota}\|}{1-\gamma_{\iota}},~~\text{and}~~\alpha_{\iota}\leq\frac{\|A_{\iota}\|^{2}}{1-\gamma_{\iota}}\sup_{\Delta W\in\mathcal{A}_{W}}\|R_{\iota}\Delta W\|. (57)
Proof.
Using the factorisation A ι ′ = ( I − A ι ​ ( R ι ​ Δ ​ W ) ) − 1 ​ A ι A^{\prime}_{\iota}=\bigl(I-A_{\iota}(R_{\iota}\Delta W)\bigr)^{-1}A_{\iota} , the assumption γ ι < 1 \gamma_{\iota}<1 implies ‖ A ι ​ ( R ι ​ Δ ​ W ) ‖ < 1 \|A_{\iota}(R_{\iota}\Delta W)\|<1 for every admissible Δ ​ W \Delta W . Hence, the Neumann-series bound gives
‖ ( I − A ι ​ ( R ι ​ Δ ​ W ) ) − 1 ‖ ≤ 1 1 − γ ι ⟹ ‖ A ι ′ ‖ ≤ ‖ A ι ‖ 1 − γ ι . \displaystyle\bigl\|\bigl(I-A_{\iota}(R_{\iota}\Delta W)\bigr)^{-1}\bigr\|\leq\frac{1}{1-\gamma_{\iota}}\quad\implies\|A^{\prime}_{\iota}\|\leq\frac{\|A_{\iota}\|}{1-\gamma_{\iota}}. (58)
Moreover,
A ι ′ − A ι = ( I − A ι ​ ( R ι ​ Δ ​ W ) ) − 1 ​ A ι ​ ( R ι ​ Δ ​ W ) ​ A ι ⟹ ‖ A ι ′ − A ι ‖ ≤ 1 1 − γ ι ​ ‖ A ι ‖ 2 ​ ‖ R ι ​ Δ ​ W ‖ . \displaystyle A^{\prime}_{\iota}-A_{\iota}=\bigl(I-A_{\iota}(R_{\iota}\Delta W)\bigr)^{-1}A_{\iota}(R_{\iota}\Delta W)A_{\iota}\quad\implies\|A^{\prime}_{\iota}-A_{\iota}\|\leq\frac{1}{1-\gamma_{\iota}}\,\|A_{\iota}\|^{2}\,\|R_{\iota}\Delta W\|. (59)
Taking the sup over Δ ​ W ∈ 𝒜 W \Delta W\in\mathcal{A}_{W} yields the second bound. ∎
The Neumann regime is therefore not needed for invertibility, which is already guaranteed by acyclicity but only for sharper quantitative control. In that regime, perturbation amplification is governed by the geometric factor ( 1 − γ ι ) − 1 (1-\gamma_{\iota})^{-1} . The preceding bounds hold for any admissible mechanism ambiguity set 𝒜 W \mathcal{A}_{W} within the fixed acyclic structural class. In the optimisation section, we instantiate 𝒜 W \mathcal{A}_{W} using several geometries: Frobenius, row-wise, column-wise, and entrywise; and derive the corresponding explicit controls of γ ι \gamma_{\iota} and α ι \alpha_{\iota} .
These stability bounds provide the quantitative control needed for robust learning: they turn local perturbations of the structural matrix into explicit bounds on the induced perturbations of the post-interventional propagators. The next section uses this control to formulate TraCA as a target-agnostic robust optimisation problem.
7 Transportation via Causal Abstractions: TraCA
(a) Classical DRO internal validity / single population Source ℙ ^ s \widehat{\mathbb{P}}_{s} ℙ s ⋆ \mathbb{P}_{s}^{\star} ℙ s wc \mathbb{P}_{s}^{\mathrm{wc}} ε \varepsilon The ambiguity set is: i. centered on the empirical source distribution; ii. hedges against sampling error (b) TraCA external validity / transportability Source ℙ s ⋆ \mathbb{P}_{s}^{\star} trusted SCM Target τ \tau ℙ t ⋆ \mathbb{P}_{t}^{\star} ℙ t wc \mathbb{P}_{t}^{\mathrm{wc}} ε \varepsilon The ambiguity set: i. centered on the available source distribution; ii. hedges against domain shift
Figure 6 : Two uses of distributional robustness. (a) Classical DRO and (b) TraCA share a minimax form, but the ambiguity set 𝔹 ε ​ ( ⋅ ) \mathbb{B}_{\varepsilon}(\cdot) (dashed ball) plays different roles. In Classical DRO (internal validity): the set is centered on the empirical source ℙ ^ s \widehat{\mathbb{P}}_{s} and hedges against sampling error ; the true law ℙ s ⋆ \mathbb{P}_{s}^{\star} is assumed to lie in it, and the minimax is evaluated at the worst case ℙ s wc \mathbb{P}_{s}^{\mathrm{wc}} . In TraCA (external validity): the set is centered on the trusted source ℙ s ⋆ \mathbb{P}_{s}^{\star} , and each admissible target perturbs it by a mechanism and an environment shift; the true target ℙ t ⋆ \mathbb{P}_{t}^{\star} is assumed to lie in it, and τ \tau is evaluated against the worst case ℙ t wc \mathbb{P}_{t}^{\mathrm{wc}} . Markers: center, worst case, true law.
Section 5 introduced approximate model-level transportability through the 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -transport error ℰ 𝒬 ​ ( K ) \mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{Q}}(K) , which measures how far a single common operator is from transporting the relevant source interventional family to the target one. Meanwhile, Section 6 provided the linear-additive setting and the stability quantities needed to control mechanism perturbations. We now combine these two ingredients in the practically important regime of domain generalisation, where the target domain is not observed ( ℛ 2 \mathcal{R}_{2} ): we move beyond the classical transportability setting ( 𝒟 s o , 𝒟 s i , 𝒟 t o ) (\mathcal{D}_{s}^{o},\mathcal{D}_{s}^{i},\mathcal{D}_{t}^{o}) to a target-agnostic regime in which 𝒟 t o \mathcal{D}_{t}^{o} is unavailable at training time. Given a source SCM, structural knowledge of which mechanisms may vary across domains, and an ambiguity model for the target environment, the goal is to learn a single transport map that remains reliable across a class of plausible target domains. This naturally suggests a distributionally robust formulation, but in a different sense from classical DRO. In DRO, the ambiguity set models uncertainty about the true data-generating distribution itself, typically around an empirical approximation of that same distribution.
In TraCA, by contrast, we take the source SCM as the trusted reference model and use DRO for domain generalisation : the ambiguity set does not represent uncertainty about the observed source domain but rather uncertainty about the unseen target domain to which the transport map will later be applied. Thus, the robust problem is not to hedge against the misspecification of the source data generating process but against structurally plausible shifts away from it. Since the target domain is unobserved, a candidate map cannot be evaluated against the true target family. In the same spirit as DiRoCA ( Felekis et al., 2026 ) , we therefore replace the target term in the transport error with its worst case over an ambiguity set, but where DiRoCA’s set captures uncertainty about a single system’s own environment, ours captures uncertainty about a distinct, unobserved population. This yields the target-agnostic robust counterpart of the earlier model-level transport error: instead of evaluating a candidate map against one fixed target family, we evaluate it against the most adverse target family consistent with the assumed structural and environmental uncertainty. This ambiguity set is not fixed once and for all. Prior domain knowledge constrains its shape : which mechanisms may shift, which entries may change, and what patterns of shift are plausible. Partial target information, when available, can instead shrink the radius of the ambiguity set around a better localised target. In TraCA, this ambiguity has two distinct components: uncertainty in the target environment (exogenous distribution) and uncertainty in the target mechanisms. The distinction is essential since a change in the reduced-form mechanism cannot, in general, be reproduced by keeping the mechanism fixed and changing only the exogenous law; see Appendix C for a formal counterexample. We therefore model both sources of target-side uncertainty explicitly.
7.1 Unified minimax formulation
The general DRO template is
inf x ∈ 𝒳 sup ℚ ∈ 𝔹 𝔼 ξ ∼ ℚ ​ [ f ⁡ ( x , ξ ) ] , \inf_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\sup_{\mathbb{Q}\in\mathbb{B}}\mathbb{E}_{\xi\sim\mathbb{Q}}[f(x,\xi)], (60)
where x x is a decision variable, ξ \xi is an uncertain quantity, f f is a loss, and 𝔹 \mathbb{B} is an ambiguity set of plausible distributions. In TraCA, the decision variable is the transport map T \operatorname{T} , and the uncertainty lies entirely on the target side. Accordingly, TraCA learns a transport map T \operatorname{T} by minimising a worst-case transport loss over a target-side ambiguity set:
min T ⁡ max ξ ∈ 𝒜 ​ 1 | ℐ | ​ ∑ ι ∈ ℐ 𝖥 ι ​ ( T , ξ ) , \min_{\operatorname{T}}\max_{\xi\in\mathcal{A}}\frac{1}{|\mathcal{I}|}\sum_{\iota\in\mathcal{I}}\mathsf{F}_{\iota}(\operatorname{T},\xi), (61)
where ξ \xi collects the adversarial target-side variables and 𝒜 \mathcal{A} denotes the corresponding ambiguity set. The term 𝖥 ι \mathsf{F}_{\iota} denotes the same loss family evaluated under intervention ι \iota , with intervention-specific propagators and target-side adversarial variables; it thereby instantiates the pointwise transport error e K ( ι , O ι ) e_{K}^{(\iota,O_{\iota})} . This objective should be read as the target-agnostic robust version of the earlier 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -transport error: in the absence of target observations, the unknown target SCM is replaced by the worst admissible target model. We denote the optimal value of ( 61 ) by ℜ ⋆ ​ ( 𝒜 ) \mathfrak{R}^{\star}(\mathcal{A}) . We focus on the LAN setting from Section 6 . In particular, we introduce a mechanism adversary Δ ​ W \Delta W , which, for each intervention ι ∈ ℐ \iota\in\mathcal{I} , perturbs the target propagator from A ι A_{\iota} to A ι ′ ​ ( Δ ​ W ) A^{\prime}_{\iota}(\Delta W) :
A ι := ( I − R ι ​ W ) − 1 , A ι ′ ​ ( Δ ​ W ) := ( I − R ι ​ ( W + Δ ​ W ) ) − 1 . A_{\iota}:=(I-R_{\iota}W)^{-1},\qquad A^{\prime}_{\iota}(\Delta W):=(I-R_{\iota}(W+\Delta W))^{-1}. (62)
The matrix A ι A_{\iota} is the nominal source propagator under intervention ι \iota , while A ι ′ ​ ( Δ ​ W ) A^{\prime}_{\iota}(\Delta W) is its perturbed target counterpart induced by a mechanism shift Δ ​ W \Delta W .
To encode the assumption that only a designated set of mechanisms may vary across domains, we define the shifted node row mask M 𝒦 := diag ( 𝟏 { 1 ∈ 𝒦 } , … , 𝟏 { d ∈ 𝒦 } ) M_{\mathcal{K}}:=\diag\bigl(\mathbf{1}\{1\in\mathcal{K}\},\dots,\mathbf{1}\{d\in\mathcal{K}\}\bigr) , where 𝒦 ⊆ [ d ] \mathcal{K}\subseteq[d] is the set of shifted nodes (Section 3.1 ). All admissible mechanism perturbations are required to satisfy
Δ ​ W = M 𝒦 ​ Δ ​ W . \Delta W=M_{\mathcal{K}}\Delta W. (63)
Hence, the ambiguity is not arbitrary: it is structurally restricted to the shifted mechanisms and may be further refined if prior knowledge suggests that only certain magnitudes or patterns of shift are plausible. Furthermore, we specialise the generic stability quantities of Section 6.3 to the spectral norm and write
γ ι := sup Δ ​ W ∈ 𝒜 W ‖ A ι ​ ( R ι ​ Δ ​ W ) ‖ 2 , α ι := sup Δ ​ W ∈ 𝒜 W ‖ A ι ′ ​ ( Δ ​ W ) − A ι ‖ 2 . \gamma_{\iota}:=\sup_{\Delta W\in\mathcal{A}_{W}}\|A_{\iota}(R_{\iota}\Delta W)\|_{2},\qquad\alpha_{\iota}:=\sup_{\Delta W\in\mathcal{A}_{W}}\|A^{\prime}_{\iota}(\Delta W)-A_{\iota}\|_{2}. (64)
Here γ ι \gamma_{\iota} and α ι \alpha_{\iota} are the spectral-norm specialisations of the general stability quantities of Section 6.3 ; the bounds of Corollary 19 make them explicit for each ambiguity geometry. The optimisation objectives and robustness bounds below use these quantities
‖ A ι ′ ​ ( Δ ​ W ) − A ι ‖ 2 ≤ α ι , ‖ A ι ′ ​ ( Δ ​ W ) ‖ 2 ≤ ‖ A ι ‖ 2 + α ι , \|A^{\prime}_{\iota}(\Delta W)-A_{\iota}\|_{2}\leq\alpha_{\iota},\qquad\|A^{\prime}_{\iota}(\Delta W)\|_{2}\leq\|A_{\iota}\|_{2}+\alpha_{\iota}, (65)
with explicit control of α ι \alpha_{\iota} supplied by the structured stability bounds of Section 6.3 .
Remark 17 .
The robust value ℜ ⋆ ​ ( 𝒜 ) := inf T sup ξ ∈ 𝒜 1 | ℐ | ​ ∑ ι ∈ ℐ 𝖥 ι ​ ( T , ξ ) \mathfrak{R}^{\star}(\mathcal{A}):=\inf_{\operatorname{T}}\sup_{\xi\in\mathcal{A}}\tfrac{1}{|\mathcal{I}|}\sum_{\iota\in\mathcal{I}}\mathsf{F}_{\iota}(\operatorname{T},\xi) scales monotonically with target-side information. Shrinking the ambiguity set can only lower the robust objective and tighten the induced certificate: if 𝒜 1 ⊆ 𝒜 2 \mathcal{A}_{1}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{2} then ℜ ⋆ ​ ( 𝒜 1 ) ≤ ℜ ⋆ ​ ( 𝒜 2 ) \mathfrak{R}^{\star}(\mathcal{A}_{1})\leq\mathfrak{R}^{\star}(\mathcal{A}_{2}) , and whenever the true target perturbation ξ ∘ \xi^{\circ} lies in 𝒜 1 \mathcal{A}_{1} , the 𝒜 1 \mathcal{A}_{1} -robust map already certifies the true target loss, 1 | ℐ | ​ ∑ ι 𝖥 ι ​ ( T 1 ⋆ , ξ ∘ ) ≤ ℜ ⋆ ​ ( 𝒜 1 ) \tfrac{1}{|\mathcal{I}|}\sum_{\iota}\mathsf{F}_{\iota}(\operatorname{T}_{1}^{\star},\xi^{\circ})\leq\mathfrak{R}^{\star}(\mathcal{A}_{1}) . When the losses are Lipschitz in ξ \xi , the gap to the oracle value ℜ ⋆ ​ ( { ξ ∘ } ) \mathfrak{R}^{\star}(\{\xi^{\circ}\}) is controlled by the ambiguity diameter, so the robust problem approaches the full-information oracle as the ambiguity shrinks to ξ ∘ \xi^{\circ} . These are direct consequences of the minimax structure; we provide them in full detail in Appendix D .
The minimax formulation above is intentionally generic: it specifies the target-side adversary and the robust objective, but not yet how the target ambiguity is parameterised in practice. Two natural choices arise from the structure of available source-side knowledge. When the source environment is summarised by sufficient statistics (mean and covariance), a moment-based Gaussian formulation matches the natural geometry. When the source environment is summarised by samples, a perturbation-based empirical formulation matches the natural geometry. We treat both since they share an algorithmic backbone but differ in the ascent objective and projection operators.
8 Optimisation
Section 7 formulated TraCA as a generic target-agnostic minimax problem. We now instantiate that framework in two concrete forms: a Gaussian moment-based version and an empirical perturbation-based version. Both are realisations of the same robust objective ( 61 ) and differ only in how the unseen target environment is parameterised. The min-max optimisation backbone common to both: the alternating projected gradient descent-ascent scheme and, in the empirical case, the Frobenius-budgeted noise perturbations follows the logic of DiRoCA ( Felekis et al., 2026 ) . A note on their relation can be found in Appendix I .
Gaussian case.
The adversary is
ξ = ( Δ ​ W , μ t , Σ t ) ∈ 𝒜 W × 𝒜 ρ ​ ( ε ) , \displaystyle\xi=(\Delta W,\mu_{t},\Sigma_{t})\in\mathcal{A}_{W}\times\mathcal{A}_{\rho}(\varepsilon), (66)
and the intervention-wise loss is
𝖥 ι ρ ​ ( T , Δ ​ W , μ t , Σ t ) := 𝒲 2 2 ​ ( 𝒩 ⁡ ( T ⁡ A ι ​ μ s , T ⁡ A ι ​ Σ s ​ A ι ⊤ ​ T ⊤ ) , 𝒩 ⁡ ( A ι ′ ​ ( Δ ​ W ) ​ μ t , A ι ′ ​ ( Δ ​ W ) ​ Σ t ​ A ι ′ ​ ( Δ ​ W ) ⊤ ) ) . \displaystyle\mathsf{F}_{\iota}^{\rho}(\operatorname{T},\Delta W,\mu_{t},\Sigma_{t}):=\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}\!\Bigl(\mathcal{N}(\operatorname{T}A_{\iota}\mu_{s},\;\operatorname{T}A_{\iota}\Sigma_{s}A_{\iota}^{\top}\operatorname{T}^{\top}),\;\mathcal{N}(A^{\prime}_{\iota}(\Delta W)\mu_{t},\;A^{\prime}_{\iota}(\Delta W)\Sigma_{t}A^{\prime}_{\iota}(\Delta W)^{\top})\Bigr). (67)
Empirical case.
The adversary is
ξ = ( Δ ​ W , Θ ) ∈ 𝒜 W × 𝒜 U ​ ( ε ) , \displaystyle\xi=(\Delta W,\Theta)\in\mathcal{A}_{W}\times\mathcal{A}_{U}(\varepsilon), (68)
and the intervention-wise loss is
𝖥 ι U ​ ( T , Δ ​ W , Θ ) := ‖ T ⁡ A ι ​ U s − A ι ′ ​ ( Δ ​ W ) ​ ( U s + Θ ) ‖ F 2 . \displaystyle\mathsf{F}_{\iota}^{U}(\operatorname{T},\Delta W,\Theta):=\bigl\|\operatorname{T}A_{\iota}U^{s}-A^{\prime}_{\iota}(\Delta W)(U^{s}+\Theta)\bigr\|_{F}^{2}. (69)
The admissible transport maps T \operatorname{T} are restricted to the constructive class: diagonal in the Markovian case and block-diagonal by district in the semi-Markovian case. No analogue of the intervened-value pinning K i ( ⋅ ∣ a ) = δ a K_{i}(\cdot\mid a)=\delta_{a} of Theorem 32 is required here. Under ι \iota , the rows of A ι ′ ​ ( Δ ​ W ) A^{\prime}_{\iota}(\Delta W) indexed by J ι J_{\iota} equal the corresponding standard basis vectors for every admissible Δ ​ W \Delta W , so the intervened coordinates carry the intervention value and nothing else. Since O ι ⊆ [ d ] ∖ J ι O_{\iota}\subseteq[d]\setminus J_{\iota} , these coordinates are excluded from every 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -restricted loss, and the corresponding rows of T \operatorname{T} do not enter any certified quantity.
Min-Max optimisation architecture.
Both formulations use the same algorithmic backbone. At each outer iteration, one first updates T \operatorname{T} by projected gradient descent, holding the adversarial variables fixed, and then updates the target-side adversarial block by projected/proximal ascent, followed by the feasibility corrections required by the corresponding ambiguity set. The Gaussian and empirical formulations, therefore, differ only in the adversarial variables, the ascent objective used for them, and the associated projection/proximal operators.
8.1 Mechanism ambiguity sets and projections
We begin with the mechanism ambiguity set 𝒜 W \mathcal{A}_{W} , since it is shared by both optimisation problems; the environment-side ambiguity sets 𝒜 ρ ​ ( ε ) \mathcal{A}_{\rho}(\varepsilon) and 𝒜 U ​ ( ε ) \mathcal{A}_{U}(\varepsilon) are introduced later in the Gaussian and empirical subsections, respectively. The set 𝒜 W \mathcal{A}_{W} constrains admissible perturbations Δ ​ W \Delta W of the target structural matrix. Throughout, these perturbations are understood to preserve the fixed acyclic support of W W (equivalently, the same topological ordering/triangular pattern), although we suppress this structural constraint from the notation for readability. We consider four geometries for 𝒜 W \mathcal{A}_{W} , each paired with its natural projection operator for the projected-gradient ascent update in Δ ​ W \Delta W .
Frobenius ball.
𝒜 W F ( η ) := { Δ W : Δ W = M 𝒦 Δ W , ∥ Δ W ∥ F ≤ η } . \displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{W}^{\mathrm{F}}(\eta):=\Bigl\{\Delta W:\Delta W=M_{\mathcal{K}}\Delta W,\ \|\Delta W\|_{F}\leq\eta\Bigr\}. (70)
Its Euclidean projection is
proj 𝒜 W F ​ ( η ) ⁡ ( Δ ~ ) = Δ ~ ​ min ⁡ ( 1 , η ‖ Δ ~ ‖ F ) . \proj_{\mathcal{A}_{W}^{\mathrm{F}}(\eta)}(\widetilde{\Delta})=\widetilde{\Delta}\min\!\left(1,\frac{\eta}{\|\widetilde{\Delta}\|_{F}}\right). (71)
Row-wise budgets.
Given row budgets ρ = ( ρ j ) j ∈ 𝒦 \rho=(\rho_{j})_{j\in\mathcal{K}} ,
𝒜 W row ( ρ ) := { Δ W : Δ W = M 𝒦 Δ W , ∑ k = 1 d | Δ W j ​ k | ≤ ρ j ∀ j ∈ 𝒦 } . \displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{W}^{\mathrm{row}}(\rho):=\Bigl\{\Delta W:\Delta W=M_{\mathcal{K}}\Delta W,\ \sum_{k=1}^{d}|\Delta W_{jk}|\leq\rho_{j}\ \forall j\in\mathcal{K}\Bigr\}. (72)
Projection is row-wise ℓ 1 \ell_{1} projection:
proj 𝒜 W row ​ ( ρ ) ( Δ ~ ) j , : = proj ∥ ⋅ ∥ 1 ≤ ρ j ( Δ ~ j , : ) . \proj_{\mathcal{A}_{W}^{\mathrm{row}}(\rho)}(\widetilde{\Delta})_{j,:}=\proj_{\|\cdot\|_{1}\leq\rho_{j}}(\widetilde{\Delta}_{j,:}). (73)
Column-wise budgets.
Given column budgets c = ( c k ) k = 1 d c=(c_{k})_{k=1}^{d} ,
𝒜 W col ( c ) := { Δ W : Δ W = M 𝒦 Δ W , ∑ j = 1 d | Δ W j ​ k | ≤ c k ∀ k } . \displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{W}^{\mathrm{col}}(c):=\Bigl\{\Delta W:\Delta W=M_{\mathcal{K}}\Delta W,\ \sum_{j=1}^{d}|\Delta W_{jk}|\leq c_{k}\ \forall k\Bigr\}. (74)
Projection is column-wise ℓ 1 \ell_{1} projection:
proj 𝒜 W col ​ ( c ) ( Δ ~ ) : , k = proj ∥ ⋅ ∥ 1 ≤ c k ( Δ ~ : , k ) . \proj_{\mathcal{A}_{W}^{\mathrm{col}}(c)}(\widetilde{\Delta})_{:,k}=\proj_{\|\cdot\|_{1}\leq c_{k}}(\widetilde{\Delta}_{:,k}). (75)
Entrywise box.
Given entrywise radii B = ( b j ​ k ) B=(b_{jk}) with b j ​ k = 0 b_{jk}=0 for j ∉ 𝒦 j\notin\mathcal{K} ,
𝒜 W box ( B ) := { Δ W : Δ W = M 𝒦 Δ W , | Δ W j ​ k | ≤ b j ​ k ∀ j , k } . \displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{W}^{\mathrm{box}}(B):=\Bigl\{\Delta W:\Delta W=M_{\mathcal{K}}\Delta W,\ |\Delta W_{jk}|\leq b_{jk}\ \forall j,k\Bigr\}. (76)
Projection is entrywise clipping:
proj 𝒜 W box ​ ( B ) ⁡ ( Δ ~ ) j ​ k = sign ⁡ ( Δ ~ j ​ k ) ​ min ⁡ ( | Δ ~ j ​ k | , b j ​ k ) . \proj_{\mathcal{A}_{W}^{\mathrm{box}}(B)}(\widetilde{\Delta})_{jk}=\sign(\widetilde{\Delta}_{jk})\,\min\bigl(|\widetilde{\Delta}_{jk}|,\,b_{jk}\bigr). (77)
Directional entrywise box.
The entrywise box above is centered at the source, encoding symmetric uncertainty Δ ​ W j ​ k ∈ [ − b j ​ k , b j ​ k ] \Delta W_{jk}\in[-b_{jk},b_{jk}] about each coefficient. When the practitioner has directional prior knowledge; i.e., a belief that a coefficient shifts predominantly in a known direction, this is encoded by recentering the box at an offset δ = ( δ j ​ k ) \delta=(\delta_{jk}) with δ j ​ k = 0 \delta_{jk}=0 for j ∉ 𝒦 j\notin\mathcal{K} :
𝒜 W box ( δ , B ) := { Δ W : Δ W = M 𝒦 Δ W , | Δ W j ​ k − δ j ​ k | ≤ b j ​ k ∀ j , k } , \displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{W}^{\mathrm{box}}(\delta,B):=\Bigl\{\Delta W:\Delta W=M_{\mathcal{K}}\Delta W,\ |\Delta W_{jk}-\delta_{jk}|\leq b_{jk}\ \forall j,k\Bigr\}, (78)
so that Δ ​ W j ​ k ∈ [ δ j ​ k − b j ​ k , δ j ​ k + b j ​ k ] \Delta W_{jk}\in[\delta_{jk}-b_{jk},\,\delta_{jk}+b_{jk}] : the admissible targets are now those near an anticipated shifted mechanism rather than those near the source one. The offset indicates which way the coefficient is expected to move and by how much, and b j ​ k b_{jk} indicates how sure one is of that. The projection is the shifted clipping
proj 𝒜 W box ​ ( δ , B ) ⁡ ( Δ ~ ) j ​ k = δ j ​ k + sign ⁡ ( Δ ~ j ​ k − δ j ​ k ) ​ min ⁡ ( | Δ ~ j ​ k − δ j ​ k | , b j ​ k ) , \displaystyle\proj_{\mathcal{A}_{W}^{\mathrm{box}}(\delta,B)}(\widetilde{\Delta})_{jk}=\delta_{jk}+\sign(\widetilde{\Delta}_{jk}-\delta_{jk})\,\min\bigl(|\widetilde{\Delta}_{jk}-\delta_{jk}|,\,b_{jk}\bigr), (79)
and setting δ = 0 \delta=0 recovers the symmetric box ( 77 ) exactly. The offset δ \delta enters the optimisation only through this recentering; the adversary, the transport update, and the feasibility corrections are otherwise unchanged. This recentering changes the solution qualitatively. At δ = 0 \delta=0 , the box is symmetric about the origin, so the opposing vertices ± B \pm B contribute equal and opposite transport gradients, and the identity map remains stationary; at δ ≠ 0 \delta\neq 0 , the vertex at δ + B \delta+B lies farther from the origin, the contributions no longer cancel, and T \operatorname{T} is driven away from the identity. The directional prior therefore yields a nontrivial point correction by construction, independently of the loss. The practical role of the directional prior is discussed in Section 11 .
Remark 18 .
The radius constraints above control only the size of the mechanism perturbation. The validity of the perturbed target model comes from the structural restriction that admissible Δ ​ W \Delta W preserve the fixed acyclic support class of W W . Thus, W + Δ ​ W W+\Delta W remains a valid linear acyclic SCM, and hence for every intervention ι \iota , A ι ′ ​ ( Δ ​ W ) A^{\prime}_{\iota}(\Delta W) is well defined.
Interpretive guide to mechanism ambiguity sets.
The choice of 𝒜 W \mathcal{A}_{W} should reflect the type of prior knowledge available about the target-side mechanism shift. The table below summarises the four geometries used in TraCA.
Modelling intent Ambiguity set Budget parameter
The total magnitude of the mechanism perturbation is globally bounded Frobenius scalar η \eta
Each shifted structural equation may change by at most a prescribed amount Row-wise vector ρ = ( ρ j ) j ∈ 𝒦 \rho=(\rho_{j})_{j\in\mathcal{K}}
The total change in the outgoing influence of each parent variable is bounded Column-wise vector c = ( c k ) k = 1 d c=(c_{k})_{k=1}^{d}
A separate bound is known for each individual structural coefficient Entrywise matrix B = ( b j ​ k ) B=(b_{jk})
Once a geometry for 𝒜 W \mathcal{A}_{W} is fixed, the stability analysis of Section 6.3 yields an explicit bound on the amplification factor γ ι \gamma_{\iota} , and hence, via the polynomial or Neumann bounds from Propositions 15 and 16 an explicit bound on the propagator stability modulus α ι \alpha_{\iota} .
Corollary 19 .
For every intervention ι ∈ ℐ \iota\in\mathcal{I} , the following bounds hold:
Δ ​ W ∈ 𝒜 W F ​ ( η ) \displaystyle\Delta W\in\mathcal{A}_{W}^{\mathrm{F}}(\eta) ⟹ γ ι ≤ ‖ A ι ‖ 2 ​ η , \displaystyle\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\gamma_{\iota}\leq\|A_{\iota}\|_{2}\,\eta, (80)
Δ ​ W ∈ 𝒜 W row ​ ( ρ ) \displaystyle\Delta W\in\mathcal{A}_{W}^{\mathrm{row}}(\rho) ⟹ γ ι ≤ d ​ max ⁡ ∑ j ∈ 𝒦 ℓ ⁡ | ( A ι ) ℓ ​ j | ​ ρ j , \displaystyle\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\gamma_{\iota}\leq\sqrt{d}\,\max_{\ell}\sum_{j\in\mathcal{K}}|(A_{\iota})_{\ell j}|\,\rho_{j}, (81)
Δ ​ W ∈ 𝒜 W col ​ ( c ) \displaystyle\Delta W\in\mathcal{A}_{W}^{\mathrm{col}}(c) ⟹ γ ι ≤ d ​ ‖ A ι ‖ 1 ​ max k ​ c k , \displaystyle\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\gamma_{\iota}\leq\sqrt{d}\,\|A_{\iota}\|_{1}\max_{k}c_{k}, (82)
Δ ​ W ∈ 𝒜 W box ​ ( B ) \displaystyle\Delta W\in\mathcal{A}_{W}^{\mathrm{box}}(B) ⟹ γ ι ≤ ‖ | A ι | ​ R ι ​ B ‖ F , \displaystyle\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\gamma_{\iota}\leq\bigl\||A_{\iota}|R_{\iota}B\bigr\|_{F}, (83)
Δ ​ W ∈ 𝒜 W box ​ ( δ , B ) \displaystyle\Delta W\in\mathcal{A}_{W}^{\mathrm{box}}(\delta,B) ⟹ γ ι ≤ ‖ | A ι | ​ R ι ​ ( | δ | + B ) ‖ F , \displaystyle\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\gamma_{\iota}\leq\bigl\||A_{\iota}|R_{\iota}(|\delta|+B)\bigr\|_{F}, (84)
where | δ | + B |\delta|+B collects the effective half-widths | δ j ​ k | + b j ​ k |\delta_{jk}|+b_{jk} , reducing to ( 83 ) at δ = 0 \delta=0 . Substituting any of ( 80 )-( 84 ) into Propositions 15 and 16 yields explicit control of the propagator stability modulus α ι \alpha_{\iota} . The proof is given in Appendix C.1 .
Unified alternating solver.
Both the Gaussian and empirical formulations use the same high-level optimisation pattern: alternating descent in the transport map T \operatorname{T} and ascent in the target-side adversarial variables. The difference between the two formulations lies only in how the target-side environment perturbation is parameterised and projected back to its feasible set. Algorithm 1 is the common optimisation backbone of TraCA. The Gaussian and empirical formulations differ only in the parameterisation of the target-side environment block, the ascent objective used for that block, and the associated feasibility correction. The analytical algorithms can be found in Appendices F.1 and F.2 .
Algorithm 1 Unified TraCA optimisation
1: Initialise T ( 0 ) \operatorname{T}^{(0)} , Δ ​ W ( 0 ) = 0 \Delta W^{(0)}=0 , and the target-side adversarial variables.
2: repeat
3: Update T \operatorname{T} by projected gradient descent on the current objective.
4: Update Δ ​ W \Delta W by gradient ascent, followed by masking and projection onto the mechanism ambiguity set 𝒜 W \mathcal{A}_{W} .
5: Update the target-side environment variables by ascent, followed by the corresponding feasibility correction/projection.
6: until convergence
7: return T \operatorname{T}
8.2 Gaussian adversarial learning
Assume the source environment is Gaussian, ρ s ∼ 𝒩 ⁡ ( μ s , Σ s ) \rho^{s}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu_{s},\Sigma_{s}) , with Σ s \Sigma_{s} diagonal in the Markovian case. The Gaussian intervention-wise loss ( 67 ) compares the transported source law and the adversarial target law under intervention ι \iota . Concretely,
τ # ​ P s ( ι ) = 𝒩 ⁡ ( T ⁡ A ι ​ μ s , T ⁡ A ι ​ Σ s ​ A ι ⊤ ​ T ⊤ ) , \displaystyle\tau_{\#}P_{s}^{(\iota)}=\mathcal{N}\bigl(\operatorname{T}A_{\iota}\mu_{s},\;\operatorname{T}A_{\iota}\Sigma_{s}A_{\iota}^{\top}\operatorname{T}^{\top}\bigr), (85)
while
P t ( ι ) ​ ( Δ ​ W , μ t , Σ t ) = 𝒩 ⁡ ( A ι ′ ​ ( Δ ​ W ) ​ μ t , A ι ′ ​ ( Δ ​ W ) ​ Σ t ​ A ι ′ ​ ( Δ ​ W ) ⊤ ) . \displaystyle P_{t}^{(\iota)}(\Delta W,\mu_{t},\Sigma_{t})=\mathcal{N}\bigl(A^{\prime}_{\iota}(\Delta W)\mu_{t},\;A^{\prime}_{\iota}(\Delta W)\Sigma_{t}A^{\prime}_{\iota}(\Delta W)^{\top}\bigr). (86)
Under ι = d ​ o ​ ( X J ι = v J ι ) \iota=do(X_{J_{\iota}}=v_{J_{\iota}}) , the coordinates in J ι J_{\iota} are fixed deterministically to the intervention values v J ι v_{J_{\iota}} in both the source and target; therefore, the displayed Gaussian laws describe the random post-interventional coordinates, which are equivalent to the full post-interventional law with the deterministic intervened coordinates suppressed. Concretely, the intervention value enters as a deterministic offset in the exogenous mean at the coordinates in J ι J_{\iota} , whose own exogenous contribution is removed; the propagator then carries it forward as usual. The effective exogenous mean under ι \iota , which we write μ s ( ι ) \mu_{s}^{(\iota)} , therefore holds the source noise mean at free coordinates and the intervention value at clamped ones.
Environment ambiguity.
Let 𝒜 ρ ​ ( ε ) \mathcal{A}_{\rho}(\varepsilon) denote the target-side Gaussian Wasserstein ambiguity set centered at ( μ s , Σ s ) (\mu_{s},\Sigma_{s}) , the Gelbrich ball ( Gelbrich, 1990 ) :
𝒜 ρ ​ ( ε ) := { ( μ t , Σ t ) : 𝒲 2 2 ​ ( 𝒩 ⁡ ( μ t , Σ t ) , 𝒩 ⁡ ( μ s , Σ s ) ) ≤ ε 2 } , \displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{\rho}(\varepsilon):=\Bigl\{(\mu_{t},\Sigma_{t}):\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}\!\bigl(\mathcal{N}(\mu_{t},\Sigma_{t}),\,\mathcal{N}(\mu_{s},\Sigma_{s})\bigr)\leq\varepsilon^{2}\Bigr\}, (87)
together with the invariance pinning μ i t = μ s , i \mu_{i}^{t}=\mu_{s,i} for i ∉ 𝒦 i\notin\mathcal{K} , and Σ i ​ j t = ( Σ s ) i ​ j \Sigma_{ij}^{t}=(\Sigma_{s})_{ij} whenever i ∉ 𝒦 i\notin\mathcal{K} or j ∉ 𝒦 j\notin\mathcal{K} . Accordingly, the Gaussian objective specialises ( 61 ) to the average intervention-wise loss ( 67 ) over ℐ \mathcal{I} :
F ⁡ ( T , Δ ​ W , μ t , Σ t ) := 1 | ℐ | ​ ∑ ι ∈ ℐ 𝖥 ι ρ ​ ( T , Δ ​ W , μ t , Σ t ) . \displaystyle F(\operatorname{T},\Delta W,\mu_{t},\Sigma_{t}):=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{I}|}\sum_{\iota\in\mathcal{I}}\mathsf{F}_{\iota}^{\rho}(\operatorname{T},\Delta W,\mu_{t},\Sigma_{t}). (88)
and the robust Gaussian abstraction error is
ℰ ρ joint ​ ( T ) := sup Δ ​ W ∈ 𝒜 W ( μ t , Σ t ) ∈ 𝒜 ρ ​ ( ε ) F ⁡ ( T , Δ ​ W , μ t , Σ t ) . \mathcal{E}_{\rho}^{\mathrm{joint}}(\operatorname{T}):=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\Delta W\in\mathcal{A}_{W}\\ (\mu_{t},\Sigma_{t})\in\mathcal{A}_{\rho}(\varepsilon)\end{subarray}}F(\operatorname{T},\Delta W,\mu_{t},\Sigma_{t}). (89)
The Wasserstein distance between Gaussians has a closed form:
𝒲 2 2 ​ ( 𝒩 ⁡ ( m 1 , C 1 ) , 𝒩 ⁡ ( m 2 , C 2 ) ) = ‖ m 1 − m 2 ‖ 2 2 + Tr ⁡ ( C 1 ) + Tr ⁡ ( C 2 ) − 2 ​ Tr ⁡ ( ( C 1 1 / 2 ​ C 2 ​ C 1 1 / 2 ) 1 / 2 ) \displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}\bigl(\mathcal{N}(m_{1},C_{1}),\mathcal{N}(m_{2},C_{2})\bigr)=\|m_{1}-m_{2}\|_{2}^{2}+\Tr(C_{1})+\Tr(C_{2})-2\Tr\bigl((C_{1}^{1/2}C_{2}C_{1}^{1/2})^{1/2}\bigr) (90)
Let C ι , s ​ ( T ) := T ⁡ A ι ​ Σ s ​ A ι ⊤ ​ T ⊤ , C ι , t ​ ( Δ ​ W , Σ t ) := A ι ′ ​ ( Δ ​ W ) ​ Σ t ​ A ι ′ ​ ( Δ ​ W ) ⊤ C_{\iota,s}(\operatorname{T}):=\operatorname{T}A_{\iota}\Sigma_{s}A_{\iota}^{\top}\operatorname{T}^{\top},~C_{\iota,t}(\Delta W,\Sigma_{t}):=A^{\prime}_{\iota}(\Delta W)\Sigma_{t}A^{\prime}_{\iota}(\Delta W)^{\top} . Then,
F ⁡ ( T , Δ ​ W , μ t , Σ t ) \displaystyle F(\operatorname{T},\Delta W,\mu_{t},\Sigma_{t}) = 1 | ℐ | ​ ∑ ι ∈ ℐ [ ‖ T ⁡ A ι ​ μ s − A ι ′ ​ ( Δ ​ W ) ​ μ t ‖ 2 2 + Tr ⁡ ( C ι , s ​ ( T ) ) + Tr ⁡ ( C ι , t ​ ( Δ ​ W , Σ t ) ) ] ⏟ F s ρ ​ (smooth term) \displaystyle=\underbrace{\frac{1}{|\mathcal{I}|}\sum_{\iota\in\mathcal{I}}\Big[\|\operatorname{T}A_{\iota}\mu_{s}-A^{\prime}_{\iota}(\Delta W)\mu_{t}\|_{2}^{2}+\Tr\!\bigl(C_{\iota,s}(\operatorname{T})\bigr)+\Tr\!\bigl(C_{\iota,t}(\Delta W,\Sigma_{t})\bigr)\Big]}_{F^{\rho}_{s}\ \text{(smooth term)}}
− 2 | ℐ | ∑ ι ∈ ℐ Tr ( ( C ι , s ( T ) 1 / 2 C ι , t ( Δ W , Σ t ) C ι , s ( T ) 1 / 2 ) 1 / 2 ) ⏟ F n ρ ​ (Bures cross-term) . \displaystyle\quad\underbrace{-\frac{2}{|\mathcal{I}|}\sum_{\iota\in\mathcal{I}}\Tr\!\Bigl(\bigl(C_{\iota,s}(\operatorname{T})^{1/2}C_{\iota,t}(\Delta W,\Sigma_{t})C_{\iota,s}(\operatorname{T})^{1/2}\bigr)^{1/2}\Bigr)}_{F^{\rho}_{n}\ \text{(Bures cross-term)}}. (91)
Smooth surrogate for the ascent step.
As in the DiRoCA optimiser ( Felekis et al., 2026 ) , we replace the Bures cross-term with a smooth lower surrogate. Since
Tr ⁡ ( ( C ι , s 1 / 2 ​ C ι , t ​ C ι , s 1 / 2 ) 1 / 2 ) = ‖ C ι , s 1 / 2 ​ C ι , t 1 / 2 ‖ ∗ , \displaystyle\Tr\!\Bigl(\bigl(C_{\iota,s}^{1/2}C_{\iota,t}C_{\iota,s}^{1/2}\bigr)^{1/2}\Bigr)=\|C_{\iota,s}^{1/2}C_{\iota,t}^{1/2}\|_{*}, (92)
the variational upper bound ‖ X ​ Y ‖ ∗ ≤ ‖ X ‖ F ​ ‖ Y ‖ F \|XY\|_{*}\leq\|X\|_{F}\|Y\|_{F} ( Srebro et al., 2004 ) , yields:
F ~ ρ ​ ( T , Δ ​ W , μ t , Σ t ) := F s ρ ​ ( T , Δ ​ W , μ t , Σ t ) − 2 | ℐ | ​ ∑ ι ∈ ℐ ‖ C ι , s ​ ( T ) 1 / 2 ‖ F ​ ‖ C ι , t ​ ( Δ ​ W , Σ t ) 1 / 2 ‖ F , \widetilde{F}^{\rho}(\operatorname{T},\Delta W,\mu_{t},\Sigma_{t}):=F_{s}^{\rho}(\operatorname{T},\Delta W,\mu_{t},\Sigma_{t})-\frac{2}{|\mathcal{I}|}\sum_{\iota\in\mathcal{I}}\|C_{\iota,s}(\operatorname{T})^{1/2}\|_{F}\,\|C_{\iota,t}(\Delta W,\Sigma_{t})^{1/2}\|_{F}, (93)
and F ~ ρ ≤ F ρ \widetilde{F}^{\rho}\leq F^{\rho} . Since the Bures cross-term is upper bounded by the Frobenius product and appears with a minus sign in ( 91 ), this replacement yields a conservative lower bound on the true Gaussian abstraction error, suitable for gradient-based ascent. This suggests performing the covariance update at the level of the transformed target covariance roots C ι , t ​ ( Δ ​ W , Σ t ) 1 / 2 C_{\iota,t}(\Delta W,\Sigma_{t})^{1/2} , rather than directly at the level of Σ t \Sigma_{t} .
Min-Max updates.
Following the shared alternating architecture from Section 7.1 , the Gaussian formulation updates T \operatorname{T} by projected gradient descent on the exact objective ( 91 ). With T \operatorname{T} fixed, the adversarial block ( Δ ​ W , μ t , Σ t ) (\Delta W,\mu_{t},\Sigma_{t}) is then updated by ascent on the smooth surrogate ( 93 ): Δ ​ W \Delta W by projected gradient ascent onto the chosen mechanism ambiguity set 𝒜 W \mathcal{A}_{W} , μ t \mu_{t} by gradient ascent, and Σ t \Sigma_{t} by a proximal-gradient update acting on the square roots of the transformed target covariances C ι , t ​ ( Δ ​ W , Σ t ) C_{\iota,t}(\Delta W,\Sigma_{t}) . After each adversarial update, the invariance constraints outside 𝒦 \mathcal{K} are re-imposed via Π inv ​ ( μ , Σ ) := ( μ ~ , Σ ~ ) \Pi_{\mathrm{inv}}(\mu,\Sigma):=(\tilde{\mu},\tilde{\Sigma}) , where μ ~ i = μ s , i \tilde{\mu}_{i}=\mu_{s,i} for i ∉ 𝒦 i\notin\mathcal{K} and μ ~ i = μ i \tilde{\mu}_{i}=\mu_{i} for i ∈ 𝒦 i\in\mathcal{K} , while Σ ~ i ​ j = ( Σ s ) i ​ j \tilde{\Sigma}_{ij}=(\Sigma_{s})_{ij} whenever i ∉ 𝒦 i\notin\mathcal{K} or j ∉ 𝒦 j\notin\mathcal{K} , and Σ ~ i ​ j = Σ i ​ j \tilde{\Sigma}_{ij}=\Sigma_{ij} otherwise; and the pair ( μ t , Σ t ) (\mu_{t},\Sigma_{t}) is projected back to the Gelbrich ball 𝒜 ρ ​ ( ε ) \mathcal{A}_{\rho}(\varepsilon) . The full sequence of updates is given in Algorithm 2 of Appendix F.1 .
Remark 20 .
The Gaussian optimiser follows the logic of the Gaussian DiRoCA optimiser, but with two TraCA-specific additions: the mechanism adversary Δ ​ W \Delta W and the invariance projection Π inv \Pi_{\mathrm{inv}} . Gradients with respect to Δ ​ W \Delta W are computed using the standard derivative of an inverse identity ( Petersen and Pedersen, 2008 ) : D ⁡ ( X − 1 ) ​ [ H ] = − X − 1 ​ ( D ​ X ​ [ H ] ) ​ X − 1 \mathrm{D}(X^{-1})[H]=-\,X^{-1}(\mathrm{D}X[H])X^{-1} . Applying this to X ⁡ ( Δ ​ W ) := I − R ι ​ ( W + Δ ​ W ) , X(\Delta W):=I-R_{\iota}(W+\Delta W), so that: A ι ′ ​ ( Δ ​ W ) = X ​ ( Δ ​ W ) − 1 A^{\prime}_{\iota}(\Delta W)=X(\Delta W)^{-1} , and noting that D ​ X ​ ( Δ ​ W ) ​ [ H ] = − R ι ​ H , \mathrm{D}X(\Delta W)[H]=-R_{\iota}H, yields
D ​ A ι ′ ​ ( Δ ​ W ) ​ [ H ] = A ι ′ ​ ( Δ ​ W ) ​ R ι ​ H ​ A ι ′ ​ ( Δ ​ W ) , \mathrm{D}A^{\prime}_{\iota}(\Delta W)[H]=A^{\prime}_{\iota}(\Delta W)\,R_{\iota}H\,A^{\prime}_{\iota}(\Delta W), (94)
for every matrix direction H H . The remaining implementation details follow the Gaussian DiRoCA template. First, the Bures cross-term is replaced in the ascent step by the same smooth lower surrogate F ~ \widetilde{F} . Second, the covariance block is handled through the transformed target covariances
C ι , t ​ ( Δ ​ W , Σ t ) := A ι ′ ​ ( Δ ​ W ) ​ Σ t ​ A ι ′ ​ ( Δ ​ W ) ⊤ , ι ∈ ℐ , \displaystyle C_{\iota,t}(\Delta W,\Sigma_{t}):=A^{\prime}_{\iota}(\Delta W)\Sigma_{t}A^{\prime}_{\iota}(\Delta W)^{\top},\qquad\iota\in\mathcal{I}, (95)
rather than directly through Σ t \Sigma_{t} alone. The proximal update is applied to the square roots C ι , t ​ ( Δ ​ W , Σ t ) 1 / 2 C_{\iota,t}(\Delta W,\Sigma_{t})^{1/2} via the Frobenius-norm proximal map
prox λ ∥ ⋅ ∥ F ( A ) = { ( 1 − λ ‖ A ‖ F ) ​ A , ‖ A ‖ F > λ , 0 , otherwise , \displaystyle\prox_{\lambda\|\cdot\|_{F}}(A)=\begin{cases}\left(1-\dfrac{\lambda}{\|A\|_{F}}\right)A,&\|A\|_{F}>\lambda,\\[4.30554pt] 0,&\text{otherwise},\end{cases} (96)
which induces the covariance update CovProx λ t \mathrm{CovProx}_{\lambda_{t}} : for each intervention, one applies the proximal map to the square root of the transformed covariance, pulls the result back to the exogenous covariance space, and then aggregates across interventions. Thus the proximal step acts on the square-root/Frobenius structure that remains in the surrogate ( 93 ), while the exact Bures cross-term itself has already been replaced by the smooth lower bound F ~ \widetilde{F} . Finally, after the adversarial covariance and mean updates, the pair ( μ t , Σ t ) (\mu_{t},\Sigma_{t}) is re-pinned by Π inv \Pi_{\mathrm{inv}} and projected onto the Gelbrich ball in ( μ , Σ 1 / 2 ) (\mu,\Sigma^{1/2}) -space.
8.3 Empirical adversarial learning
Let U s ∈ ℝ d × N U^{s}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times N} be the abducted source exogenous samples. The target empirical adversary perturbs them through U t = U s + Θ U^{t}=U^{s}+\Theta , where Θ ∈ ℝ d × N \Theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times N} is constrained to lie in the target-side ambiguity set
𝒜 U ( ε ) := { Θ : Θ = M 𝒦 Θ , ∥ Θ ∥ F ≤ ε N } . \mathcal{A}_{U}(\varepsilon):=\Bigl\{\Theta:\Theta=M_{\mathcal{K}}\Theta,\;\|\Theta\|_{F}\leq\varepsilon\sqrt{N}\Bigr\}. (97)
The factor N \sqrt{N} normalises for sample size, so ε \varepsilon controls the per-sample perturbation magnitude rather than the total. By the finite-dimensional reduction of Kuhn et al. (2019) , this Frobenius-ball constraint is analytically equivalent to restricting the target empirical environment to a Wasserstein ball of radius ε \varepsilon around the source. For each intervention ι ∈ ℐ \iota\in\mathcal{I} , the intervention-wise empirical loss from ( 69 ) takes the form
𝖥 ι U ​ ( T , Δ ​ W , Θ ) := ‖ T ⁡ A ι ​ U s − A ι ′ ​ ( Δ ​ W ) ​ ( U s + Θ ) ‖ F 2 . \mathsf{F}_{\iota}^{U}(\operatorname{T},\Delta W,\Theta):=\bigl\|\operatorname{T}A_{\iota}U^{s}-A^{\prime}_{\iota}(\Delta W)(U^{s}+\Theta)\bigr\|_{F}^{2}. (98)
Accordingly, the empirical objective specialises ( 61 ) to
F ⁡ ( T , Δ ​ W , Θ ) := 1 | ℐ | ​ ∑ ι ∈ ℐ 𝖥 ι U ​ ( T , Δ ​ W , Θ ) . F(\operatorname{T},\Delta W,\Theta):=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{I}|}\sum_{\iota\in\mathcal{I}}\mathsf{F}_{\iota}^{U}(\operatorname{T},\Delta W,\Theta). (99)
and the corresponding robust empirical abstraction error is
ℰ U joint ​ ( T ) := sup Δ ​ W ∈ 𝒜 W Θ ∈ 𝒜 U ​ ( ε ) F ⁡ ( T , Δ ​ W , Θ ) . \mathcal{E}_{U}^{\mathrm{joint}}(\operatorname{T}):=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\Delta W\in\mathcal{A}_{W}\\ \Theta\in\mathcal{A}_{U}(\varepsilon)\end{subarray}}F(\operatorname{T},\Delta W,\Theta). (100)
In the implementation, we work with the normalised empirical objective N − 1 ​ ℰ U joint ​ ( T ) N^{-1}\mathcal{E}_{U}^{\mathrm{joint}}(\operatorname{T}) , equivalently N − 1 ​ 𝖥 ι U N^{-1}\mathsf{F}_{\iota}^{U} at the intervention level. This does not change the minimiser in T \operatorname{T} , but keeps the empirical losses 𝒪 ⁡ ( 1 ) \mathcal{O}(1) as the sample size N N grows.
Min-Max updates.
Following the shared alternating architecture from Section 7.1 , the empirical formulation updates T \operatorname{T} by projected gradient descent on the exact empirical objective F ⁡ ( T , Δ ​ W , Θ ) F(\operatorname{T},\Delta W,\Theta) , followed by projection back to the constructive class. With T \operatorname{T} fixed, the adversarial variables Δ ​ W \Delta W and Θ \Theta are updated by projected gradient ascent on the same objective: Δ ​ W \Delta W is masked and projected onto the chosen mechanism ambiguity set 𝒜 W \mathcal{A}_{W} , while Θ \Theta is masked and projected onto the empirical ambiguity set 𝒜 U ​ ( ε ) \mathcal{A}_{U}(\varepsilon) . The full update sequence is given in Algorithm 3 of Appendix F.2 .
Remark 21 .
The empirical optimiser is the direct projected-gradient analogue of the empirical DiRoCA scheme, but now with a single target perturbation block Θ \Theta together with the mechanism block Δ ​ W \Delta W . The empirical perturbation is constrained by 𝒜 U ​ ( ε ) \mathcal{A}_{U}(\varepsilon) so the ascent step for Θ \Theta is followed by masking and projection onto a Frobenius ball. Concretely, for an intermediate update Θ ~ \widetilde{\Theta} , one first enforces the shifted-node structure via Θ ~ ← M 𝒦 ​ Θ ~ \widetilde{\Theta}\leftarrow M_{\mathcal{K}}\widetilde{\Theta} , and then applies the standard Frobenius rescaling
proj 𝒜 U ​ ( ε ) ⁡ ( Θ ~ ) = Θ ~ ​ min ⁡ ( 1 , ε ​ N ‖ Θ ~ ‖ F ) . \displaystyle\proj_{\mathcal{A}_{U}(\varepsilon)}(\widetilde{\Theta})=\widetilde{\Theta}\,\min\!\Bigl(1,\frac{\varepsilon\sqrt{N}}{\|\widetilde{\Theta}\|_{F}}\Bigr). (101)
Thus, the empirical adversary remains feasible after every ascent step. Gradients with respect to the mechanism perturbation Δ ​ W \Delta W again propagate through the perturbed interventional propagator via the resolvent identity ( 94 ).
8.4 Query-restricted relaxation
The full-joint objectives remain the conceptual center of TraCA, as they aim to align the full post-interventional family.
When only a designated output family matters, however, it is natural to restrict the loss to those coordinates. Recall the query family from Eq. ( 2 ),
𝒬 = { ( ι , O ι ) : ι ∈ ℐ , O ι ⊆ [ d ] ∖ J ι } , \displaystyle\mathcal{Q}=\{(\iota,O_{\iota}):\iota\in\mathcal{I},\;O_{\iota}\subseteq[d]\setminus J_{\iota}\}, (102)
with J ι J_{\iota} the intervened coordinates and O ι O_{\iota} the outputs of interest. For every nonempty O ⊆ [ d ] O\subseteq[d] , let
S O ∈ { 0 , 1 } | O | × d \displaystyle S_{O}\in\{0,1\}^{|O|\times d} (103)
denote the coordinate-selection matrix associated with the projection π O \pi_{O} , so that π O ​ ( x ) = S O ​ x \pi_{O}(x)=S_{O}x for x ∈ ℝ d x\in\mathbb{R}^{d} , and π O ​ ( X ) = S O ​ X \pi_{O}(X)=S_{O}X for X ∈ ℝ d × N X\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times N} . Query restriction changes only the loss target : the map T \operatorname{T} and the adversarial variables remain global.
Gaussian query-restricted loss.
In the Gaussian formulation, the full post-interventional source and target laws under intervention ι \iota are
T # ⁡ P s ( ι ) = 𝒩 ⁡ ( T ⁡ A ι ​ μ s , T ⁡ A ι ​ Σ s ​ A ι ⊤ ​ T ⊤ ) , \displaystyle\operatorname{T}_{\#}P_{s}^{(\iota)}=\mathcal{N}\bigl(\operatorname{T}A_{\iota}\mu_{s},\;\operatorname{T}A_{\iota}\Sigma_{s}A_{\iota}^{\top}\operatorname{T}^{\top}\bigr), (104)
and
P t ( ι ) ​ ( Δ ​ W , μ t , Σ t ) = 𝒩 ⁡ ( A ι ′ ​ ( Δ ​ W ) ​ μ t , A ι ′ ​ ( Δ ​ W ) ​ Σ t ​ A ι ′ ​ ( Δ ​ W ) ⊤ ) . \displaystyle P_{t}^{(\iota)}(\Delta W,\mu_{t},\Sigma_{t})=\mathcal{N}\bigl(A^{\prime}_{\iota}(\Delta W)\mu_{t},\;A^{\prime}_{\iota}(\Delta W)\Sigma_{t}A^{\prime}_{\iota}(\Delta W)^{\top}\bigr). (105)
Restricting attention to the coordinates in O ι O_{\iota} means pushing both laws forward through π O ι \pi_{O_{\iota}} , equivalently left- and bi-sided multiplication by S O ι S_{O_{\iota}} . This yields the intervention-wise 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -restricted Gaussian loss
𝖥 ( ι , O ι ) ρ , 𝒬 ​ ( T , Δ ​ W , μ t , Σ t ) \displaystyle\mathsf{F}_{(\iota,O_{\iota})}^{\rho,\mathcal{Q}}(\operatorname{T},\Delta W,\mu_{t},\Sigma_{t}) : = 𝒲 2 2 ​ ( 𝒩 ⁡ ( S O ι ​ T ⁡ A ι ​ μ s , S O ι ​ T ⁡ A ι ​ Σ s ​ A ι ⊤ ​ T ⊤ ​ S O ι ⊤ ) CLOSE , \displaystyle:=\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}\!\Bigl(\mathcal{N}\!\bigl(S_{O_{\iota}}\operatorname{T}A_{\iota}\mu_{s},\;S_{O_{\iota}}\operatorname{T}A_{\iota}\Sigma_{s}A_{\iota}^{\top}\operatorname{T}^{\top}S_{O_{\iota}}^{\top}\bigr),
OPEN 𝒩 ⁡ ( S O ι ​ A ι ′ ​ ( Δ ​ W ) ​ μ t , S O ι ​ A ι ′ ​ ( Δ ​ W ) ​ Σ t ​ A ι ′ ​ ( Δ ​ W ) ⊤ ​ S O ι ⊤ ) ) . \displaystyle\hskip 91.04872pt\mathcal{N}\!\bigl(S_{O_{\iota}}A^{\prime}_{\iota}(\Delta W)\mu_{t},\;S_{O_{\iota}}A^{\prime}_{\iota}(\Delta W)\Sigma_{t}A^{\prime}_{\iota}(\Delta W)^{\top}S_{O_{\iota}}^{\top}\bigr)\Bigr). (106)
Averaging over 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} , define
F 𝒬 ρ ​ ( T , Δ ​ W , μ t , Σ t ) := 1 | 𝒬 | ​ ∑ ( ι , O ι ) ∈ 𝒬 𝖥 ( ι , O ι ) ρ , 𝒬 ​ ( T , Δ ​ W , μ t , Σ t ) , F_{\mathcal{Q}}^{\rho}(\operatorname{T},\Delta W,\mu_{t},\Sigma_{t}):=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{Q}|}\sum_{(\iota,O_{\iota})\in\mathcal{Q}}\mathsf{F}_{(\iota,O_{\iota})}^{\rho,\mathcal{Q}}(\operatorname{T},\Delta W,\mu_{t},\Sigma_{t}), (107)
and the corresponding robust query-restricted Gaussian abstraction error
ℰ ρ , 𝒬 joint ​ ( T ) := sup Δ ​ W ∈ 𝒜 W ( μ t , Σ t ) ∈ 𝒜 ρ ​ ( ε ) F 𝒬 ρ ​ ( T , Δ ​ W , μ t , Σ t ) . \mathcal{E}_{\rho,\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{joint}}(\operatorname{T}):=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\Delta W\in\mathcal{A}_{W}\\ (\mu_{t},\Sigma_{t})\in\mathcal{A}_{\rho}(\varepsilon)\end{subarray}}F_{\mathcal{Q}}^{\rho}(\operatorname{T},\Delta W,\mu_{t},\Sigma_{t}). (108)
Remark 22 .
In the Gaussian case, query restriction must be imposed at the level of the marginal Gaussian itself. If S O ι S_{O_{\iota}} is the selector onto the coordinates in O ι O_{\iota} , then the relevant source and target laws are
𝒩 ⁡ ( S O ι ​ μ 1 , S O ι ​ Σ 1 ​ S O ι ⊤ ) , 𝒩 ⁡ ( S O ι ​ μ 2 , S O ι ​ Σ 2 ​ S O ι ⊤ ) , \displaystyle\mathcal{N}(S_{O_{\iota}}\mu_{1},\;S_{O_{\iota}}\Sigma_{1}S_{O_{\iota}}^{\top}),\qquad\mathcal{N}(S_{O_{\iota}}\mu_{2},\;S_{O_{\iota}}\Sigma_{2}S_{O_{\iota}}^{\top}), (109)
and the Wasserstein loss must be computed between these marginals. The reason is that the Gaussian 𝒲 2 2 \mathcal{W}_{2}^{2} formula contains the nonlinear Bures term, so coordinate restriction does not commute with the matrix square root; i.e. ( S O ι ​ C ​ S O ι ⊤ ) 1 / 2 ≠ S O ι ​ C 1 / 2 ​ S O ι ⊤ (S_{O_{\iota}}CS_{O_{\iota}}^{\top})^{1/2}\neq S_{O_{\iota}}C^{1/2}S_{O_{\iota}}^{\top} in general. Hence, one must first form the marginal covariance S O ι ​ C ​ S O ι ⊤ S_{O_{\iota}}CS_{O_{\iota}}^{\top} , and then compute its square root.
Empirical query-restricted loss.
In the empirical formulation, the full residual under intervention ι \iota is
R ι ​ ( T , Δ ​ W , Θ ) := T ⁡ A ι ​ U s − A ι ′ ​ ( Δ ​ W ) ​ ( U s + Θ ) ∈ ℝ d × N . \displaystyle R_{\iota}(\operatorname{T},\Delta W,\Theta):=\operatorname{T}A_{\iota}U^{s}-A^{\prime}_{\iota}(\Delta W)(U^{s}+\Theta)\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times N}. (110)
Query restriction simply selects the rows indexed by O ι O_{\iota} . Hence, the intervention-wise 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -restricted empirical loss is
𝖥 ( ι , O ι ) U , 𝒬 ​ ( T , Δ ​ W , Θ ) := ‖ S O ι ​ R ι ​ ( T , Δ ​ W , Θ ) ‖ F 2 = ‖ S O ι ​ T ⁡ A ι ​ U s − S O ι ​ A ι ′ ​ ( Δ ​ W ) ​ ( U s + Θ ) ‖ F 2 . \mathsf{F}_{(\iota,O_{\iota})}^{U,\mathcal{Q}}(\operatorname{T},\Delta W,\Theta):=\bigl\|S_{O_{\iota}}R_{\iota}(\operatorname{T},\Delta W,\Theta)\bigr\|_{F}^{2}=\bigl\|S_{O_{\iota}}\operatorname{T}A_{\iota}U^{s}-S_{O_{\iota}}A^{\prime}_{\iota}(\Delta W)(U^{s}+\Theta)\bigr\|_{F}^{2}. (111)
Averaging over 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} , define
F 𝒬 U ​ ( T , Δ ​ W , Θ ) := 1 | 𝒬 | ​ ∑ ( ι , O ι ) ∈ 𝒬 𝖥 ( ι , O ι ) U , 𝒬 ​ ( T , Δ ​ W , Θ ) , F_{\mathcal{Q}}^{U}(\operatorname{T},\Delta W,\Theta):=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{Q}|}\sum_{(\iota,O_{\iota})\in\mathcal{Q}}\mathsf{F}_{(\iota,O_{\iota})}^{U,\mathcal{Q}}(\operatorname{T},\Delta W,\Theta), (112)
and the corresponding robust query-restricted empirical abstraction error
ℰ U , 𝒬 joint ​ ( T ) := sup Δ ​ W ∈ 𝒜 W Θ ∈ 𝒜 U ​ ( ε ) F 𝒬 U ​ ( T , Δ ​ W , Θ ) . \mathcal{E}_{U,\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{joint}}(\operatorname{T}):=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\Delta W\in\mathcal{A}_{W}\\ \Theta\in\mathcal{A}_{U}(\varepsilon)\end{subarray}}F_{\mathcal{Q}}^{U}(\operatorname{T},\Delta W,\Theta). (113)
Remark 23 (Extreme cases of the 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -restricted objective) .
The 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -restricted formulation contains both the full-joint and single-query settings as special cases. If, for each intervention ι \iota , the selected output set O ι O_{\iota} is the full set of non-intervened coordinates, then π O ι ​ # \pi_{O_{\iota}\#} imposes no further restriction and one recovers the full post-interventional objective. At the other extreme, if 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} contains only one pair ( ι 0 , { k } ) (\iota_{0},\{k\}) , then the objective reduces to a single intervention/output loss: a robust transport guarantee for one target query, the query-level end of the framework. Thus 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} simply specifies how much of the post-interventional family the optimisation is required to match.
The 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -restricted formulation does not require a different optimisation architecture: one replaces the full post-interventional losses with their 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -restricted counterparts and applies the same alternating ascent-descent schemes. In the Gaussian case, the only additional care is that the Bures term and its smooth surrogate must be evaluated on the | O ι | × | O ι | |O_{\iota}|\times|O_{\iota}| marginal covariances for each ( ι , O ι ) ∈ 𝒬 (\iota,O_{\iota})\in\mathcal{Q} .
These optimisation problems are not merely algorithmic surrogates; their structure also permits explicit worst-case guarantees. We next show how the learned map and the stability moduli α ι \alpha_{\iota} yield certified bounds on both model-level transport error and downstream queries.
9 Provable Robustness and Query-Level Transport
The optimisation problems of Section 8 also yield explicit worst-case guarantees for the abstraction error. We first state the more general 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -restricted certificates. For each ( ι , O ι ) ∈ 𝒬 (\iota,O_{\iota})\in\mathcal{Q} , let S O ι ∈ { 0 , 1 } | O ι | × d S_{O_{\iota}}\in\{0,1\}^{|O_{\iota}|\times d} denote the selector for π O ι \pi_{O_{\iota}} . Full post-interventional guarantees are recovered by taking O ι = [ d ] ∖ J ι O_{\iota}=[d]\setminus J_{\iota} , and the single-query case when | 𝒬 | = 1 |\mathcal{Q}|=1 . Each certificate decomposes into transport, mechanism, and environment terms. Hence, the bounds are small when T \operatorname{T} is close to the identity on the relevant outputs, the intervention amplifies mechanism perturbations only mildly (small α ι \alpha_{\iota} ), and the admissible target environment is close to the source.
Theorem 24 (Query-restricted Gaussian certificate) .
Consider the Gaussian setting, with source law ( μ s , Σ s ) (\mu_{s},\Sigma_{s}) , linear map T \operatorname{T} , and mechanism ambiguity set 𝒜 W \mathcal{A}_{W} . For each ( ι , O ι ) ∈ 𝒬 (\iota,O_{\iota})\in\mathcal{Q} , define
δ ι , O ι ρ ​ ( T ) 2 := \displaystyle\delta_{\iota,O_{\iota}}^{\rho}(\operatorname{T})^{2}:=\; 4 ​ ‖ S O ι ​ ( T − I ) ​ A ι ​ μ s ‖ 2 2 + 4 ​ ‖ Σ s ‖ 2 ​ ‖ S O ι ​ ( T − I ) ​ A ι ‖ F 2 ⏟ transport mismatch \displaystyle\underbrace{4\|S_{O_{\iota}}(\operatorname{T}-I)A_{\iota}\mu_{s}\|_{2}^{2}+4\|\Sigma_{s}\|_{2}\,\|S_{O_{\iota}}(\operatorname{T}-I)A_{\iota}\|_{F}^{2}}_{\text{transport mismatch}}
+ 4 ​ α ι 2 ​ ‖ μ s ‖ 2 2 + 4 ​ | O ι | ​ ‖ Σ s ‖ 2 ​ α ι 2 ⏟ mechanism perturbation + 2 ​ ( ‖ S O ι ​ A ι ‖ 2 + α ι ) 2 ​ ε 2 ⏟ environment ambiguity . \displaystyle\quad+\underbrace{4\alpha_{\iota}^{2}\|\mu_{s}\|_{2}^{2}+4|O_{\iota}|\,\|\Sigma_{s}\|_{2}\,\alpha_{\iota}^{2}}_{\text{mechanism perturbation}}+\underbrace{2(\|S_{O_{\iota}}A_{\iota}\|_{2}+\alpha_{\iota})^{2}\varepsilon^{2}}_{\text{environment ambiguity}}. (114)
Then for every T \operatorname{T} ,
ℰ ρ , 𝒬 joint ​ ( T ) ≤ 1 | 𝒬 | ​ ∑ ( ι , O ι ) ∈ 𝒬 δ ι , O ι ρ ​ ( T ) 2 . \mathcal{E}_{\rho,\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{joint}}(\operatorname{T})\leq\frac{1}{|\mathcal{Q}|}\sum_{(\iota,O_{\iota})\in\mathcal{Q}}\delta_{\iota,O_{\iota}}^{\rho}(\operatorname{T})^{2}. (115)
In particular, any minimiser T ⋆ \operatorname{T}^{\star} of the 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -restricted Gaussian adversarial objective satisfies the same bound evaluated at T ⋆ \operatorname{T}^{\star} .
Proof.
(sketch) Fix ( ι , O ι ) ∈ 𝒬 (\iota,O_{\iota})\in\mathcal{Q} . We interpolate between the transported source law and the target law through an intermediate Gaussian that carries the perturbed target mechanism A ι ′ ​ ( Δ ​ W ) A^{\prime}_{\iota}(\Delta W) but retains the source environment ( μ s , Σ s ) (\mu_{s},\Sigma_{s}) ; applying the triangle inequality in 𝒲 2 \mathcal{W}_{2} then splits the error into two parts: one measuring the mismatch between the transported source law and the same-environment perturbed law, and one measuring the effect of changing the environment from ( μ s , Σ s ) (\mu_{s},\Sigma_{s}) to ( μ t , Σ t ) (\mu_{t},\Sigma_{t}) . The first term is controlled by coupling both laws through the same source Gaussian and writing
T ⁡ A ι − A ι ′ ​ ( Δ ​ W ) = ( T − I ) ​ A ι + ( A ι − A ι ′ ​ ( Δ ​ W ) ) . \operatorname{T}A_{\iota}-A^{\prime}_{\iota}(\Delta W)=(\operatorname{T}-I)A_{\iota}+(A_{\iota}-A^{\prime}_{\iota}(\Delta W)).
This separates transport mismatch from mechanism perturbation. The transport part gives the terms involving ( T − I ) ​ A ι (\operatorname{T}-I)A_{\iota} , while the perturbation part is controlled by the stability bound ‖ A ι ′ ​ ( Δ ​ W ) − A ι ‖ 2 ≤ α ι \|A^{\prime}_{\iota}(\Delta W)-A_{\iota}\|_{2}\leq\alpha_{\iota} .
The second term is controlled by the fact that pushforward by a fixed linear map is Lipschitz in 𝒲 2 \mathcal{W}_{2} . Since ( μ t , Σ t ) ∈ 𝒜 ρ ​ ( ε ) (\mu_{t},\Sigma_{t})\in\mathcal{A}_{\rho}(\varepsilon) , this contributes a factor ε \varepsilon , multiplied by the operator norm of the perturbed projected propagator, which is bounded by ‖ S O ι ​ A ι ‖ 2 + α ι \|S_{O_{\iota}}A_{\iota}\|_{2}+\alpha_{\iota} . Combining the two bounds yields the stated pointwise certificate, and averaging over ( ι , O ι ) ∈ 𝒬 (\iota,O_{\iota})\in\mathcal{Q} gives the result. ∎
The full analytical proof is deferred to Appendix E . Concretely, the mechanism part scales with α ι \alpha_{\iota} , so it grows when intervention ι \iota amplifies admissible mechanism perturbations. The environment part scales with the ambiguity radius ε \varepsilon and the projected propagator ‖ S O ι ​ A ι ‖ 2 + α ι \|S_{O_{\iota}}A_{\iota}\|_{2}+\alpha_{\iota} . Thus, the certificate grows with transport mismatch, intervention instability, and target-side uncertainty.
Theorem 25 (Query-restricted empirical certificate) .
For each ( ι , O ι ) ∈ 𝒬 (\iota,O_{\iota})\in\mathcal{Q} , define
δ ι , O ι U ​ ( T ) := ‖ S O ι ​ ( T − I ) ​ A ι ​ U s ‖ F ⏟ transport mismatch + α ι ​ ‖ U s ‖ F ⏟ mechanism perturbation + ε ​ N ​ ( ‖ S O ι ​ A ι ‖ 2 + α ι ) ⏟ environment ambiguity . \delta_{\iota,O_{\iota}}^{U}(\operatorname{T}):=\underbrace{\|S_{O_{\iota}}(\operatorname{T}-I)A_{\iota}U^{s}\|_{F}}_{\text{transport mismatch}}+\underbrace{\alpha_{\iota}\|U^{s}\|_{F}}_{\text{mechanism perturbation}}+\underbrace{\varepsilon\sqrt{N}\,(\|S_{O_{\iota}}A_{\iota}\|_{2}+\alpha_{\iota})}_{\text{environment ambiguity}}. (116)
Then for every T \operatorname{T} ,
ℰ U , 𝒬 joint ​ ( T ) ≤ 1 | 𝒬 | ​ ∑ ( ι , O ι ) ∈ 𝒬 δ ι , O ι U ​ ( T ) 2 . \mathcal{E}_{U,\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{joint}}(\operatorname{T})\leq\frac{1}{|\mathcal{Q}|}\sum_{(\iota,O_{\iota})\in\mathcal{Q}}\delta_{\iota,O_{\iota}}^{U}(\operatorname{T})^{2}. (117)
In particular, any minimiser T ⋆ \operatorname{T}^{\star} of the 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -restricted empirical adversarial objective satisfies the same bound evaluated at T ⋆ \operatorname{T}^{\star} .
Proof.
By the same three-term decomposition (transport, mechanism, environment) in Frobenius norm; see Appendix E . ∎
The empirical certificate has the same three-part structure in the Frobenius norm. The first term measures the residual transport error of T \operatorname{T} on the source sample; the second captures mechanism instability through α ι \alpha_{\iota} , and the third measures the effect of an admissible environment perturbation of radius ε ​ N \varepsilon\sqrt{N} after propagation through A ι A_{\iota} . As in the Gaussian case, the bound is larger when transport mismatch, mechanism instability, or target ambiguity are larger.
Proposition 26 (Full post-interventional recovery) .
Let 𝒬 full := { ( ι , [ d ] ∖ J ι ) : ι ∈ ℐ } \mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{full}}:=\{(\iota,[d]\setminus J_{\iota}):\iota\in\mathcal{I}\} . Then the query-restricted certificates of Theorems 24 and 25 specialise to the full post-interventional output family. In particular, for every T \operatorname{T} ,
ℰ ρ , 𝒬 full joint ( T ) ≤ 1 | ℐ | ∑ ι ∈ ℐ [ \displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\rho,\mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{full}}}^{\mathrm{joint}}(\operatorname{T})\leq\frac{1}{|\mathcal{I}|}\sum_{\iota\in\mathcal{I}}\Big[ 4 ​ ‖ S [ d ] ∖ J ι ​ ( T − I ) ​ A ι ​ μ s ‖ 2 2 + 4 ​ ‖ Σ s ‖ 2 ​ ‖ S [ d ] ∖ J ι ​ ( T − I ) ​ A ι ‖ F 2 \displaystyle 4\|S_{[d]\setminus J_{\iota}}(\operatorname{T}-I)A_{\iota}\mu_{s}\|_{2}^{2}+4\|\Sigma_{s}\|_{2}\,\|S_{[d]\setminus J_{\iota}}(\operatorname{T}-I)A_{\iota}\|_{F}^{2}
+ 4 α ι 2 ∥ μ s ∥ 2 2 + 4 ( d − | J ι | ) ∥ Σ s ∥ 2 α ι 2 + 2 ( ∥ S [ d ] ∖ J ι A ι ∥ 2 + α ι ) 2 ε 2 ] , \displaystyle+4\alpha_{\iota}^{2}\|\mu_{s}\|_{2}^{2}+4(d-|J_{\iota}|)\|\Sigma_{s}\|_{2}\,\alpha_{\iota}^{2}+2\bigl(\|S_{[d]\setminus J_{\iota}}A_{\iota}\|_{2}+\alpha_{\iota}\bigr)^{2}\varepsilon^{2}\Big], (118)
and
ℰ U , 𝒬 full joint ​ ( T ) ≤ 1 | ℐ | ​ ∑ ι ∈ ℐ ( ‖ S [ d ] ∖ J ι ​ ( T − I ) ​ A ι ​ U s ‖ F + α ι ​ ‖ U s ‖ F + ε ​ N ​ ( ‖ S [ d ] ∖ J ι ​ A ι ‖ 2 + α ι ) ) 2 . \mathcal{E}_{U,\mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{full}}}^{\mathrm{joint}}(\operatorname{T})\leq\frac{1}{|\mathcal{I}|}\sum_{\iota\in\mathcal{I}}\Bigl(\|S_{[d]\setminus J_{\iota}}(\operatorname{T}-I)A_{\iota}U^{s}\|_{F}+\alpha_{\iota}\|U^{s}\|_{F}+\varepsilon\sqrt{N}\,\bigl(\|S_{[d]\setminus J_{\iota}}A_{\iota}\|_{2}+\alpha_{\iota}\bigr)\Bigr)^{2}. (119)
Moreover, these imply the simpler selector-free full-joint bounds
ℰ ρ , 𝒬 full joint ( T ) ≤ 1 | ℐ | ∑ ι ∈ ℐ [ \displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\rho,\mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{full}}}^{\mathrm{joint}}(\operatorname{T})\leq\frac{1}{|\mathcal{I}|}\sum_{\iota\in\mathcal{I}}\Big[ 4 ​ ‖ ( T − I ) ​ A ι ​ μ s ‖ 2 2 + 4 ​ ‖ Σ s ‖ 2 ​ ‖ ( T − I ) ​ A ι ‖ F 2 \displaystyle 4\|(\operatorname{T}-I)A_{\iota}\mu_{s}\|_{2}^{2}+4\|\Sigma_{s}\|_{2}\,\|(\operatorname{T}-I)A_{\iota}\|_{F}^{2}
+ 4 α ι 2 ∥ μ s ∥ 2 2 + 4 d ∥ Σ s ∥ 2 α ι 2 + 2 ( ∥ A ι ∥ 2 + α ι ) 2 ε 2 ] , \displaystyle+4\alpha_{\iota}^{2}\|\mu_{s}\|_{2}^{2}+4d\,\|\Sigma_{s}\|_{2}\,\alpha_{\iota}^{2}+2(\|A_{\iota}\|_{2}+\alpha_{\iota})^{2}\varepsilon^{2}\Big], (120)
and
ℰ U , 𝒬 full joint ​ ( T ) ≤ 1 | ℐ | ​ ∑ ι ∈ ℐ ( ‖ ( T − I ) ​ A ι ​ U s ‖ F + α ι ​ ‖ U s ‖ F + ε ​ N ​ ( ‖ A ι ‖ 2 + α ι ) ) 2 . \mathcal{E}_{U,\mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{full}}}^{\mathrm{joint}}(\operatorname{T})\leq\frac{1}{|\mathcal{I}|}\sum_{\iota\in\mathcal{I}}\Bigl(\|(\operatorname{T}-I)A_{\iota}U^{s}\|_{F}+\alpha_{\iota}\|U^{s}\|_{F}+\varepsilon\sqrt{N}\,(\|A_{\iota}\|_{2}+\alpha_{\iota})\Bigr)^{2}. (121)
Proof.
Set O ι = [ d ] ∖ J ι , ∀ ι ∈ ℐ O_{\iota}=[d]\setminus J_{\iota},\forall\,\iota\in\mathcal{I} . Then 𝒬 = 𝒬 full \mathcal{Q}=\mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{full}} , so the averages in Theorems 24 and 25 run over the full post-interventional output family. Substituting this choice of O ι O_{\iota} into the query-restricted Gaussian and empirical certificates gives ( 118 ) and ( 119 ). The selector-free bounds ( 120 ) and ( 121 ) then follow from the monotonicity of coordinate restriction:
‖ S [ d ] ∖ J ι ​ M ‖ 2 ≤ ‖ M ‖ 2 , ‖ S [ d ] ∖ J ι ​ M ‖ F ≤ ‖ M ‖ F , d − | J ι | ≤ d , \displaystyle\|S_{[d]\setminus J_{\iota}}M\|_{2}\leq\|M\|_{2},\qquad\|S_{[d]\setminus J_{\iota}}M\|_{F}\leq\|M\|_{F},\qquad d-|J_{\iota}|\leq d, (122)
applied to the corresponding terms in ( 118 ) and ( 119 ). ∎
Corollary 27 (Single-query certificates) .
Let 𝒬 single := { ( ι 0 , O 0 ) } \mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{single}}:=\{(\iota_{0},O_{0})\} for some ι 0 ∈ ℐ \iota_{0}\in\mathcal{I} and O 0 ⊆ [ d ] ∖ J ι 0 O_{0}\subseteq[d]\setminus J_{\iota_{0}} . Then for every T \operatorname{T} ,
ℰ ρ , 𝒬 single joint ​ ( T ) ≤ δ ι 0 , O 0 ρ ​ ( T ) 2 , \mathcal{E}_{\rho,\mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{single}}}^{\mathrm{joint}}(\operatorname{T})\leq\delta_{\iota_{0},O_{0}}^{\rho}(\operatorname{T})^{2}, (123)
and
ℰ U , 𝒬 single joint ​ ( T ) ≤ δ ι 0 , O 0 U ​ ( T ) 2 . \mathcal{E}_{U,\mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{single}}}^{\mathrm{joint}}(\operatorname{T})\leq\delta_{\iota_{0},O_{0}}^{U}(\operatorname{T})^{2}. (124)
In particular, any minimiser of the corresponding 𝒬 single \mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{single}} -restricted objective satisfies both bounds at that minimiser.
Proof.
Immediate from Theorems 24 and 25 by taking | 𝒬 | = 1 |\mathcal{Q}|=1 . ∎
Remark 28 .
Let
O ι post := [ d ] ∖ J ι , δ ι , post ρ ​ ( T ) := δ ι , O ι post ρ ​ ( T ) , δ ι , post U ​ ( T ) := δ ι , O ι post U ​ ( T ) . \displaystyle O_{\iota}^{\mathrm{post}}:=[d]\setminus J_{\iota},\qquad\delta_{\iota,\mathrm{post}}^{\rho}(\operatorname{T}):=\delta_{\iota,O_{\iota}^{\mathrm{post}}}^{\rho}(\operatorname{T}),\qquad\delta_{\iota,\mathrm{post}}^{U}(\operatorname{T}):=\delta_{\iota,O_{\iota}^{\mathrm{post}}}^{U}(\operatorname{T}). (125)
The query-restricted certificates have two immediate consequences. First, for every included query ( ι , O ι ) ∈ 𝒬 (\iota,O_{\iota})\in\mathcal{Q} and every T \operatorname{T}
δ ι , O ι ρ ​ ( T ) 2 ≤ δ ι , post ρ ​ ( T ) 2 , δ ι , O ι U ​ ( T ) ≤ δ ι , post U ​ ( T ) . \delta_{\iota,O_{\iota}}^{\rho}(\operatorname{T})^{2}\leq\delta_{\iota,\mathrm{post}}^{\rho}(\operatorname{T})^{2},\qquad\delta_{\iota,O_{\iota}}^{U}(\operatorname{T})\leq\delta_{\iota,\mathrm{post}}^{U}(\operatorname{T}). (126)
Thus, on any query explicitly included in 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} , the restricted certificate is never worse than the corresponding full post-interventional one, though the gap may be small when the mechanism and environment terms dominate the bound. The selector-free bounds ( 120 ) and ( 121 ) are looser still, but they are often convenient when no query family is specified. Second, if a pair ( ι , O ) (\iota,O) does not belong to 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} , then the restricted objective contains no term associated with that query. Consequently, no certificate of the form ( 115 ) or ( 117 ) is induced for that excluded query by the restricted objective alone. In this sense, query-restricted training sharpens robustness guarantees on the queries that are explicitly targeted, but does not provide analogous guarantees for outputs left out of 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} .
9.1 Specialisation to query-level transport
The certificates above control the worst-case discrepancy between the transported source and the target at the level of the post-interventional marginals selected by 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} . We now specialise these model-level guarantees to downstream causal queries. The key observation is that once a query functional is Lipschitz with respect to the discrepancy used in the certificate, the abstraction-error bound transfers directly to the query value itself. In the Gaussian case, the relevant discrepancy is 𝒲 2 \mathcal{W}_{2} , while in the empirical case, it is the Frobenius norm. This yields certified target-query intervals centered at the transported-source query value for each designated query pair ( ι , O ι ) ∈ 𝒬 (\iota,O_{\iota})\in\mathcal{Q} .
Theorem 29 .
Let ( ι , O ι ) ∈ 𝒬 (\iota,O_{\iota})\in\mathcal{Q} and let Q t ( ι , O ι ) := Φ ( ι , O ι ) ​ ( π O ι ​ # ​ P t ( ι ) ) Q_{t}^{(\iota,O_{\iota})}:=\Phi_{(\iota,O_{\iota})}\!\left(\pi_{O_{\iota}\#}P_{t}^{(\iota)}\right) be a causal query. Then,
(i) Gaussian case. Suppose Φ ( ι , O ι ) \Phi_{(\iota,O_{\iota})} is L ( ι , O ι ) L_{(\iota,O_{\iota})} -Lipschitz with respect to 𝒲 2 \mathcal{W}_{2} on probability measures over ℝ | O ι | \mathbb{R}^{|O_{\iota}|} . Then for every admissible ( Δ ​ W , μ t , Σ t ) (\Delta W,\mu_{t},\Sigma_{t}) ,
| Φ ( ι , O ι ) ​ ( π O ι ​ # ​ P t ( ι ) ​ ( Δ ​ W , μ t , Σ t ) ) − Φ ( ι , O ι ) ​ ( π O ι ​ # ​ ( T # ⁡ P s ( ι ) ) ) | ≤ L ( ι , O ι ) ​ δ ι , O ι ρ ​ ( T ) . \displaystyle\Bigl|\Phi_{(\iota,O_{\iota})}\!\left(\pi_{O_{\iota}\#}P_{t}^{(\iota)}(\Delta W,\mu_{t},\Sigma_{t})\right)-\Phi_{(\iota,O_{\iota})}\!\left(\pi_{O_{\iota}\#}(\operatorname{T}_{\#}P_{s}^{(\iota)})\right)\Bigr|\leq L_{(\iota,O_{\iota})}\,\delta_{\iota,O_{\iota}}^{\rho}(\operatorname{T}). (127)
(ii) Empirical case. Let Φ ^ ( ι , O ι ) \widehat{\Phi}_{(\iota,O_{\iota})} be a functional on | O ι | × N |O_{\iota}|\times N data matrices that is L ^ ( ι , O ι ) \widehat{L}_{(\iota,O_{\iota})} -Lipschitz with respect to the Frobenius norm. Then for every admissible ( Δ ​ W , Θ ) (\Delta W,\Theta) ,
| Φ ^ ( ι , O ι ) ​ ( S O ι ​ A ι ′ ​ ( Δ ​ W ) ​ ( U s + Θ ) ) − Φ ^ ( ι , O ι ) ​ ( S O ι ​ T ⁡ A ι ​ U s ) | ≤ L ^ ( ι , O ι ) ​ δ ι , O ι U ​ ( T ) . \displaystyle\Bigl|\widehat{\Phi}_{(\iota,O_{\iota})}\!\left(S_{O_{\iota}}A^{\prime}_{\iota}(\Delta W)(U^{s}+\Theta)\right)-\widehat{\Phi}_{(\iota,O_{\iota})}\!\left(S_{O_{\iota}}\operatorname{T}A_{\iota}U^{s}\right)\Bigr|\leq\widehat{L}_{(\iota,O_{\iota})}\,\delta_{\iota,O_{\iota}}^{U}(\operatorname{T}). (128)
Proof.
For part (i), the Lipschitz property of Φ ( ι , O ι ) \Phi_{(\iota,O_{\iota})} implies that for any two probability measures ν 1 , ν 2 \nu_{1},\nu_{2} on ℝ | O ι | \mathbb{R}^{|O_{\iota}|} ,
| Φ ( ι , O ι ) ​ ( ν 1 ) − Φ ( ι , O ι ) ​ ( ν 2 ) | ≤ L ( ι , O ι ) ​ 𝒲 2 ​ ( ν 1 , ν 2 ) . \displaystyle\bigl|\Phi_{(\iota,O_{\iota})}(\nu_{1})-\Phi_{(\iota,O_{\iota})}(\nu_{2})\bigr|\leq L_{(\iota,O_{\iota})}\,\mathcal{W}_{2}(\nu_{1},\nu_{2}). (129)
Apply this with
ν 1 = π O ι ​ # ​ P t ( ι ) ​ ( Δ ​ W , μ t , Σ t ) , ν 2 = π O ι ​ # ​ ( T # ⁡ P s ( ι ) ) . \displaystyle\nu_{1}=\pi_{O_{\iota}\#}P_{t}^{(\iota)}(\Delta W,\mu_{t},\Sigma_{t}),\qquad\nu_{2}=\pi_{O_{\iota}\#}(\operatorname{T}_{\#}P_{s}^{(\iota)}). (130)
By the pointwise bound established in the proof of Theorem 24 ,
𝒲 2 ​ ( π O ι ​ # ​ P t ( ι ) ​ ( Δ ​ W , μ t , Σ t ) , π O ι ​ # ​ ( T # ⁡ P s ( ι ) ) ) ≤ δ ι , O ι ρ ​ ( T ) , \displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{2}\!\Bigl(\pi_{O_{\iota}\#}P_{t}^{(\iota)}(\Delta W,\mu_{t},\Sigma_{t}),\;\pi_{O_{\iota}\#}(\operatorname{T}_{\#}P_{s}^{(\iota)})\Bigr)\leq\delta_{\iota,O_{\iota}}^{\rho}(\operatorname{T}), (131)
which gives ( 127 ). For part (ii), use the Lipschitz property of Φ ^ ( ι , O ι ) \widehat{\Phi}_{(\iota,O_{\iota})} with respect to the Frobenius norm:
| Φ ^ ( ι , O ι ) ​ ( Y 1 ) − Φ ^ ( ι , O ι ) ​ ( Y 2 ) | ≤ L ^ ( ι , O ι ) ​ ‖ Y 1 − Y 2 ‖ F \displaystyle\bigl|\widehat{\Phi}_{(\iota,O_{\iota})}(Y_{1})-\widehat{\Phi}_{(\iota,O_{\iota})}(Y_{2})\bigr|\leq\widehat{L}_{(\iota,O_{\iota})}\,\|Y_{1}-Y_{2}\|_{F} (132)
for any two | O ι | × N |O_{\iota}|\times N matrices Y 1 , Y 2 Y_{1},Y_{2} . Apply this with
Y 1 = S O ι ​ A ι ′ ​ ( Δ ​ W ) ​ ( U s + Θ ) , Y 2 = S O ι ​ T ⁡ A ι ​ U s . \displaystyle Y_{1}=S_{O_{\iota}}A^{\prime}_{\iota}(\Delta W)(U^{s}+\Theta),\qquad Y_{2}=S_{O_{\iota}}\operatorname{T}A_{\iota}U^{s}. (133)
The pointwise estimate underlying Theorem 25 gives
‖ S O ι ​ ( T ⁡ A ι ​ U s − A ι ′ ​ ( Δ ​ W ) ​ ( U s + Θ ) ) ‖ F ≤ δ ι , O ι U ​ ( T ) , \displaystyle\bigl\|S_{O_{\iota}}\bigl(\operatorname{T}A_{\iota}U^{s}-A^{\prime}_{\iota}(\Delta W)(U^{s}+\Theta)\bigr)\bigr\|_{F}\leq\delta_{\iota,O_{\iota}}^{U}(\operatorname{T}), (134)
and substituting this bound yields ( 128 ). ∎
Equivalently, writing ℓ := Q τ ​ # ​ s ( ι , O ι ) − L ( ι , O ι ) ​ δ ι , O ι ρ ​ ( T ) \ell:=Q_{\tau\#s}^{(\iota,O_{\iota})}-L_{(\iota,O_{\iota})}\delta_{\iota,O_{\iota}}^{\rho}(\operatorname{T}) and L := Q τ ​ # ​ s ( ι , O ι ) + L ( ι , O ι ) ​ δ ι , O ι ρ ​ ( T ) L:=Q_{\tau\#s}^{(\iota,O_{\iota})}+L_{(\iota,O_{\iota})}\delta_{\iota,O_{\iota}}^{\rho}(\operatorname{T}) , every admissible target satisfies ℓ ≤ Q t ( ι , O ι ) ≤ L \ell\leq Q_{t}^{(\iota,O_{\iota})}\leq L : the certified interval, centered at the transported-source value. These certificates above are general: they bound arbitrary Lipschitz query functionals by first controlling a discrepancy between full query-relevant marginals. This generality is useful, but conservative for linear and mean queries. The modulus α ι \alpha_{\iota} controls the worst admissible mechanism perturbation in every input and output direction, whereas a linear query reads out only one direction of the selected marginal. In particular, for
Φ ( ι , O ι ) ​ ( ν ) = 𝔼 X ∼ ν ​ [ c ⊤ ​ X ] , c ∈ ℝ | O ι | , \displaystyle\Phi_{(\iota,O_{\iota})}(\nu)=\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\nu}[c^{\top}X],\qquad c\in\mathbb{R}^{|O_{\iota}|}, (135)
define the lifted readout
q := S O ι ⊤ ​ c ∈ ℝ d . \displaystyle q:=S_{O_{\iota}}^{\top}c\in\mathbb{R}^{d}. (136)
Then the query depends on the full post-interventional vector only through the scalar q ⊤ ​ X q^{\top}X . Concretely, q q is a vector of weights recording which coordinates the query reads and with what coefficients: for the single-outcome queries of our benchmarks, it is the indicator of the outcome node, so that q ⊤ ​ X q^{\top}X is that coordinate and nothing else. This motivates replacing the global mechanism modulus with a directional one.
Definition 18 (Directional mechanism modulus) .
For an intervention ι ∈ ℐ \iota\in\mathcal{I} , a readout direction q ∈ ℝ d q\in\mathbb{R}^{d} , and a vector v ∈ ℝ d v\in\mathbb{R}^{d} , define
m ι , q ​ ( v ) := sup Δ ​ W ∈ 𝒜 W | q ⊤ ​ ( A ι ′ ​ ( Δ ​ W ) − A ι ) ​ v | . \displaystyle m_{\iota,q}(v):=\sup_{\Delta W\in\mathcal{A}_{W}}\left|q^{\top}\bigl(A_{\iota}^{\prime}(\Delta W)-A_{\iota}\bigr)v\right|. (137)
The contrast with α ι \alpha_{\iota} is that the latter is a spectral norm, hence a supremum over the input and output directions as well as over Δ ​ W \Delta W , whereas m ι , q ​ ( v ) m_{\iota,q}(v) fixes the input to v v and the output to q q , leaving only the mechanism adversarial.
Corollary 30 (Directional certificate for linear queries) .
Let ( ι , O ι ) ∈ 𝒬 (\iota,O_{\iota})\in\mathcal{Q} , and let
Φ ( ι , O ι ) ​ ( ν ) = 𝔼 X ∼ ν ​ [ c ⊤ ​ X ] \displaystyle\Phi_{(\iota,O_{\iota})}(\nu)=\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\nu}[c^{\top}X] (138)
be a linear query on ℝ | O ι | \mathbb{R}^{|O_{\iota}|} . Set q := S O ι ⊤ ​ c q:=S_{O_{\iota}}^{\top}c , and define the transported-source query value
Q τ ​ # ​ s ( ι , O ι ) := Φ ( ι , O ι ) ​ ( π O ι ​ # ​ T # ​ P s ( ι ) ) . \displaystyle Q_{\tau\#s}^{(\iota,O_{\iota})}:=\Phi_{(\iota,O_{\iota})}\left(\pi_{O_{\iota}\#}\operatorname{T}_{\#}P_{s}^{(\iota)}\right). (139)
Then every admissible target satisfies
| Q t ( ι , O ι ) − Q τ ​ # ​ s ( ι , O ι ) | ≤ | q ⊤ ​ ( T − I ) ​ A ι ​ v | ⏟ transport mismatch + m ι , q ​ ( v ) ⏟ mechanism shift + ε ​ sup Δ ​ W ∈ 𝒜 W ‖ M 𝒦 ​ A ι ′ ​ ( Δ ​ W ) ⊤ ​ q ‖ 2 ⏟ environment shift , \displaystyle\left|Q_{t}^{(\iota,O_{\iota})}-Q_{\tau\#s}^{(\iota,O_{\iota})}\right|\leq\;\underbrace{\left|q^{\top}(\operatorname{T}-I)A_{\iota}v\right|}_{\textnormal{transport mismatch}}+\underbrace{m_{\iota,q}(v)}_{\textnormal{mechanism shift}}+\underbrace{\varepsilon\sup_{\Delta W\in\mathcal{A}_{W}}\left\|M_{\mathcal{K}}A_{\iota}^{\prime}(\Delta W)^{\top}q\right\|_{2}}_{\textnormal{environment shift}}, (140)
where v = μ s ( ι ) v=\mu_{s}^{(\iota)} in the Gaussian case and v = U ¯ s , ( ι ) := 1 N ​ U s , ( ι ) ​ 𝟏 v=\bar{U}^{s,(\iota)}:=\frac{1}{N}U^{s,(\iota)}\mathbf{1} in the empirical case: the effective exogenous mean under ι \iota of Section 8.2 , which carries the intervention value at the clamped coordinates.
