The first-order part of m ι , q ​ ( v ) m_{\iota,q}(v) admits explicit support-function expressions for each mechanism ambiguity geometry, and the full directional modulus is controlled by this first-order part plus a finite higher-order remainder; see Appendix E.1 .
Proof.
This is the directional specialisation of Theorem 29 . Since the query is a rank-one readout q ⊤ ​ X q^{\top}X , the residual decomposition can be projected onto q q before taking norms. The mechanism term is then controlled by Definition 18 , while the environment term follows from Cauchy-Schwarz and the fact that admissible environment perturbations are supported only on the shifted coordinates. The full proof and the support-function expressions for m ι , q m_{\iota,q} are given in Appendix E.1 . ∎
We refer to the bounds of Theorems 24 and 25 as the general certificate since they hold for any Lipschitz functional and any admissible target, and to Corollary 30 as the directional certificate; these are the two intervals reported in Section 10 .
Remark 31 (Why the directional certificate is tighter) .
The general certificates use the global perturbation modulus α ι \alpha_{\iota} , which controls the entire operator ( A ι ′ ​ ( Δ ​ W ) − A ι ) (A_{\iota}^{\prime}(\Delta W)-A_{\iota}) . The directional certificate removes two sources of conservatism. First, the mechanism term controls only the scalar perturbation seen by the query direction q q , and in the empirical case, the query reads out the sample mean U ¯ s \bar{U}^{s} rather than the full sample cloud U s U^{s} ; for centered data, this alone is a substantial reduction. Second, the environment term uses the masked operator
‖ M 𝒦 ​ A ι ′ ​ ( Δ ​ W ) ⊤ ​ q ‖ 2 \displaystyle\left\|M_{\mathcal{K}}A_{\iota}^{\prime}(\Delta W)^{\top}q\right\|_{2} (141)
in place of the unmasked full-output quantity ( ‖ S O ι ​ A ι ‖ 2 + α ι ) (\|S_{O_{\iota}}A_{\iota}\|_{2}+\alpha_{\iota}) , because admissible environment shifts are pinned outside the shifted coordinates. Thus, the broad certificates protect all Lipschitz functionals of the selected marginal, while the directional certificate exploits the exact rank-one readout used by mean queries. In the extreme case, the mechanism term vanishes outright: if the signal reaching a shifted coefficient is zero under a given intervention, no admissible perturbation of it can move the query, and m ι , q ​ ( v ) = 0 m_{\iota,q}(v)=0 while α ι \alpha_{\iota} remains at its full value. This is the mechanism behind the coincident directional widths on 𝔼 ⁡ [ Y ∣ d ​ o ​ ( X = 0 ) ] \mathbb{E}[Y\mid do(X{=}0)] in Figure 9 .
Theorem 29 turns the preceding model-level certificates into query-specific bounds for arbitrary Lipschitz functionals, while Corollary 30 sharpens this transfer for the linear and mean queries that dominate our benchmarks. More broadly, for a fixed transport map T \operatorname{T} , the ambiguity sets 𝒜 W \mathcal{A}_{W} , 𝒜 ρ ​ ( ε ) \mathcal{A}_{\rho}(\varepsilon) , and 𝒜 U ​ ( ε ) \mathcal{A}_{U}(\varepsilon) determine the admissible target marginals associated with the queries in 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} ; in the Gaussian case, these are exactly
{ ( π O ι ​ # P t ( ι ) ( Δ W , μ t , Σ t ) ) ( ι , O ι ) ∈ 𝒬 : Δ W ∈ 𝒜 W , ( μ t , Σ t ) ∈ 𝒜 ρ ( ε ) } , \displaystyle\left\{\left(\pi_{O_{\iota}\#}P_{t}^{(\iota)}(\Delta W,\mu_{t},\Sigma_{t})\right)_{(\iota,O_{\iota})\in\mathcal{Q}}:\Delta W\in\mathcal{A}_{W},(\mu_{t},\Sigma_{t})\in\mathcal{A}_{\rho}(\varepsilon)\right\}, (142)
and analogously in the empirical case with S O ι ​ A ι ′ ​ ( Δ ​ W ) ​ ( U s + Θ ) S_{O_{\iota}}A_{\iota}^{\prime}(\Delta W)(U^{s}+\Theta) and Θ ∈ 𝒜 U ​ ( ε ) \Theta\in\mathcal{A}_{U}(\varepsilon) . In this sense, the robust objectives ℰ ρ , 𝒬 joint ​ ( T ) \mathcal{E}_{\rho,\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{joint}}(\operatorname{T}) and ℰ U , 𝒬 joint ​ ( T ) \mathcal{E}_{U,\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{joint}}(\operatorname{T}) measure worst-case abstraction discrepancy over these admissible target families, while the Gaussian and empirical certificates provide the corresponding model-level bounds. The Lipschitz theorem converts those model-level bounds into certified intervals for arbitrary designated queries, and the directional corollary provides a sharper specialisation when the query is a linear readout. This is close in spirit to the target-class viewpoint in partial-transportability analyses ( Jalaldoust et al., 2024 ) , but here the relevant family is induced by worst-case robust ambiguity sets rather than by the set of target domains compatible with the source distributions and graphical assumptions. Once a single transport map T \operatorname{T} is learned, the logic of the framework is:
Δ ​ W ⟶ γ ι ⟶ α ι ⟶ δ ι , O ι ​ ( T ) ⟶ certified interval for ​ Q t ( ι , O ι ) . \displaystyle\Delta W\longrightarrow\gamma_{\iota}\longrightarrow\alpha_{\iota}\longrightarrow\delta_{\iota,O_{\iota}}(\operatorname{T})\longrightarrow\text{certified interval for }Q_{t}^{(\iota,O_{\iota})}. (143)
10 Experiments
We evaluate TraCA on three synthetic benchmark families and one real-world benchmark. The synthetic families isolate the two regimes that the framework targets: a query that classical transportability cannot handle ( ℛ 1 \mathcal{R}_{1} ), and a target-agnostic setting in which no target data are available at all ( ℛ 2 \mathcal{R}_{2} ). The real-world benchmark fixes a single observed target domain with invariant mechanisms and a shifted covariate distribution, testing the certificate on data we did not generate. Details on the full structural equations, parameters, intervention sets, graphs, and the remaining configurations are deferred to Appendix G . Across the four benchmarks, we ask three questions.
Q1. Does the certificate hold, and is it informative? This is the claim the theory makes: the certified interval [ ℓ , L ] [\ell,L] contains the true query value for every admissible target. A guarantee of this form is trivially satisfiable by a sufficiently wide interval, so coverage must be read jointly with width. We call a certificate informative when its width is below 10 × 10\times the mean absolute query value (the query scale, QS), and vacuous otherwise, and we compare widths only where they are informative. The factor of ten is a deliberately loose order-of-magnitude convention rather than a tuned threshold: an interval ten times wider than the typical query value carries almost no information about that query, whatever the units. Normalising by the query scale makes the criterion scale-free, so it transfers across benchmarks whose queries differ by orders of magnitude. The certificates themselves are, of course, scale-dependent, since they bound | Q t − Q τ ​ # ​ s | |Q_{t}-Q_{\tau\#s}| , which carries the units of the query; this is precisely why widths are reported and compared in QS units rather than in absolute terms.
Q2. Does a directional prior improve the learned map? A belief about the direction of the target shift is encoded by recentering the mechanism box at an offset δ \delta (Section 8.1 ).
Q3. Can the operating radius be calibrated without interventional target data? Where the target can be observed but not intervened upon, the radius must be chosen from observational quantities alone.
Throughout, we call a run symmetric when δ = 0 \delta=0 and offset when δ ≠ 0 \delta\neq 0 , and reserve general and directional for the two certificates of Section 9 ; both certificates are computed under both variants. The codebase is available at https://github.com/yfelekis/traca .
10.1 Benchmarks
Figure 7 shows the causal graph of each benchmark and its shifted nodes.
X X Y Y (a) ATE
Z Z X X Y Y (b) ATCE
𝑆𝑚 \mathit{Sm} 𝐺𝑒 \mathit{Ge} 𝐴𝑙 \mathit{Al} 𝐿𝐶 \mathit{LC} 𝐶𝑜 \mathit{Co} 𝐹𝑎 \mathit{Fa} (c) LiLUCAS
Z Z S S X X Y Y (d) Portland
Figure 7 : Causal graphs of the four benchmarks. Shaded nodes are shifted across domains; the dashed arc in (a) is latent confounding.
ATE ( ℛ 1 \mathcal{R}_{1} ).
ATE is a two-variable semi-Markovian graph X → Y X\to Y with latent confounding X ↔ Y X\leftrightarrow Y and a domain shift on the mechanism of Y Y . Because the confounder is hidden, there is no valid adjustment set, so P ⁡ ( Y ∣ d ​ o ​ ( X ) ) P(Y\mid do(X)) is not transportable by the classical do-calculus transport formula (Figure 6b from Pearl and Bareinboim (2014) ). It is therefore our ℛ 1 \mathcal{R}_{1} benchmark: the certified interval is informative precisely where classical transportability is silent. We report the certified interval for the two queries 𝔼 t ​ [ Y ∣ d ​ o ​ ( X = 0 ) ] \mathbb{E}_{t}[Y\mid do(X{=}0)] and 𝔼 t ​ [ Y ∣ d ​ o ​ ( X = 1 ) ] \mathbb{E}_{t}[Y\mid do(X{=}1)] .
ATCE ( ℛ 2 \mathcal{R}_{2} ).
ATCE is a three-variable graph Z → X → Y Z\to X\to Y with an additional Z → Y Z\to Y edge and is one of our ℛ 2 \mathcal{R}_{2} benchmarks: it is target-agnostic, and no target data enters at any stage. As in ATE, we evaluate the query family { 𝔼 t ​ [ Y ∣ d ​ o ​ ( X = 0 ) ] , 𝔼 t ​ [ Y ∣ d ​ o ​ ( X = 1 ) ] } \{\mathbb{E}_{t}[Y\mid do(X{=}0)],\,\mathbb{E}_{t}[Y\mid do(X{=}1)]\} , whose queries remain sensitive to mechanism perturbation.
LiLUCAS ( ℛ 2 \mathcal{R}_{2} ).
LiLUCAS is a six-node Markovian DAG based on the original LUCAS dataset, 1 1 1 www.causality.inf.ethz.ch/data/LUCAS.html modelling lung cancer with roots Smoking ( Sm ), Genetics ( Ge ), and Allergy ( Al ), and downstream LungCancer ( LC ), Coughing ( Co ), and Fatigue ( Fa ); the shift is placed on the LC mechanism. As with ATCE, this benchmark is target-agnostic. We evaluate the query family { 𝔼 t ​ [ 𝐿𝐶 ∣ d ​ o ​ ( 𝑆𝑚 = v ) ] , 𝔼 t ​ [ 𝐿𝐶 ∣ d ​ o ​ ( 𝐺𝑒 = v ) ] } \{\mathbb{E}_{t}[\mathit{LC}\mid do(\mathit{Sm}{=}v)],\ \mathbb{E}_{t}[\mathit{LC}\mid do(\mathit{Ge}{=}v)]\} for v ∈ { 0 , 1 } v\in\{0,1\} .
Portland (real-world).
The Portland benchmark ( Tabell et al., 2026 ) has been constructed to ask whether riparian tree-canopy cover causally affects dissolved oxygen in urban streams and whether that effect transports from the observed source watersheds to a held-out target, Fanno Creek, whose environmental conditions differ from the source. We use the dataset and causal graph of the original study directly. Tabell et al. (2026) model the system as a linear-Gaussian SCM over four variables: canopy cover X X , dissolved oxygen Y Y , a one-dimensional environmental summary Z Z (Appendix G.4 ), and season S S , with graph Z → X Z\to X , Z → Y Z\to Y , S → Y S\to Y , X → Y X\to Y and structural coefficients w Z ​ X , w Z ​ Y , w S ​ Y , w X ​ Y w_{ZX},w_{ZY},w_{SY},w_{XY} , and treat the structural mechanisms as invariant across watersheds while the environmental context shifts. Each structural equation is fit by ordinary least squares, without intercept, on the source observational data, after standardising all variables to zero mean and unit variance using source statistics; the exogenous-noise variances are set to the unbiased residual variance of the corresponding regression. Fanno Creek is the target environment for which we have N t = 100 N_{t}=100 observational samples, and the remaining watersheds form the source ( N s = 247 N_{s}=247 ).
We share the modelling assumptions of the original study, the linear-Gaussian form, and the same four-variable graph but differ in how the interventional query is obtained. Tabell et al. (2026) estimate 𝔼 t ​ [ Y ∣ d ​ o ​ ( X = x ) ] \mathbb{E}_{t}[Y\mid do(X{=}x)] by back-door adjustment, regressing Y Y on X , Z , S X,Z,S and averaging over the target covariate distribution; because TraCA transports a structural model rather than a single estimate, we instead realise each d ​ o ​ ( X = x ) do(X{=}x) on the fitted SCM, fixing X X and propagating the exogenous noise through the resulting interventional propagator. For this graph, the two coincide exactly since both give 𝔼 t ​ [ Y ∣ d ​ o ​ ( X = x ) ] = w Z ​ Y ​ 𝔼 t ​ [ Z ] + w S ​ Y ​ 𝔼 t ​ [ S ] + w X ​ Y ​ x \mathbb{E}_{t}[Y\mid do(X{=}x)]=w_{ZY}\,\mathbb{E}_{t}[Z]+w_{SY}\,\mathbb{E}_{t}[S]+w_{XY}\,x under the linear-Gaussian model, as the only back-door path X ← Z → Y X\leftarrow Z\to Y is blocked by Z Z and S S opens none; the choice of representation therefore does not change the query, only the object that the transport map and certificate act on. We evaluate interventions x ∈ { 45 , 50 , 55 , 60 } % x\in\{45,50,55,60\}\% canopy cover, the values considered by the original study.
10.2 Setup and evaluation protocol
Models and objective.
The three synthetic benchmarks are generated from linear additive-noise SCMs with Gaussian exogenous noise; Portland is fitted to real data as described above and is likewise a linear-Gaussian SCM. The optimisation objective differs by benchmark: ATE, ATCE, and Portland use the Gaussian objective, while LiLUCAS is presented under the empirical residual-based objective, with a Gaussian objective variant retained as a cross-check.
Shift geometry and budget.
Although the certificates of Section 9 hold for all mechanism geometries, the three synthetic benchmarks use entrywise (box) constraints: each shifted mechanism coefficient W j ​ k W_{jk} is given its own interval budget Δ ​ W j ​ k ∈ [ δ j ​ k − η , δ j ​ k + η ] \Delta W_{jk}\in[\delta_{jk}-\eta,\ \delta_{jk}+\eta] , so the mechanism radius η \eta is the per-coefficient perturbation half-width; symmetric about zero when δ = 0 \delta{=}0 and recentered at the declared offset δ \delta in the offset case. In the notation of Section 8.1 , this is 𝒜 W box ​ ( δ , B ) \mathcal{A}_{W}^{\mathrm{box}}(\delta,B) with b j ​ k = η b_{jk}=\eta on the budgeted coefficients and b j ​ k = 0 b_{jk}=0 elsewhere, so the applicable amplification bound is the entrywise one, Eq. ( 83 ), evaluated at the effective half-widths | δ | + η |\delta|+\eta . The entrywise form is more interpretable than a global norm bound and matches how domain shifts are typically specified in practice. The budgeted mechanism coefficients are the incoming edges of each benchmark’s shifted node: the X → Y X\to Y coefficient w X → Y w_{X\to Y} in ATE; the two incoming edges of Y Y , w Z → Y w_{Z\to Y} , and w X → Y w_{X\to Y} in ATCE; and the two incoming edges of LC , w 𝑆𝑚 → 𝐿𝐶 w_{\mathit{Sm}\to\mathit{LC}} and w 𝐺𝑒 → 𝐿𝐶 w_{\mathit{Ge}\to\mathit{LC}} , in LiLUCAS. In ATCE, the root Z Z additionally carries an environmental (exogenous) shift, and in Portland, the sole shifted node Z Z is a root with no incoming edges, so no mechanism coefficient moves ( η = 0 \eta{=}0 ), and the entire budget falls on the environment of Z Z : a Gelbrich ball 𝒜 ρ ​ ( ε ) \mathcal{A}_{\rho}(\varepsilon) of radius ε \varepsilon on Z Z ’s exogenous Gaussian law, centered at the source. Table 4 in Appendix G summarises these choices across all four benchmarks.
The training radius is swept over { 0 , 0.2 , 0.5 , 1.0 , 2.0 , 4.0 } \{0,\,0.2,\,0.5,\,1.0,\,2.0,\,4.0\} ; for ATCE and LiLUCAS, the mechanism and environment radii are tied ( ε = η \varepsilon=\eta ); for ATE, they vary independently over the full ( ε , η ) (\varepsilon,\eta) grid, and for Portland, only the environment radius ε \varepsilon , the Gelbrich-ball radius on Z Z , is swept ( η = 0 \eta=0 ), since the shift is purely environmental. Throughout, we write r train r_{\mathrm{train}} for the ambiguity radius at which a map is trained (equal to ε = η \varepsilon{=}\eta on the tied-radius benchmarks, and to ε \varepsilon alone on Portland), and r test r_{\mathrm{test}} for the magnitude of the target shift a map is evaluated against. The two are independent: a map trained against 𝒜 ⁡ ( r train ) \mathcal{A}(r_{\mathrm{train}}) can be evaluated against shifts drawn from 𝒜 ⁡ ( r test ) \mathcal{A}(r_{\mathrm{test}}) both inside and beyond the ball for which it was trained, the two sets coinciding only when r train = r test r_{\mathrm{train}}=r_{\mathrm{test}} .
Training protocol.
In all cases, the transport map T \operatorname{T} is a diagonal rescaling of the shifted nodes (block-diagonal in the semi-Markovian case), learned by minimising the worst-case transport loss over the ambiguity set. Throughout, we train and certify against the 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -restricted objective of Section 8.4 , with 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} being the query family listed for each benchmark above. We use 5 5 -fold cross-validation: for each fold and training radius, we learn T \operatorname{T} on the source split alone and store it; a separate evaluation pass loads the stored maps and scores them against targets. The identity map ( r train = 0 r_{\mathrm{train}}=0 ) acts as the internal baseline throughout. In no case does target interventional information enter fitting or radius selection; the target interventional query values are held out entirely and revealed only at scoring time, as the ground-truth values against which coverage of the certified interval is checked.
Benchmarks with a single observed target.
For ATE and Portland, a single real target is available: the synthetic ATE target and the Fanno Creek watershed, respectively. Only the target’s observational distribution enters the pipeline, and only to select the operating radius: we sweep r train r_{\mathrm{train}} and retain the one minimising the observational transport distance 𝒲 2 2 ​ ( τ # ​ P s obs , P t obs ) \mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}\!\bigl(\tau_{\#}P_{s}^{\mathrm{obs}},P_{t}^{\mathrm{obs}}\bigr) , a criterion that uses no interventional information. Section 10.3 examines when this selector succeeds and when it does not. The sweep here is over the training radius alone: we vary the ambiguity radius and, at each value, check whether the certified interval brackets the real target’s interventional query.
Benchmarks with no target data.
For the target-agnostic benchmarks ATCE and LiLUCAS, no target data exist at all. We instead draw K = 100 K=100 admissible targets from the ambiguity geometry itself, so each map is scored against synthetic shifts it was never shown, and we probe how the maps and certificates respond across shift sizes by sweeping r test r_{\mathrm{test}} . At each test magnitude, the K K admissible targets are drawn from the ambiguity geometry rescaled to that radius, so the sampled shifts are precisely those that the certificate claims to cover. In the symmetric variant, the mechanism perturbation Δ ​ W \Delta W is drawn from an entrywise box centered at zero; in the offset variant, the same box is recentered at a declared offset δ \delta , fixed at δ = 0.5 \delta=0.5 throughout our experiments. We also examine how the offset results degrade when the declared direction is wrong. Both the mechanism and the exogenous noise perturbation are applied to the shifted node and propagated structurally through the perturbed propagator, yielding a consistent target SCM. The mechanism perturbation is drawn uniformly from the box, while the exogenous perturbation is added to the same held-out source realisations, paired sample for sample, and normalised to lie exactly on the boundary of 𝒜 U ​ ( ε ) \mathcal{A}_{U}(\varepsilon) (or of the Gelbrich ball under the Gaussian objective). Every sampled target is therefore admissible by construction, and the environment component is drawn at the extremal magnitude that the certificate must cover. Sampling is seeded independently of the fold, so every trained variant is scored against the same K K targets at each magnitude, and differences between variants are attributable to the map rather than to the draw.
Scoring and aggregation.
For every evaluation instance, indexed by radius, target, fold, and variant, we record the transport error ℰ 𝒬 \mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{Q}} from Definition 17 ; the true query value; whether the certified interval, fixed at the variant’s own training radius, contains it; and the interval width. The discrepancy 𝖣 \mathsf{D} entering ℰ 𝒬 \mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{Q}} is the one used for training, 𝒲 2 2 \mathcal{W}_{2}^{2} under the Gaussian objective, and N − 1 ∥ ⋅ ∥ F 2 N^{-1}\|\cdot\|_{F}^{2} under the empirical one. All query-level quantities are computed on the benchmark’s query family alone, never pooled over coordinates that the map was not trained to transport, and coverage is reported per query rather than requiring every query in the family to be contained simultaneously. For the ℛ 2 \mathcal{R}_{2} benchmarks, coverage is aggregated in two steps: the per-fold fraction of the K K contained targets, followed by the mean across folds, while transport error is averaged over all sampled targets and folds jointly; for the single-target benchmarks (ATE, Portland), the corresponding quantities are averaged over folds alone. All comparisons in the transport-error tables use a two-sided paired t t -test across the K K perturbation seeds, with the seed as the pairing unit. The tabulated standard deviations are marginal, dominated at large r test r_{\mathrm{test}} by each seed’s target difficulty; variation shared across methods and cancelled by pairing: the paired standard deviation is typically 3 3 - 12 % 12\% of the marginal one. In Tables 2 and 3 , bold marks the column minimum together with every entry not significantly worse than it ( p > 0.05 p>0.05 ); in Table 1 , each radius is instead compared against the identity baseline, and bold marks the best entry that improves on it significantly ( p < 0.05 p<0.05 ).
10.3 Results & Analysis
10.3.1 Synthetics
ATE.
The observational selector finds a covering cell. The first question we take up is (Q3): whether a practitioner can select an operating radius without any interventional target data. Sweeping ( ε , η ) (\varepsilon,\eta) on the ATE benchmark, we select the cell minimising the observational transport distance 𝒲 2 2 ​ ( τ # ​ P s obs , P t obs ) \mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}\!\bigl(\tau_{\#}P_{s}^{\mathrm{obs}},P_{t}^{\mathrm{obs}}\bigr) , subject to ε , η > 0 \varepsilon,\eta>0 . Under the offset variant, the selector lands on ( ε = 0.2 , η = 0.2 ) (\varepsilon{=}0.2,\,\eta{=}0.2) at 𝒲 2 2 = 0.040 \mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}=0.040 (Figure 8 ). The offset prior recenters the box at δ = 0.5 \delta=0.5 , informing the optimiser that the shifted coefficient moves upward rather than in an unknown direction. The learned map T \operatorname{T} can therefore commit to a correction in that direction, and the transported source lands much closer to the observed target: the same cell under the symmetric variant reads 0.63 0.63 , an order of magnitude worse. This tightening is what makes the observational selector land on a covering cell rather than on the identity. The minimiser is not isolated: along ε = 0.2 \varepsilon{=}0.2 , the offset distance is 0.63 0.63 at η = 0 \eta{=}0 , where the mechanism adversary is inactive and the offset prior cannot yet act, and then 0.04 0.04 at all five values η ≥ 0.2 \eta\geq 0.2 , a five-way tie that we break to the smallest η \eta (Figure 8 ).
Figure 8 : ATE: observational transport distance (left column) and certificate widths (center: general, right: directional) over the ( ε , η ) (\varepsilon,\eta) grid, for the symmetric (top) and offset (bottom) variants. The observational distance uses no interventional information; the selected cell ( ε = 0.2 , η = 0.2 ) (\varepsilon{=}0.2,\,\eta{=}0.2) , boxed, sits at 𝒲 2 2 = 0.040 \mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}=0.040 under the offset variant.
The directional bound remains informative where the general becomes vacuous. Considering (Q1) , Figure 9 shows how the two certificate widths grow with r train = ε = η r_{\mathrm{train}}=\varepsilon{=}\eta along the diagonal. Under the symmetric variant, the general certificate becomes vacuous (width > 10 × QS = 10.5 >10\times\text{QS}=10.5 ) at r train ≥ 1.0 r_{\mathrm{train}}\geq 1.0 , while the directional certificate remains informative across the entire grid on 𝔼 ⁡ [ Y ∣ d ​ o ​ ( X = 0 ) ] \mathbb{E}[Y\mid do(X{=}0)] and crosses into vacuity only at r train = 2.0 r_{\mathrm{train}}{=}2.0 on 𝔼 ⁡ [ Y ∣ d ​ o ​ ( X = 1 ) ] \mathbb{E}[Y\mid do(X{=}1)] . Under the offset variant, the general widths are larger still ( 6.88 6.88 and 8.70 8.70 at r train = 0.2 r_{\mathrm{train}}{=}0.2 ), while the directional widths remain moderate ( 0.42 0.42 and 3.20 3.20 ). The widening is mostly because the offset raises the effective mechanism bound from η \eta to | δ | + η |\delta|+\eta , so γ ι \gamma_{\iota} and α ι \alpha_{\iota} inflate by a factor of 3.5 3.5 and enter the general certificate quadratically. Holding T \operatorname{T} at its symmetric optimum and changing only the amplification constants reproduces about three quarters of the increase.
Figure 9 : ATE: certificate width along the diagonal r train = ε = η r_{\mathrm{train}}=\varepsilon{=}\eta for both variants and both certificates (log scale). The horizontal line is the vacuity threshold ( 10 × QS = 10.5 10\times\text{QS}=10.5 ). Note that in the left panel the two directional curves, symmetric (solid, circles) and offset (dashed, squares), coincide exactly and are drawn on top of one another: the intervention sets X X to zero, so the shifted X → Y X\to Y coefficient contributes nothing to this query and the offset prior has nothing to act on; the width is then set by the environment term alone. This is the vanishing-mechanism case of Remark 31 .
Figure 10 : ATE: certified intervals at the observationally selected cell r train = 0.2 r_{\mathrm{train}}{=}0.2 , for both variants and both queries. Under the symmetric variant the directional certificate misses 𝔼 ⁡ [ Y ∣ d ​ o ​ ( X = 1 ) ] \mathbb{E}[Y\mid do(X{=}1)] by a gap of 0.08 0.08 (annotated); the offset variant covers both queries.
The offset prior is what makes small-radius selection work. On (Q2) , the coverage contrast between the two variants is sharp. Figure 10 shows the certified intervals at the observationally selected cell r train = 0.2 r_{\mathrm{train}}{=}0.2 . The offset variant covers both queries in all folds. The symmetric variant covers 𝔼 ⁡ [ Y ∣ d ​ o ​ ( X = 0 ) ] \mathbb{E}[Y\mid do(X{=}0)] but not 𝔼 ⁡ [ Y ∣ d ​ o ​ ( X = 1 ) ] \mathbb{E}[Y\mid do(X{=}1)] : the truth ( 1.80 1.80 ) lies 0.08 0.08 above the directional upper bound ( 1.72 1.72 ), missing coverage in all five folds. The zero-centered ambiguity set at this radius simply does not contain the realised shift on that query, and only at r train ≥ 0.5 r_{\mathrm{train}}\geq 0.5 does the directional certificate recover coverage under the symmetric variant, with a correspondingly wider interval. A symmetric prior therefore requires a larger ambiguity set to obtain the same guarantee, and it is the offset prior that makes observational radius selection viable at a small radius. We show how the intervals expand with r train r_{\mathrm{train}} in Figure 24 (Appendix J ): there, the general certificate grows rapidly, becoming vacuous by r train = 1.0 r_{\mathrm{train}}{=}1.0 , while the directional certificate grows more slowly and remains informative longer.
What happens when the offset prior is wrong? The offset results above declare δ = 0.5 \delta=0.5 , which, on this benchmark, is exactly the realised shift β t − β s \beta_{t}-\beta_{s} , so they represent the favourable case for a directional prior. Figure 11 holds the target fixed and varies the offset. The radius is held at r train = 0.2 r_{\mathrm{train}}=0.2 , the value the observational selector returns above, so that the effect of a wrong offset is isolated from re-selection. Two things hold: (a) The learned map improves on the identity exactly when the declared box contains the realised shift; that is, when | δ − 0.5 | ≤ η |\delta-0.5|\leq\eta , and (b) it is worse than not transporting outside that window; within it, the best offset lies below the truth because the map overshoots its declared center ( τ Y ​ X ≈ δ + 0.165 \tau_{YX}\approx\delta+0.165 throughout the sweep), though how much better depends on where the grid happens to fall. The certificate, however, does not become tighter as the offset becomes more incorrect: the mechanism budget is the effective half-width | δ | + η |\delta|+\eta , so a wrong offset of large magnitude widens the interval. Its width decomposes exactly, with transport and mechanism each contributing | δ | + η |\delta|+\eta to the half-width and the environment contributing η \eta ; the d ​ o ​ ( X = 0 ) do(X{=}0) width is flat because the intervention zeroes the shifted coefficient (Remark 31 ). Coverage, therefore, survives at δ = − 0.5 \delta=-0.5 and δ = 1.0 \delta=1.0 , whose boxes both miss the truth, and fails at the single point δ = 0 \delta=0 , the symmetric variant, whose 0.08 0.08 miss is the one already reported above. Thus, a small and incorrect offset is the failure mode; one that is large and incorrect is not.
Figure 11 : ATE: directional misspecification. The target is fixed and the offset prior δ \delta varies, at r train = ε = η = 0.2 r_{\mathrm{train}}=\varepsilon{=}\eta{=}0.2 . Shading marks the δ \delta for which the box [ δ − η , δ + η ] [\delta{-}\eta,\,\delta{+}\eta] contains the true shift β t − β s = 0.5 \beta_{t}-\beta_{s}=0.5 . Left: transport error relative to the identity (log scale); the minimum falls at δ = 0.3 \delta=0.3 , where the learned coefficient τ Y ​ X ≈ 0.465 \tau_{YX}\approx 0.465 is closest to the truth on the grid. Right: directional width, following 4 ​ ( | δ | + η ) + 2 ​ η 4(|\delta|{+}\eta)+2\eta (dashed) on d ​ o ​ ( X = 1 ) do(X{=}1) and constant at 0.415 0.415 on d ​ o ​ ( X = 0 ) do(X{=}0) . Coverage fails only at δ = 0 \delta=0 (red).
ATCE.
A DRO crossover: no single radius dominates. Figure 12 shows how transport error evolves as the test perturbation magnitude r test r_{\mathrm{test}} increases for maps trained at each r train r_{\mathrm{train}} . The identity baseline ( τ = I \tau{=}I , dashed) increases monotonically, confirming that any improvement comes from the learned map rather than from the evaluation geometry. A DRO crossover is visible. At small r test r_{\mathrm{test}} , the baseline wins: any robustification adds unnecessary distortion, and Table 1 confirms this at every training radius. At r test = 4.0 r_{\mathrm{test}}{=}4.0 , the picture reverses: maps trained at r train ∈ { 0.2 , 0.5 , 1.0 } r_{\mathrm{train}}\in\{0.2,0.5,1.0\} significantly reduce error relative to the baseline ( r train = 1.0 r_{\mathrm{train}}{=}1.0 gives 66.38 66.38 against the baseline’s 68.79 68.79 ), while the more aggressive radii { 2.0 , 4.0 } \{2.0,4.0\} show no significant improvement. Note that the best radius at r test = 4.0 r_{\mathrm{test}}{=}4.0 is not r train = 4.0 r_{\mathrm{train}}{=}4.0 but r train = 1.0 r_{\mathrm{train}}{=}1.0 : training hedges against the single worst perturbation in the ambiguity ball, whereas the sampled targets are spread across it, so a radius matched to the test magnitude buys protection in directions the typical target does not take, at the cost of accuracy in the ones it does. No single training radius, therefore, dominates everywhere: this is the classic DRO trade-off, and its lesson is that the operating radius should be sized to the anticipated shift, not to the worst imaginable one.
Figure 12 : ATCE: transport error against r test r_{\mathrm{test}} , one line per r train r_{\mathrm{train}} (log scale). The identity baseline (dashed) grows monotonically. The lines separate most clearly at moderate r test r_{\mathrm{test}} ; at r test = 4.0 r_{\mathrm{test}}{=}4.0 the errors are large and their differences small in relative terms, so the large-shift comparison is quantified in Table 1 .

For each perturbation seed k k , we average the error over 5 5 folds, yielding n = 100 n{=}100 paired observations; the pairing unit is k k , so the folds do not inflate the sample. Paired t t -tests compare each r train r_{\mathrm{train}} against the baseline ( τ = I \tau{=}I ). At small r test r_{\mathrm{test}} , the baseline wins significantly; i.e. no robustification is needed. At large r test r_{\mathrm{test}} , TraCA wins significantly.

Tighter and a more faithful diagnostic. Moving to (Q1) again, both queries remain sensitive to mechanism perturbation, which is what makes the certificate non-trivial here: the intervention removes the edge Z → X Z\to X , so Z Z no longer reaches Y Y through the treatment, but the direct path Z → Y Z\to Y is untouched, and Z Z remains random. A perturbation of the Z → Y Z\to Y coefficient therefore moves the query at both intervention values, and, by linearity, a perturbation of the X → Y X\to Y coefficient moves it whenever x ≠ 0 x\neq 0 . Figure 13 shows the certified intervals at three representative operating points, with r test = 0.5 r_{\mathrm{test}}{=}0.5 fixed and r train r_{\mathrm{train}} varied: below the test magnitude ( r train = 0.2 r_{\mathrm{train}}{=}0.2 , out-of-ball), matched ( r train = 0.5 r_{\mathrm{train}}{=}0.5 ), and above ( r train = 1.0 r_{\mathrm{train}}{=}1.0 , in-ball with margin). At the matched point, both certificates are informative and contain the sampled targets, with the directional interval 3.7 × 3.7\times tighter than the general one on 𝔼 ⁡ [ Y ∣ d ​ o ​ ( X = 0 ) ] \mathbb{E}[Y\mid do(X{=}0)] and 2.5 × 2.5\times on 𝔼 ⁡ [ Y ∣ d ​ o ​ ( X = 1 ) ] \mathbb{E}[Y\mid do(X{=}1)] ( 2.9 × 2.9\times family-averaged). Below the matched point, the directional certificate under-covers ( 0.61 0.61 and 0.89 0.89 across the two queries), while the general certificate still covers all targets; the tighter bound correctly reports that the target lies outside the ball for which it was built. Above the matched point, both provide coverage, and the directional certificate remains tighter throughout.
Figure 13 : ATCE: certified intervals at three operating points. Left: r train < r test r_{\mathrm{train}}<r_{\mathrm{test}} (out-of-ball). Center: matched. Right: r train > r test r_{\mathrm{train}}>r_{\mathrm{test}} (in-ball with margin). Blue = general, orange = directional; each red point is one sampled target’s true query value, so coverage is the fraction falling inside the band.
Figure 14 quantifies this through the utilisation ratio :
u := | Q t − Q τ ​ # ​ s | δ ι , O ι ​ ( T ) , \displaystyle u:=\frac{\bigl|Q_{t}-Q_{\tau\#s}\bigr|}{\delta_{\iota,O_{\iota}}(\operatorname{T})}, (144)
the distance from the interval’s center to the true query value, expressed as a fraction of its half-width. Because the certified interval is centered at the transported-source value Q τ ​ # ​ s Q_{\tau\#s} , a target is covered exactly when u ≤ 1 u\leq 1 . Therefore, u u measures tightness on a scale where coverage is the unit: values near zero mean the interval is far wider than the target requires, values just below one mean it is as tight as it can be while still covering, and values above one mean the target lies outside it. Higher is therefore better, up to one. We report the mean u ¯ \bar{u} over targets and folds; note that u ¯ < 1 \bar{u}<1 does not imply full coverage, since individual targets may exceed the interval while the mean does not, which is why coverage is reported separately. At the matched point u ¯ < 1 \bar{u}<1 for both certificates, the directional bound consumes the larger fraction of its interval: tighter at no cost to coverage. At the out-of-ball point, the directional u ¯ \bar{u} exceeds one, correctly reporting that the average target now lies beyond the guarantee’s intended scope. The tighter bound is thus also the more faithful diagnostic. Fixing r test r_{\mathrm{test}} and increasing r train r_{\mathrm{train}} , directional coverage rises to one exactly as r train r_{\mathrm{train}} reaches r test r_{\mathrm{test}} , tracking the in-ball boundary, whereas the wider general certificate already covers below it, admitting targets outside the ball it was built for. We show this coverage transition in Figure 25 (Appendix J ).
Figure 14 : ATCE: mean utilisation ratio u ¯ \bar{u} at the same three operating points. The dashed line marks u ¯ = 1 \bar{u}{=}1 , the coverage boundary; higher is tighter, and a mean above the line indicates the average target lies outside the interval (out-of-ball regime).
LiLUCAS.
LiLUCAS is our six-variable benchmark and the only one under the empirical objective. We run three configurations: empirical symmetric, empirical offset, and Gaussian symmetric. Figure 15 contrasts the two empirical variants: transport error against the test magnitude r test r_{\mathrm{test}} , one curve per r train r_{\mathrm{train}} , against the identity baseline. Under the symmetric variant, no trained map ever significantly improves on the identity: at small r test r_{\mathrm{test}} , every trained map is significantly worse than the baseline, and at large r test r_{\mathrm{test}} , the smaller radii are statistically indistinguishable from it until over-robustification degrades them at r train ≥ 2.0 r_{\mathrm{train}}\geq 2.0 (Table 2 ). Under the offset variant, the picture changes: at small r test r_{\mathrm{test}} , the trained maps reduce error by up to 60 % 60\% ( r train = 0.2 r_{\mathrm{train}}{=}0.2 : 0.286 0.286 against the baseline’s 0.716 0.716 , p < 0.001 p<0.001 , with the improvement between 59.7 % 59.7\% and 60.7 % 60.7\% across all five folds; 54 % 54\% and 31 % 31\% at r train = 0.5 r_{\mathrm{train}}{=}0.5 and 1.0 1.0 ). The offset declares a direction for the shift, and the learned map exploits it, anticipating a systematic displacement that the identity ignores. The two baselines differ ( 0.034 0.034 symmetric against 0.716 0.716 offset) because the two ambiguity sets are centered differently: the symmetric box is centered at zero, so perturbations partially cancel, and the identity performs well, while the offset box is centered at δ = 0.5 \delta{=}0.5 , so every target carries a consistent positive displacement that the identity cannot absorb. Each map is therefore compared against the identity on its own prior’s target distribution, and the 60 % 60\% is a within-variant claim. The offset advantage is also confined to small r test r_{\mathrm{test}} , and at every r test r_{\mathrm{test}} , over-robustification at r train ≥ 2.0 r_{\mathrm{train}}\geq 2.0 degrades both variants.
Figure 15 : LiLUCAS (empirical): transport error against r test r_{\mathrm{test}} for the symmetric (left) and offset (right) variants, one line per r train r_{\mathrm{train}} (log scale), against the identity baseline (dashed).

The baselines differ because the offset selector centres its ambiguity set at δ = 0.5 \delta{=}0.5 .

Robustification also helps at large shifts under the Gaussian objective. The Gaussian symmetric configuration shows the complementary crossover (Figure 16 , Table 3 ): at small r test r_{\mathrm{test}} , the identity is again near-optimal, and only the smallest trained radius matches it. However, at the largest test magnitude r test = 4.0 r_{\mathrm{test}}{=}4.0 , the trained maps significantly reduce error relative to the baseline: by 1.4 % 1.4\% at r train = 0.2 r_{\mathrm{train}}{=}0.2 , growing monotonically to 17 % 17\% at r train = 2.0 r_{\mathrm{train}}{=}2.0 (all p < 0.001 p<0.001 , fold-stable to within 0.1 0.1 percentage points). As with ATCE, this is an interior optimum: robustification helps up to r train = 2.0 r_{\mathrm{train}}{=}2.0 and then over-robustifies, with r train = 4.0 r_{\mathrm{train}}{=}4.0 falling well below the baseline. Where the empirical offset earns its advantage at small shifts through the directional prior, the Gaussian symmetric map earns a smaller advantage at large shifts through robustification alone; two faces of the same shift-sizing trade-off seen on ATCE.

The directional certificate is the informative bound. On the certificate side, the results mirror ATCE: as the radius grows, the general certificate quickly becomes vacuous, while the directional certificate remains informative longer. For the Gaussian configuration, the general width crosses the vacuity threshold ( 10 × QS 10\times\text{QS} ) between r train = 0.2 r_{\mathrm{train}}{=}0.2 and 0.5 0.5 , while the directional width stays roughly an order of magnitude below it and remains informative through r train = 0.5 r_{\mathrm{train}}{=}0.5 . The directional certificate is thus the only usable bound at r train = 0.5 r_{\mathrm{train}}{=}0.5 : at r train = 0.2 r_{\mathrm{train}}{=}0.2 , both are informative, and by r train = 1.0 r_{\mathrm{train}}{=}1.0 , neither is. Under the empirical symmetric variant, the general certificate is already vacuous at the smallest radius, so only the directional certificate is ever informative. This is a query scale effect rather than a wider interval: the empirical general certificate is, in fact, narrower than the Gaussian one ( 7.46 7.46 against 10.14 10.14 at r train = 0.2 r_{\mathrm{train}}{=}0.2 ), but the smaller empirical query scale lowers the vacuity threshold below the width. The full width-versus-radius profiles for both configurations are given in Appendix J (Figures 26(b) and 26(a) ).
Figure 17 shows the certified intervals at three operating points for the Gaussian symmetric configuration, r test = 0.5 r_{\mathrm{test}}{=}0.5 fixed. At the matched radius r train = 0.5 r_{\mathrm{train}}{=}0.5 , the directional certificate is informative and covers all four queries in full, while the general certificate is already vacuous: the regime where the directional bound is the only usable one. The out-of-ball panel ( r train = 0.2 r_{\mathrm{train}}{=}0.2 ) illustrates the tightness/coverage trade-off. Here, both certificates are informative, and the directional certificate is 7.6 7.6 - 17.2 × 17.2\times narrower than the general per query ( 10.4 × 10.4\times family-averaged), but because the test shift exceeds the training radius, the directional interval covers only 30 % 30\% of targets. The directional utilisation indeed exceeds one for two of the four queries (Figure 18 ), confirming that the average target lies outside the interval: the tightness is real, but the guarantee holds only within the trained ball. The general certificate covers here, but only because it is wide. At the margin panel ( r train = 1.0 r_{\mathrm{train}}{=}1.0 ) both certificates are vacuous. The empirical symmetric configuration tells the same story, except that the general certificate is never informative: the directional certificate is informative and fully covering at the two smallest radii while the general one is vacuous throughout, and both become vacuous by r train = 1.0 r_{\mathrm{train}}{=}1.0 (Figures 19 and 20 ).

10.3.2 Real-world
The certificate covers at the radius the real shift requires.
On the Portland dataset, the Wasserstein distance between source and target exogenous distributions on the shifted coordinate Z Z of Fanno Creek is 𝒲 2 = 1.165 \mathcal{W}_{2}=1.165 , placing the target inside the Gelbrich ball only for ε ≥ 2 \varepsilon\geq 2 . At and beyond that radius, the directional certificate attains full coverage on all four canopy interventions (Figure 21 ). Across the grid, the general certificate becomes vacuous ( > 10 × QS = 1.63 >10\times\text{QS}=1.63 ) from ε = 0.5 \varepsilon{=}0.5 onward (width 1.75 1.75 ), while the directional certificate remains informative throughout, reaching 1.02 1.02 ( 6.3 × QS 6.3\times\text{QS} ) only at ε = 4.0 \varepsilon{=}4.0 (Figure 27(a) , Appendix J ). Since Z Z is a root, the mechanism set is empty throughout ( η = 0 \eta{=}0 ) and the whole certificate is carried by the environment term; the directional tightening derives entirely from that term’s direction-specificity.
Figure 21 : Portland: coverage against r train r_{\mathrm{train}} . The dashed line marks the Wasserstein distance of the realised Fanno shift ( 𝒲 2 = 1.165 \mathcal{W}_{2}=1.165 ); the green region indicates ε ≥ 𝒲 2 \varepsilon\geq\mathcal{W}_{2} , where the target lies inside the ball.
Observational calibration can point the wrong way. Unlike ATE, choosing the operating radius here from observational data is not entirely safe. The observational transport distance 𝒲 2 2 ​ ( τ # ​ P s obs , P t obs ) \mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}\!\bigl(\tau_{\#}P_{s}^{\mathrm{obs}},P_{t}^{\mathrm{obs}}\bigr) increases monotonically in r train r_{\mathrm{train}} : enlarging the ambiguity set pushes the learned map further from the identity, degrading the fit to the observed target distribution, even though a larger radius is exactly what coverage of the interventional query requires. Therefore, the selector returns the smallest admissible radius, ε = 0.2 \varepsilon{=}0.2 , at which directional coverage is zero. On ATE, the same selector succeeded because there the shifted mechanism gives the observational distance a nontrivial profile over the ( ε , η ) (\varepsilon,\eta) grid with an interior minimum; on Portland, the shift is purely environmental and only ε \varepsilon is swept, leaving no interior optimum for the selector to find. Whether a purely environmental shift always yields a monotone observational profile, or whether this is specific to the present single-radius sweep, we leave for future work. We report the observational-distance profile in Figure 27(b) (Appendix J ).
The consequence is visible at the selected radius. At ε = 0.2 \varepsilon{=}0.2 , the directional interval is tight ( 0.05 0.05 , i.e., 0.3 × QS 0.3\times\text{QS} ), but does not yet cover the truth, which lies outside the narrow band; the general interval does contain the truth, but only because it is wide enough ( 0.70 0.70 ) to span it (Figure 22 ). Increasing r train r_{\mathrm{train}} resolves this: the directional certificate covers all four queries once r train r_{\mathrm{train}} reaches 2.0 2.0 , the radius at which the ball contains the realised shift, while the general certificate widens without becoming more informative (Figure 23 ). Where observational calibration is uninformative, the certificate still supplies the guarantee, at the price of a conservative but valid interval, and coverage is recovered once r train r_{\mathrm{train}} reaches the shift magnitude.
Figure 22 : Portland: certified intervals at r train = 0.2 r_{\mathrm{train}}{=}0.2 for four canopy interventions. Left: full view (general + directional). Right: directional zoom. The directional interval is tight but does not yet cover the truth at this radius.
Figure 23 : Portland: certified intervals across r train r_{\mathrm{train}} for each canopy intervention. Blue = general, orange = directional, dashed = truth. The vertical line marks r train = 2.0 r_{\mathrm{train}}{=}2.0 , where directional coverage reaches one for all queries.
11 Guidelines for Practitioners
We distil the preceding results into concrete guidance on how to apply TraCA in practice, encode prior knowledge, and interpret the guarantees it returns.
Choosing the ambiguity geometry.
The geometry of the ambiguity set should mirror the form of the prior knowledge available about how the target mechanism differs from the source. If only a global bound on the total mechanism shift is known, the Frobenius ball suffices; if per-equation or per-parent bounds are available, the row- or column-wise budgets are appropriate; and if a separate bound is known for each coefficient, the entrywise box is the tightest and most informative choice. The entrywise box is the most useful default since structural knowledge about a causal system is usually localised to specific edges.
Encoding a directional prior.
When the practitioner believes a coefficient shifts in a known direction; i.e., that a treatment effect strengthens or that an environmental driver intensifies in the target population, this belief is encoded through the offset δ \delta of the directional box 𝒜 W box ​ ( δ , B ) \mathcal{A}_{W}^{\mathrm{box}}(\delta,B) . The sign of δ j ​ k \delta_{jk} gives the direction of the anticipated shift, and its magnitude indicates the expected size, while the radius b j ​ k b_{jk} expresses the residual uncertainty around that expectation. Crucially, δ \delta is a declared prior , not a quantity estimated from target data: it encodes assumed domain knowledge, and the guarantees TraCA returns are conditional on that knowledge. A symmetric box ( δ = 0 \delta=0 ) makes no directional commitment and is the appropriate choice when the direction of the shift is genuinely unknown. A wrongly declared offset of large magnitude widens the certificate rather than narrowing it, so the guarantee is robust to a confidently wrong direction only when the declared magnitude is also large; the risky declaration is a small one that points in the wrong direction.
What to expect from the map.
Whether the learned map T \operatorname{T} improves on the identity depends on the information supplied. Under a symmetric ambiguity set, the map departs from the identity but improves on the baseline only in specific regimes: in our experiments at large shifts under the Gaussian objective, where robustification alone yields a significant gain, but not at small shifts, where the identity is already near-optimal. The practical value of a symmetric run, therefore, lies as much in its certified intervals as in its point correction. A directional prior changes this. By recentering the ambiguity set, it gives the map a genuine correction in the declared direction, present by construction and independent of the loss, which can substantially outperform the identity: up to a 60 % 60\% error reduction in our experiments, when the realised shift is both small and aligned with the declared direction. The improvement is not unconditional. If the ambiguity radius is chosen much larger than the shift that actually occurs, the map over-robustifies, and a smaller radius would have served better. Practitioners should, therefore, size the radius to their genuine uncertainty rather than inflating it for perceived safety: larger radii cover more scenarios at the cost of a wider interval and a less precise point estimate.
Calibrating the radius from observational data.
When the target system can be observed under natural conditions but not intervened upon, one may attempt to select the operating radius from observational quantities alone: sweep the training radius, evaluate the observational transport distance 𝒲 2 2 ​ ( τ # ​ P s obs , P t obs ) \mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\tau_{\#}P_{s}^{\mathrm{obs}},P_{t}^{\mathrm{obs}}) at each point, and select the radius that best matches the observed target. This can succeed when the observational distance has an interior optimum over the radius grid. On ATE, where the shift acts on a mechanism, the selector lands on an operating point whose certified interval contains the true interventional query, even though the interventional value was never used. It is not, however, guaranteed to work: on Portland, where the shift is purely environmental, the observational distance increases monotonically with the radius, so enlarging the ambiguity set degrades the observational fit, even though a larger radius is exactly what interventional coverage requires, and the selector returns the smallest radius at which the directional certificate does not yet cover. The practical rule is, therefore, that observational calibration is a useful heuristic when the observational distance is informative about the radius, but it can point the wrong way. Where it fails, the certificate still supplies a valid guarantee, at the price of a conservative radius sized from domain knowledge rather than from the observational fit. When even observational access to the target is unavailable, the radius must be set from domain knowledge alone, and the certificate read against the declared ambiguity set.
Reading the certificates.
TraCA returns two certified intervals per query: a general certificate, valid for any target in the ambiguity set, and a sharper directional certificate that exploits the declared prior. The directional interval is several times tighter than the general one, and on some benchmarks, it is the only informative bound at all, where the general certificate is already vacuous. Within the training ball, it covers the truth at no cost to validity. Outside the ball, when the realised shift is larger than the radius the map was trained for, the directional interval can fail to cover; a feature rather than a defect, since the resulting under-coverage signals that the declared uncertainty was too small for the shift that occurred. The general certificate, being wider, tolerates such misspecification more readily. A practitioner should therefore read the two together: a directional interval that covers indicates the shift lies within the declared ball, while directional under-coverage is a diagnostic that the radius should be enlarged.
12 Conclusion
We recast transportability as a problem of model-level causal abstraction: rather than asking whether individual queries transport, we ask whether a single map carries the entire source interventional family to the target. From this reframing the paper’s contributions follow. We define a hierarchy of source-target consistency notions, from observational matching to a single constructive map transporting the full interventional family, and isolate the compatibility gap that separates query-level from model-level transport. We give exact existence characterisations for a common constructive transport operator in both the Markovian and semi-Markovian cases, reducing, in finite state spaces, to linear feasibility conditions. For linear additive noise models, we formulate approximate transport as a distributionally robust optimisation over target-side mechanism and environment perturbations around a trusted source, derive the stability bounds it rests on, and instantiate it as a practical min-max algorithm. When no exact map exists, the best approximate one still certifies query intervals ℓ ≤ Q t ≤ L \ell\leq Q_{t}\leq L from its residual error, informative precisely in the two regimes where classical transportability is silent: non-transportable queries ( ℛ 1 \mathcal{R}_{1} ) and absent target data ( ℛ 2 \mathcal{R}_{2} ).
These certificates are the practical payoff, and our experiments bear them out. Across a non-transportable effect, two target-agnostic benchmarks, and a real ecological dataset, the certified intervals bracket the ground-truth query at the radius the true shift requires. Where both certificates are informative, the directional one is several times tighter: 2.9 × 2.9\times on ATCE and as much as 17 × 17\times per query on the higher-dimensional LiLUCAS. Furthermore, where the general bound is already vacuous, it remains informative, including on the real ecological data, where at the radius the shift requires, it is the only usable interval. This is also a difference in kind from the classical theory: do-calculus returns a formula when one exists and leaves estimation to the practitioner, whereas TraCA returns the transported estimate together with an interval containing the truth. Transportability, on this view, is not a binary gate but a special, best-case point in a continuum of certified approximate same-level abstraction; i.e., transportability is the existence of a structural relation between two causal models, and where that relation fails to hold exactly, its best approximation still certifies what can be transported.
Limitations.
The framework is currently limited to linear abstraction maps and linear structural causal models. The stability bounds of Section 6.3 and the closed forms underlying both certificates rest on the reduced form 𝒳 = A ​ 𝒰 \mathcal{X}=A\mathcal{U} and the resolvent identity for A = ( I − W ) − 1 A=(I-W)^{-1} , neither of which applies in a nonlinear setting. It further assumes access to the true causal DAG or an accurate estimate obtained by causal discovery: the graph fixes which mechanisms may shift, which transport components are free, and, in the semi-Markovian case, the districts over which the map factorises. A second assumption concerns the source. TraCA treats the source SCM as a trusted causal reference rather than an object to be estimated and places the entire ambiguity on the target side. This is the right division when the source is a well-studied population and the target the unobserved one; the setting the framework is built for, but it means the guarantees inherit whatever error is present in the source model: sampling and estimation errors in the reference are not accounted to the final certified interval.
Future work.
Several directions follow naturally. When the source SCM cannot be treated as a trusted reference, the natural extension is a doubly distributionally robust formulation: a standard DRO ambiguity set on the source, absorbing sampling and estimation errors in the reference model, alongside the target-side set we already use for domain generalisation; robustness over both the model we start from and the shift we transport to. A second direction relaxes the acyclicity of the district quotient, where transport may still hold by non-constructive means beyond the present existence theory. A third concerns the ambiguity set itself: we currently declare the shift geometry, which mechanisms may move, and in the directional case, the offset δ \delta ; a natural next step is to learn this geometry from whatever partial target information is available, shrinking the ambiguity radius around a better-localised target and, with it, tightening the certificate. Finally, our certificates bound arbitrary Lipschitz query functionals and sharpen for linear readouts; extending the directional analysis to richer functionals and deriving tighter stability constants would narrow the gap between the certified interval and the true worst case.
