Appendix overview
The Appendix collects the following supporting material. First, it contains the full proofs and finite-state refinements of the exact Markovian and semi-Markovian existence results from Section 4 (Appendix A ), together with the attainment results for the transport error (Appendix B ). Second, it records auxiliary LAN and minimax results deferred from Sections 6 and 7.1 : a structural counterexample separating mechanism from environment shifts (Appendix C ), the proof of the geometry-specific amplification bounds (Appendix C.1 ), and the information-monotonicity and oracle-convergence properties of the robust value (Appendix D ). Third, it contains the full analytical proofs of the robustness certificates from Section 9 (Appendix E ), including the directional certificates for linear queries and their computable support-function bounds (Appendix E.1 ). Fourth, it provides the formulation-specific optimisation details omitted from the unified presentation in Section 8 , including the Gaussian and empirical adversarial algorithms (Appendix F ). Fifth, it records the structural equations and intervention sets for all the benchmarks of Section 10 (Appendix G ) and the implementation and optimisation details (Appendix H ). Sixth, it clarifies the relationship between TraCA and DiRoCA ( Felekis et al., 2026 ) , separating the shared optimisation machinery from TraCA’s distinct problem and contributions (Appendix I ). Finally, it collects the supplementary experimental results deferred from Section 10 , organised by benchmark (Appendix J ).
Appendix A Exact transportability existence results
This section gathers the exact existence results deferred from Section 4 . It mirrors the structure of the main text: we provide the full recursive proofs in the Markovian and semi-Markovian settings, as well as the finite-state reductions and uniqueness refinements that make these results algorithmically checkable.
A.1 Markovian characterisation
This subsection contains the full proof of the Markovian recursive assembly theorem from Section 4.1.2 . The argument formalises the stagewise prefix-matching intuition used in the main text.
Proof of Theorem 8 . ( ⇒ \Rightarrow ) Assume K K is a CICk, so P s ( ι ) ​ K = P t ( ι ) , ∀ ι ∈ ℐ P_{s}^{(\iota)}K=P_{t}^{(\iota)},~\forall\,\iota\in{\cal I} . Hence, R d ( ι ) = P s ( ι ) ​ K = P t ( ι ) R_{d}^{(\iota)}=P_{s}^{(\iota)}K=P_{t}^{(\iota)} . For each i i , the later kernels K ¯ i + 1 , … , K ¯ d \bar{K}_{i+1},\dots,\bar{K}_{d} do not alter the first i i coordinates, so
R i ( ι ) ​ ( X 1 , … , X i ) = R d ( ι ) ​ ( X 1 , … , X i ) = P t ( ι ) ​ ( X 1 , … , X i ) . \displaystyle R_{i}^{(\iota)}(X_{1},\dots,X_{i})=R_{d}^{(\iota)}(X_{1},\dots,X_{i})=P_{t}^{(\iota)}(X_{1},\dots,X_{i}).
If i ∈ J ι i\in J_{\iota} , then P t ( ι ) ​ ( X i ) = δ a i P_{t}^{(\iota)}(X_{i})=\delta_{a_{i}} , hence R i ( ι ) ​ ( X i ) = δ a i R_{i}^{(\iota)}(X_{i})=\delta_{a_{i}} . If i ∉ J ι i\notin J_{\iota} , then by truncated factorisation in the target model, P t ( ι ) ​ ( X i ∣ X PA ⁡ ( X i ) ) = ℙ ℳ t ​ ( X i ∣ X PA ⁡ ( X i ) ) P_{t}^{(\iota)}\!\bigl(X_{i}\mid X_{\operatorname{PA}(X_{i})}\bigr)=\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{M}_{t}}\!\bigl(X_{i}\mid X_{\operatorname{PA}(X_{i})}\bigr) , and the same conditional therefore holds under R i ( ι ) R_{i}^{(\iota)} , since R i ( ι ) R_{i}^{(\iota)} and P t ( ι ) P_{t}^{(\iota)} agree on the prefix ( X 1 , … , X i ) (X_{1},\dots,X_{i}) .
( ⇐ ) (\Leftarrow) Assume now that conditions (i), (ii) hold for every ι ∈ ℐ \iota\in{\cal I} and every node i ∈ [ d ] i\in[d] . We prove by induction on i i that
R i ( ι ) ​ ( X 1 , … , X i ) = P t ( ι ) ​ ( X 1 , … , X i ) ∀ ι ∈ ℐ . \displaystyle R_{i}^{(\iota)}(X_{1},\dots,X_{i})=P_{t}^{(\iota)}(X_{1},\dots,X_{i})\qquad\forall\,\iota\in{\cal I}.
The case i = 0 i=0 is trivial. Suppose the claim holds for i − 1 i-1 . Since PA ⁡ ( X i ) ⊆ { X 1 , … , X i − 1 } \operatorname{PA}(X_{i})\subseteq\{X_{1},\dots,X_{i-1}\} , the induction hypothesis implies that R i − 1 ( ι ) R_{i-1}^{(\iota)} and P t ( ι ) P_{t}^{(\iota)} agree on the parent tuple of X i X_{i} . If i ∈ J ι i\in J_{\iota} , then by condition (i), R i ( ι ) ​ ( X i ) = δ a i R_{i}^{(\iota)}(X_{i})=\delta_{a_{i}} , which matches the intervened factor in P t ( ι ) P_{t}^{(\iota)} . If i ∉ J ι i\notin J_{\iota} , then by condition (ii) R i ( ι ) ​ ( X i ∣ X PA ⁡ ( X i ) ) = ℙ ℳ t ​ ( X i ∣ X PA ⁡ ( X i ) ) R_{i}^{(\iota)}\!\bigl(X_{i}\mid X_{\operatorname{PA}(X_{i})}\bigr)=\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{M}_{t}}\!\bigl(X_{i}\mid X_{\operatorname{PA}(X_{i})}\bigr) . Since K K is stagewise Markov-preserving,
R i ( ι ) ​ ( X i ∣ X 1 , … , X i − 1 ) = R i ( ι ) ​ ( X i ∣ X PA ⁡ ( X i ) ) = ℙ ℳ t ​ ( X i ∣ X PA ⁡ ( X i ) ) . \displaystyle R_{i}^{(\iota)}\!\bigl(X_{i}\mid X_{1},\dots,X_{i-1}\bigr)=R_{i}^{(\iota)}\!\bigl(X_{i}\mid X_{\operatorname{PA}(X_{i})}\bigr)=\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{M}_{t}}\!\bigl(X_{i}\mid X_{\operatorname{PA}(X_{i})}\bigr).
By the target truncated factorisation,
P t ( ι ) ​ ( X i ∣ X 1 , … , X i − 1 ) = ℙ ℳ t ​ ( X i ∣ X PA ⁡ ( X i ) ) . \displaystyle P_{t}^{(\iota)}\!\bigl(X_{i}\mid X_{1},\dots,X_{i-1}\bigr)=\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{M}_{t}}\!\bigl(X_{i}\mid X_{\operatorname{PA}(X_{i})}\bigr). (145)
Thus,
R i ( ι ) ​ ( X i ∣ X 1 , … , X i − 1 ) = P t ( ι ) ​ ( X i ∣ X 1 , … , X i − 1 ) . \displaystyle R_{i}^{(\iota)}\!\bigl(X_{i}\mid X_{1},\dots,X_{i-1}\bigr)=P_{t}^{(\iota)}\!\bigl(X_{i}\mid X_{1},\dots,X_{i-1}\bigr). (146)
Combining this with equality of the prefix law on ( X 1 , … , X i − 1 ) (X_{1},\dots,X_{i-1}) yields
R i ( ι ) ​ ( X 1 , … , X i ) = P t ( ι ) ​ ( X 1 , … , X i ) . \displaystyle R_{i}^{(\iota)}(X_{1},\dots,X_{i})=P_{t}^{(\iota)}(X_{1},\dots,X_{i}). (147)
This closes the induction. Taking i = d i=d gives
R d ( ι ) = P t ( ι ) . \displaystyle R_{d}^{(\iota)}=P_{t}^{(\iota)}. (148)
Since R d ( ι ) = P s ( ι ) ​ K R_{d}^{(\iota)}=P_{s}^{(\iota)}K , it follows that K K is a CICk over ℐ {\cal I} . The deterministic case is obtained by restricting to kernels of the form K i ( ⋅ ∣ x i ) = δ τ i ​ ( x i ) K_{i}(\cdot\mid x_{i})=\delta_{\tau_{i}(x_{i})} . ■ \blacksquare
A.2 Markovian finite-state stagewise compatibility, uniqueness, and deterministic specialisation
This subsection records the finite-state consequences of the Markovian recursive assembly theorem: a linear-feasibility formulation of stagewise existence, a rank-based uniqueness criterion, and the deterministic specialisation.
We now specialise the stage- i i alignment condition to the case where the state space of a node is finite. Fix a node i ∈ [ d ] i\in[d] and suppose dom ⁡ [ X i ] = { 1 , … , n } \operatorname{dom}[X_{i}]=\{1,\dots,n\} . For each intervention ι ∈ ℐ \iota\in{\cal I} with i ∉ J ι i\notin J_{\iota} , and each parent context p ∈ supp ⁡ ( R i − 1 ( ι ) ​ ( PA ⁡ ( X i ) ) ) p\in\mathrm{supp}\!\Bigl(R_{i-1}^{(\iota)}\bigl(\operatorname{PA}(X_{i})\bigr)\Bigr) , define
μ ι , p ( i − 1 ) := R i − 1 ( ι ) ​ ( X i ∣ PA ⁡ ( X i ) = p ) ∈ Δ ⁡ ( dom ⁡ [ X i ] ) , ν p := ℙ ℳ t ​ ( X i ∣ PA ⁡ ( X i ) = p ) ∈ Δ ⁡ ( dom ⁡ [ X i ] ) , \displaystyle\mu_{\iota,p}^{(i-1)}:=R_{i-1}^{(\iota)}\!\bigl(X_{i}\mid\operatorname{PA}(X_{i})=p\bigr)\in\Delta(\operatorname{dom}[X_{i}]),\nu_{p}:=\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{M}_{t}}\!\bigl(X_{i}\mid\operatorname{PA}(X_{i})=p\bigr)\in\Delta(\operatorname{dom}[X_{i}]),
where Δ ⁡ ( dom ⁡ [ X i ] ) \Delta(\operatorname{dom}[X_{i}]) denotes the probability simplex. Let
𝒜 i := { ( ι , p ) : ι ∈ ℐ , i ∉ J ι , p ∈ supp ( R i − 1 ( ι ) ( PA ( X i ) ) ) } \displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{i}:=\left\{(\iota,p):\iota\in{\cal I},\ i\notin J_{\iota},\ p\in\mathrm{supp}\!\Bigl(R_{i-1}^{(\iota)}\bigl(\operatorname{PA}(X_{i})\bigr)\Bigr)\right\} (149)
denote the set of admissible stage- i i contexts.
Theorem 32 .
At stage i i , the following are equivalent:
(i) There exists a row-stochastic matrix K i ∈ [ 0 , 1 ] n × n K_{i}\in[0,1]^{n\times n} such that
μ ι , p ( i − 1 ) K i = ν p , ∀ ( ι , p ) ∈ 𝒜 i and K i ( ⋅ ∣ a ) = δ a \displaystyle\mu_{\iota,p}^{(i-1)}K_{i}=\nu_{p},~\forall\,(\iota,p)\in\mathcal{A}_{i}~\text{ and }~K_{i}(\cdot\mid a)=\delta_{a} (150)
for every intervention value a a assigned to node i i by some intervention in ℐ {\cal I} .
(ii) There exist probability vectors k 1 , … , k n ∈ Δ ⁡ ( dom ⁡ [ X i ] ) k_{1},\dots,k_{n}\in\Delta(\operatorname{dom}[X_{i}]) , one for each source state v ∈ dom ⁡ [ X i ] v\in\operatorname{dom}[X_{i}] , such that k a = δ a k_{a}=\delta_{a} for every intervention value a a assigned to node i i by some intervention in ℐ {\cal I} , and for every ( ι , p ) ∈ 𝒜 i (\iota,p)\in\mathcal{A}_{i} and every y ∈ dom ⁡ [ X i ] y\in\operatorname{dom}[X_{i}] ,
ν p ​ ( y ) = ∑ v = 1 n μ ι , p ( i − 1 ) ​ ( v ) ​ k v ​ ( y ) . \displaystyle\nu_{p}(y)=\sum_{v=1}^{n}\mu_{\iota,p}^{(i-1)}(v)\,k_{v}(y). (151)
(iii) The linear system
∑ v = 1 n μ ι , p ( i − 1 ) ​ ( v ) ​ K i ​ ( y ∣ v ) = ν p ​ ( y ) ∀ ( ι , p ) ∈ 𝒜 i , ∀ y ∈ dom ⁡ [ X i ] , \displaystyle\sum_{v=1}^{n}\mu_{\iota,p}^{(i-1)}(v)\,K_{i}(y\mid v)=\nu_{p}(y)\qquad\forall\,(\iota,p)\in\mathcal{A}_{i},\ \forall\,y\in\operatorname{dom}[X_{i}], (152)
together with the row-fixing constraints K i ( ⋅ ∣ a ) = δ a K_{i}(\cdot\mid a)=\delta_{a} for every intervention value a a assigned to node i i by some intervention in ℐ {\cal I} , and the row-stochasticity constraints K i ​ ( y ∣ v ) ≥ 0 ​ ∀ v , y , ∑ y = 1 n K i ​ ( y ∣ v ) = 1 ​ ∀ v K_{i}(y\mid v)\geq 0\ \forall\,v,y,~\sum_{y=1}^{n}K_{i}(y\mid v)=1\ \forall\,v , is feasible.
Proof.
The equivalence of (i), (ii), and (iii) is only a matter of notation. A local kernel on the finite state space dom ⁡ [ X i ] = { 1 , … , n } \operatorname{dom}[X_{i}]=\{1,\dots,n\} is the same thing as a row-stochastic matrix K i ∈ [ 0 , 1 ] n × n K_{i}\in[0,1]^{n\times n} . If we write k v := K i ( ⋅ ∣ v ) ∈ Δ ( dom [ X i ] ) k_{v}:=K_{i}(\cdot\mid v)\in\Delta(\operatorname{dom}[X_{i}]) for its v v -th row, then for every admissible pair ( ι , p ) ∈ 𝒜 i (\iota,p)\in\mathcal{A}_{i} and every y ∈ dom ⁡ [ X i ] y\in\operatorname{dom}[X_{i}] ,
( μ ι , p ( i − 1 ) ​ K i ) ​ ( y ) = ∑ v = 1 n μ ι , p ( i − 1 ) ​ ( v ) ​ K i ​ ( y ∣ v ) = ∑ v = 1 n μ ι , p ( i − 1 ) ​ ( v ) ​ k v ​ ( y ) . \displaystyle(\mu_{\iota,p}^{(i-1)}K_{i})(y)=\sum_{v=1}^{n}\mu_{\iota,p}^{(i-1)}(v)\,K_{i}(y\mid v)=\sum_{v=1}^{n}\mu_{\iota,p}^{(i-1)}(v)\,k_{v}(y). (153)
Thus, condition (i) written entrywise is exactly condition (iii), and rewriting the rows of K i K_{i} as probability vectors gives condition (ii). ∎
Theorem 32 is the finite-state version of the stage- i i alignment condition from Theorem 8 . Once the earlier kernels K 1 , … , K i − 1 K_{1},\dots,K_{i-1} have been fixed, the search for K i K_{i} reduces to a linear feasibility problem. The remaining assembly issue is whether these stagewise compatible kernels also satisfy the stagewise Markov-preserving constraint; this is itself a stagewise property of K i K_{i} , given the earlier kernels.
Once stagewise existence has been reduced to a linear system, uniqueness can also be studied node by node: at stage i i , the kernel K i K_{i} is determined by the transformed source conditional family { μ ι , p ( i − 1 ) } ( ι , p ) ∈ 𝒜 i \{\mu_{\iota,p}^{(i-1)}\}_{(\iota,p)\in\mathcal{A}_{i}} , together with the requirement that intervention values be fixed.
Proposition 33 (Uniqueness of the stagewise kernel) .
Fix a node i ∈ [ d ] i\in[d] with a finite state space dom ⁡ [ X i ] = { 1 , … , n } \operatorname{dom}[X_{i}]=\{1,\dots,n\} , and suppose earlier kernels K 1 , … , K i − 1 K_{1},\dots,K_{i-1} have been fixed. Assume there exists a row-stochastic kernel K i K_{i} satisfying the stagewise compatibility conditions of Theorem 32 . Let M i M_{i} be the matrix whose rows are the transformed source conditionals μ ι , p ( i − 1 ) \mu_{\iota,p}^{(i-1)} , one for each ( ι , p ) ∈ 𝒜 i (\iota,p)\in\mathcal{A}_{i} , and one additional row δ a \delta_{a} for each intervention value a a assigned to node i i by some intervention in ℐ {\cal I} . Let N i N_{i} be the matrix whose corresponding rows are the target conditionals ν p \nu_{p} , and the same point masses δ a \delta_{a} . Then:
(i) If rank ⁡ ( M i ) = n \operatorname{rank}(M_{i})=n , then the compatible row-stochastic kernel K i K_{i} is unique.
(ii) If rank ⁡ ( M i ) < n \operatorname{rank}(M_{i})<n , then the equation M i ​ K i = N i M_{i}K_{i}=N_{i} does not uniquely determine K i K_{i} . Its solution set in ℝ n × n \mathbb{R}^{n\times n} is an affine space of positive dimension. Hence, uniqueness, if it still occurs, can only come from the row-stochasticity constraints.
(iii) Uniqueness requires that the rows of M i M_{i} contain at least n n linearly independent vectors. In particular, the total number of admissible stage- i i contexts, together with the number of distinct intervention values assigned to node i i , must be at least n n .
Proof.
By finite discrete stagewise compatibility, the stage- i i constraints are exactly the equations
μ ι , p ( i − 1 ) ​ K i = ν p ∀ ( ι , p ) ∈ 𝒜 i , \displaystyle\mu_{\iota,p}^{(i-1)}K_{i}=\nu_{p}\qquad\forall\,(\iota,p)\in\mathcal{A}_{i}, (154)
together with the intervention-preservation constraints δ a ​ K i = δ a \delta_{a}K_{i}=\delta_{a} for every intervention value a a assigned to node i i by some intervention in ℐ {\cal I} . Stacking these equations gives the matrix equation M i ​ K i = N i M_{i}K_{i}=N_{i} .
For each output state y ∈ dom ⁡ [ X i ] y\in\operatorname{dom}[X_{i}] , let c y ∈ ℝ n c_{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{n} with c y ​ ( v ) := K i ​ ( y ∣ v ) c_{y}(v):=K_{i}(y\mid v) be the y y -th column of K i K_{i} , and let ν ⋅ y \nu_{\cdot y} denote the y y -th column of N i N_{i} . Then M i ​ K i = N i M_{i}K_{i}=N_{i} is equivalent to the family of column systems
M i ​ c y = ν ⋅ y ∀ y ∈ dom ⁡ [ X i ] . \displaystyle M_{i}c_{y}=\nu_{\cdot y}\qquad\forall\,y\in\operatorname{dom}[X_{i}]. (155)
If rank ⁡ ( M i ) = n \operatorname{rank}(M_{i})=n , then ker ⁡ ( M i ) = { 0 } \ker(M_{i})=\{0\} , so each column system has at most one solution. Hence, every column c y c_{y} is uniquely determined, and therefore K i K_{i} is unique. This proves (i).
If rank ⁡ ( M i ) < n \operatorname{rank}(M_{i})<n , then ker ⁡ ( M i ) ≠ { 0 } \ker(M_{i})\neq\{0\} . Thus, if c y c_{y} solves M i ​ c y = ν ⋅ y M_{i}c_{y}=\nu_{\cdot y} , then so does c y + δ c_{y}+\delta for every δ ∈ ker ⁡ ( M i ) \delta\in\ker(M_{i}) , since M i ​ ( c y + δ ) = M i ​ c y + M i ​ δ = ν ⋅ y M_{i}(c_{y}+\delta)=M_{i}c_{y}+M_{i}\delta=\nu_{\cdot y} . Therefore the unconstrained solution set of M i ​ K i = N i M_{i}K_{i}=N_{i} has positive dimension. This proves (ii).
Finally, full column rank n n requires at least n n rows. Thus, the rows of M i M_{i} must contain at least n n linearly independent vectors, which proves (iii). ∎
Remark 34 (Global from stagewise uniqueness) .
Suppose a CICk K = ∏ i = 1 d K i K=\prod_{i=1}^{d}K_{i} exists. If, at each stage i ∈ [ d ] i\in[d] , the corresponding compatible kernel K i K_{i} is unique, then the CICk K K is unique since, by constructiveness, the global kernel factorises componentwise K = ∏ i = 1 d K i K=\prod_{i=1}^{d}K_{i} , and the invariant-node components are fixed to the identity.
Remark 35 .
The row-stochasticity constraints cut out a convex polytope inside ℝ n × n \mathbb{R}^{n\times n} . Hence, the set of stagewise compatible stochastic kernels is the intersection of the affine space { K i : M i ​ K i = N i } \{K_{i}:M_{i}K_{i}=N_{i}\} with that polytope. In particular, when rank ⁡ ( M i ) < n \operatorname{rank}(M_{i})<n , the unconstrained linear system is never unique, but the stochasticity constraints may still reduce the admissible set to a singleton.
Remark 36 .
The matrix M i M_{i} is controlled by the intervention family ℐ {\cal I} through the admissible stage- i i contexts 𝒜 i \mathcal{A}_{i} and through the intervention values that node i i is required to fix. Interventions on ancestors of X i X_{i} alter the partially transformed law R i − 1 ( ι ) R_{i-1}^{(\iota)} , create new admissible contexts ( ι , p ) (\iota,p) , and may increase the rank of M i M_{i} . Direct interventions on X i X_{i} add the corresponding row-fixing constraints. Thus, richer intervention families do not merely make the stagewise compatibility condition more stringent; they can also make the compatible kernel K i K_{i} more tightly determined and, in favourable cases, uniquely determined.
Remark 37 (Deterministic specialisation) .
At stage i i , the recursive compatibility condition is deterministic if and only if there exists a measurable map τ i : dom ⁡ [ X i ] → dom ⁡ [ X i ] \tau_{i}:\operatorname{dom}[X_{i}]\to\operatorname{dom}[X_{i}] such that
( τ i ) # ​ μ ι , p ( i − 1 ) = ν p , ∀ ( ι , p ) ∈ 𝒜 i ​ and ​ τ i ​ ( a ) = a \displaystyle(\tau_{i})_{\#}\mu_{\iota,p}^{(i-1)}=\nu_{p},~\forall\,(\iota,p)\in\mathcal{A}_{i}~\text{ and }~\tau_{i}(a)=a (156)
for every intervention value a a assigned to node i i by some intervention in ℐ {\cal I} . Equivalently, the stagewise kernel can be chosen in the form K i ( ⋅ ∣ x i ) = δ τ i ​ ( x i ) K_{i}(\cdot\mid x_{i})=\delta_{\tau_{i}(x_{i})} . This is strictly more restrictive than stochastic stagewise compatibility: one must find a single deterministic state map, independent of the intervention and the parent context, that transports every transformed source mechanism slice to the corresponding target slice while preserving all intervention values. In particular, a global CICm can exist only if this deterministic stagewise condition holds at every node and the resulting deterministic kernel is stagewise Markov-preserving.
A.3 Semi-Markovian finite-state district compatibility, uniqueness, and related remarks
This subsection records the finite-state districtwise reductions of the semi-Markovian theory. These are the exact district-level analogues of the Markovian finite-state compatibility and uniqueness results.
Finite discrete district compatibility and uniqueness.
Suppose now that dom ⁡ [ X D r ] = { 1 , … , N r } \operatorname{dom}[X_{D_{r}}]=\{1,\dots,N_{r}\} is finite. For every intervention ι ∈ ℐ \iota\in{\cal I} with D r ∩ J ι = ∅ D_{r}\cap J_{\iota}=\emptyset , and every parent context p ∈ supp ⁡ ( R r − 1 ( ι ) ​ ( PA ⁡ ( D r ) ) ) p\in\mathrm{supp}\!\Bigl(R_{r-1}^{(\iota)}(\operatorname{PA}(D_{r}))\Bigr) , define
μ ι , p ( r − 1 ) := R r − 1 ( ι ) ( X D r ∣ PA ( D r ) = p ) ∈ Δ ( dom [ X D r ] ) , ν p := q D r t ( ⋅ ∣ p ) ∈ Δ ( dom [ X D r ] ) , \displaystyle\mu_{\iota,p}^{(r-1)}:=R_{r-1}^{(\iota)}\!\bigl(X_{D_{r}}\mid\operatorname{PA}(D_{r})=p\bigr)\in\Delta(\operatorname{dom}[X_{D_{r}}]),\qquad\nu_{p}:=q_{D_{r}}^{t}(\cdot\mid p)\in\Delta(\operatorname{dom}[X_{D_{r}}]),
and let
𝒜 D r := { ( ι , p ) : ι ∈ ℐ , D r ∩ J ι = ∅ , p ∈ supp ( R r − 1 ( ι ) ( PA ( D r ) ) ) } . \displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{D_{r}}:=\left\{(\iota,p):\iota\in{\cal I},\ D_{r}\cap J_{\iota}=\emptyset,\ p\in\mathrm{supp}\!\Bigl(R_{r-1}^{(\iota)}(\operatorname{PA}(D_{r}))\Bigr)\right\}. (157)
With these substitutions, the finite discrete stagewise compatibility and uniqueness results from the Markovian case carry over verbatim:
i ↝ D r , dom [ X i ] ↝ dom [ X D r ] , n ↝ N r , μ ι , p ( i − 1 ) , ν p , K i ↝ μ ι , p ( r − 1 ) , ν p , K D r . \displaystyle i\rightsquigarrow D_{r},\qquad\operatorname{dom}[X_{i}]\rightsquigarrow\operatorname{dom}[X_{D_{r}}],\qquad n\rightsquigarrow N_{r},\qquad\mu_{\iota,p}^{(i-1)},\nu_{p},K_{i}\rightsquigarrow\mu_{\iota,p}^{(r-1)},\nu_{p},K_{D_{r}}. (158)
In particular, the existence of a compatible stagewise district kernel is, once again, a linear feasibility problem. Exact global CICk existence then follows, provided these stagewise district kernels can be assembled into a stagewise district-preserving districtwise product kernel.
Remark 38 .
The district-based semi-Markovian specialisation preserves the logical structure of the Markovian theory, but the local state space is now dom ⁡ [ X D ] \operatorname{dom}[X_{D}] rather than dom ⁡ [ X i ] \operatorname{dom}[X_{i}] . Thus, the stagewise linear program at a shifted district has N r 2 N_{r}^{2} unknowns, where N r = ∏ i ∈ D r | dom ⁡ [ X i ] | N_{r}=\prod_{i\in D_{r}}|\operatorname{dom}[X_{i}]| , so the computational cost grows rapidly with district size. This is the main practical price of moving from nodes to districts.
Remark 39 .
The query-level consequence of a common constructive kernel has exactly the same formal shape as in the Markovian case:
Φ ⁡ ( π O ι ​ # ​ P t ( ι ) ) = Φ ⁡ ( π O ι ​ # ​ ( P s ( ι ) ​ K ) ) ∀ ( ι , O ι ) ∈ 𝒬 . \displaystyle\Phi\!\left(\pi_{O_{\iota}\#}P_{t}^{(\iota)}\right)=\Phi\!\left(\pi_{O_{\iota}\#}\bigl(P_{s}^{(\iota)}K\bigr)\right)\qquad\forall\,(\iota,O_{\iota})\in\mathcal{Q}. (159)
The difference is that, in the semi-Markovian setting, this identity is no longer merely a structural re-expression of already transportable queries. It can provide a genuine universal transport operator in a regime where direct transportability fails.
Appendix B Attainment of the transport error
Proposition 40 .
Suppose 𝖣 ⁡ ( μ , ν ) = 0 \mathsf{D}(\mu,\nu)=0 iff μ = ν \mu=\nu . Then:
(i) For any admissible common kernel K K , ℰ 𝒬 ​ ( K ) = 0 \mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{Q}}(K)=0 if and only if K K realises exact 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -restricted consistency.
(ii) Given a class 𝔎 \mathfrak{K} of admissible common kernels, let ℰ 𝒬 ⋆ ​ ( 𝔎 ) := inf K ∈ 𝔎 ℰ 𝒬 ​ ( K ) \mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{\star}(\mathfrak{K}):=\inf_{K\in\mathfrak{K}}\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{Q}}(K) . If the infimum is attained, then ℰ 𝒬 ⋆ ​ ( 𝔎 ) = 0 \mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{\star}(\mathfrak{K})=0 if and only if there exists K ∈ 𝔎 K\in\mathfrak{K} realising exact 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -restricted consistency.
Proof.
For (i), ℰ 𝒬 ​ ( K ) \mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{Q}}(K) is a finite average of nonnegative terms e K ( ι , O ι ) e_{K}^{(\iota,O_{\iota})} . Hence ℰ 𝒬 ​ ( K ) = 0 ⇔ e K ( ι , O ι ) = 0 , ∀ ( ι , O ι ) ∈ 𝒬 \mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{Q}}(K)=0\iff e_{K}^{(\iota,O_{\iota})}=0,~\forall\,(\iota,O_{\iota})\in\mathcal{Q} . By the defining property of 𝖣 \mathsf{D} , each pointwise error vanishes if and only if π O ι ​ # ​ ( P s ( ι ) ​ K ) = π O ι ​ # ​ P t ( ι ) , ∀ ( ι , O ι ) ∈ 𝒬 , \pi_{O_{\iota}\#}\bigl(P_{s}^{(\iota)}K\bigr)=\pi_{O_{\iota}\#}P_{t}^{(\iota)},~\forall\,(\iota,O_{\iota})\in\mathcal{Q}, which is exactly 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -restricted consistency.
For (ii), if some K ∈ 𝔎 K\in\mathfrak{K} realises exact 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -restricted consistency, then by (i) ℰ 𝒬 ​ ( K ) = 0 \mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{Q}}(K)=0 , so ℰ 𝒬 ⋆ ​ ( 𝔎 ) = 0 \mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{\star}(\mathfrak{K})=0 . Conversely, if the infimum is attained and ℰ 𝒬 ⋆ ​ ( 𝔎 ) = 0 \mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{\star}(\mathfrak{K})=0 , let K ⋆ ∈ 𝔎 K^{\star}\in\mathfrak{K} be a minimiser; then ℰ 𝒬 ​ ( K ⋆ ) = 0 \mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{Q}}(K^{\star})=0 , and part (i) implies that K ⋆ K^{\star} realises exact 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -restricted consistency. ∎
Corollary 41 .
Suppose 𝔎 \mathfrak{K} is compact in a topology under which ℰ 𝒬 \mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{Q}} is lower semicontinuous. Then the infimum
ℰ 𝒬 ⋆ ​ ( 𝔎 ) := inf K ∈ 𝔎 ℰ 𝒬 ​ ( K ) \mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{\star}(\mathfrak{K}):=\inf_{K\in\mathfrak{K}}\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{Q}}(K)
is attained. Consequently, ℰ 𝒬 ⋆ ​ ( 𝔎 ) = 0 \mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{\star}(\mathfrak{K})=0 if and only if ∃ K ∈ 𝔎 \exists\,K\in\mathfrak{K} realising exact 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} -restricted consistency.
Proof.
By the generalised Weierstrass theorem, every lower semicontinuous real-valued function on a compact set attains its minimum. Hence ℰ 𝒬 ⋆ ​ ( 𝔎 ) \mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{\star}(\mathfrak{K}) is attained under the stated hypotheses. The final equivalence then follows from Proposition 40 (ii). ∎
Accordingly, whenever the admissible class 𝔎 \mathfrak{K} satisfies these compactness and lower semicontinuity conditions, vanishing transport error is equivalent to exact realisability within 𝔎 \mathfrak{K} .
Appendix C Auxiliary structural results
Proposition 42 .
Let two SCMs share the same endogenous space 𝒳 \mathcal{X} and exogenous space 𝒰 \mathcal{U} , but have different reduced-form maps g , g ′ : 𝒰 → 𝒳 g,g^{\prime}:\mathcal{U}\to\mathcal{X} . Fix an environment ρ \rho on 𝒰 \mathcal{U} , and define the induced observable distributions
P := g # ​ ρ , P ′ := g # ′ ​ ρ . \displaystyle P:=g_{\#}\rho,\qquad P^{\prime}:=g^{\prime}_{\#}\rho. (160)
In general, there need not exist any exogenous law ρ ⋆ \rho^{\star} on 𝒰 \mathcal{U} such that
g # ​ ρ ⋆ = P ′ . \displaystyle g_{\#}\rho^{\star}=P^{\prime}. (161)
Equivalently, the distribution generated by changing the reduced-form mechanism from g g to g ′ g^{\prime} cannot in general be reproduced by keeping g g fixed and changing only the exogenous law.
Proof.
Take 𝒰 = 𝒳 = { 0 , 1 } \mathcal{U}=\mathcal{X}=\{0,1\} , let ρ \rho be the uniform distribution on { 0 , 1 } \{0,1\} , and define the source reduced-form map g g by g ⁡ ( 0 ) = g ⁡ ( 1 ) = 0 g(0)=g(1)=0 . Then the source SCM outputs the endogenous value 0 0 almost surely, regardless of the environment. Hence, for every probability measure ρ ⋆ \rho^{\star} on 𝒰 \mathcal{U} , g # ​ ρ ⋆ = δ 0 g_{\#}\rho^{\star}=\delta_{0} . Now define the target reduced-form map g ′ g^{\prime} by g ′ ​ ( y ) = y g^{\prime}(y)=y . Under the same environment ρ \rho ,
g # ′ ​ ρ = 1 2 ​ δ 0 + 1 2 ​ δ 1 . \displaystyle g^{\prime}_{\#}\rho=\tfrac{1}{2}\delta_{0}+\tfrac{1}{2}\delta_{1}. (162)
This distribution cannot be written as g # ​ ρ ⋆ g_{\#}\rho^{\star} for any ρ ⋆ \rho^{\star} , because every pushforward through g g is equal to δ 0 \delta_{0} and therefore assigns zero mass to the state 1 1 . ∎
C.1 Corollary 19
Proof of Corollary 19 . The result follows by bounding ‖ A ι ​ ( R ι ​ Δ ​ W ) ‖ 2 \|A_{\iota}(R_{\iota}\Delta W)\|_{2} in a way adapted to each ambiguity geometry. Different geometries naturally control different matrix norms: row budgets control the induced ∞ \infty -norm, column budgets the induced 1 1 -norm, and entrywise bounds give direct entrywise control. Since γ ι \gamma_{\iota} is defined through the spectral norm, we convert these to ∥ ⋅ ∥ 2 \|\cdot\|_{2} via standard norm inequalities.
• Frobenius ball. Submultiplicativity gives
‖ A ι ​ ( R ι ​ Δ ​ W ) ‖ 2 ≤ ‖ A ι ‖ 2 ​ ‖ R ι ​ Δ ​ W ‖ 2 ≤ ‖ A ι ‖ 2 ​ ‖ Δ ​ W ‖ F ≤ ‖ A ι ‖ 2 ​ η . \displaystyle\|A_{\iota}(R_{\iota}\Delta W)\|_{2}\leq\|A_{\iota}\|_{2}\|R_{\iota}\Delta W\|_{2}\leq\|A_{\iota}\|_{2}\|\Delta W\|_{F}\leq\|A_{\iota}\|_{2}\eta. (163)
• Row-wise budgets. For each row ℓ \ell ,
∑ k | ( A ι ​ ( R ι ​ Δ ​ W ) ) ℓ ​ k | ≤ ∑ j | ( A ι ) ℓ ​ j | ​ ∑ k | ( R ι ​ Δ ​ W ) j ​ k | . \displaystyle\sum_{k}|(A_{\iota}(R_{\iota}\Delta W))_{\ell k}|\leq\sum_{j}|(A_{\iota})_{\ell j}|\sum_{k}|(R_{\iota}\Delta W)_{jk}|. (164)
Since R ι R_{\iota} is diagonal it can only zero out rows, and admissible perturbations satisfy Δ W j ⋅ = 0 \Delta W_{j\cdot}=0 for j ∉ 𝒦 j\notin\mathcal{K} , so
∑ k | ( A ι ​ ( R ι ​ Δ ​ W ) ) ℓ ​ k | ≤ ∑ j ∈ 𝒦 | ( A ι ) ℓ ​ j | ​ ∑ k | Δ ​ W j ​ k | ≤ ∑ j ∈ 𝒦 | ( A ι ) ℓ ​ j | ​ ρ j . \displaystyle\sum_{k}|(A_{\iota}(R_{\iota}\Delta W))_{\ell k}|\leq\sum_{j\in\mathcal{K}}|(A_{\iota})_{\ell j}|\sum_{k}|\Delta W_{jk}|\leq\sum_{j\in\mathcal{K}}|(A_{\iota})_{\ell j}|\rho_{j}. (165)
Hence ‖ A ι ​ ( R ι ​ Δ ​ W ) ‖ ∞ ≤ max ⁡ ∑ j ∈ 𝒦 ℓ ⁡ | ( A ι ) ℓ ​ j | ​ ρ j \|A_{\iota}(R_{\iota}\Delta W)\|_{\infty}\leq\max_{\ell}\sum_{j\in\mathcal{K}}|(A_{\iota})_{\ell j}|\rho_{j} , and by ‖ M ‖ 2 ≤ d ​ ‖ M ‖ ∞ \|M\|_{2}\leq\sqrt{d}\,\|M\|_{\infty} ,
‖ A ι ​ ( R ι ​ Δ ​ W ) ‖ 2 ≤ d ​ max ⁡ ∑ j ∈ 𝒦 ℓ ⁡ | ( A ι ) ℓ ​ j | ​ ρ j . \displaystyle\|A_{\iota}(R_{\iota}\Delta W)\|_{2}\leq\sqrt{d}\,\max_{\ell}\sum_{j\in\mathcal{K}}|(A_{\iota})_{\ell j}|\rho_{j}. (166)
• Column-wise budgets. For each column k k ,
∑ ℓ | ( A ι ​ ( R ι ​ Δ ​ W ) ) ℓ ​ k | ≤ ∑ j ( ∑ ℓ | ( A ι ) ℓ ​ j | ) ​ | ( R ι ​ Δ ​ W ) j ​ k | . \displaystyle\sum_{\ell}|(A_{\iota}(R_{\iota}\Delta W))_{\ell k}|\leq\sum_{j}\Bigl(\sum_{\ell}|(A_{\iota})_{\ell j}|\Bigr)|(R_{\iota}\Delta W)_{jk}|. (167)
Again R ι R_{\iota} can only zero out rows, so
∑ ℓ | ( A ι ​ ( R ι ​ Δ ​ W ) ) ℓ ​ k | ≤ ‖ A ι ‖ 1 ​ ∑ j | Δ ​ W j ​ k | ≤ ‖ A ι ‖ 1 ​ c k . \displaystyle\sum_{\ell}|(A_{\iota}(R_{\iota}\Delta W))_{\ell k}|\leq\|A_{\iota}\|_{1}\sum_{j}|\Delta W_{jk}|\leq\|A_{\iota}\|_{1}c_{k}. (168)
Hence ‖ A ι ​ ( R ι ​ Δ ​ W ) ‖ 1 ≤ ‖ A ι ‖ 1 ​ max k ​ c k \|A_{\iota}(R_{\iota}\Delta W)\|_{1}\leq\|A_{\iota}\|_{1}\max_{k}c_{k} , and by ‖ M ‖ 2 ≤ d ​ ‖ M ‖ 1 \|M\|_{2}\leq\sqrt{d}\,\|M\|_{1} ,
‖ A ι ​ ( R ι ​ Δ ​ W ) ‖ 2 ≤ d ​ ‖ A ι ‖ 1 ​ max k ​ c k . \displaystyle\|A_{\iota}(R_{\iota}\Delta W)\|_{2}\leq\sqrt{d}\,\|A_{\iota}\|_{1}\max_{k}c_{k}. (169)
• Entrywise box. The bound | Δ ​ W j ​ k | ≤ b j ​ k |\Delta W_{jk}|\leq b_{jk} gives | ( R ι ​ Δ ​ W ) j ​ k | ≤ ( R ι ) j ​ j ​ b j ​ k |(R_{\iota}\Delta W)_{jk}|\leq(R_{\iota})_{jj}b_{jk} , so entrywise | A ι ​ ( R ι ​ Δ ​ W ) | ≤ | A ι | ​ R ι ​ B |A_{\iota}(R_{\iota}\Delta W)|\leq|A_{\iota}|R_{\iota}B , and therefore
‖ A ι ​ ( R ι ​ Δ ​ W ) ‖ 2 ≤ ‖ A ι ​ ( R ι ​ Δ ​ W ) ‖ F ≤ ‖ | A ι | ​ R ι ​ B ‖ F . \displaystyle\|A_{\iota}(R_{\iota}\Delta W)\|_{2}\leq\|A_{\iota}(R_{\iota}\Delta W)\|_{F}\leq\bigl\||A_{\iota}|R_{\iota}B\bigr\|_{F}. (170)
For the directional box 𝒜 W box ​ ( δ , B ) \mathcal{A}_{W}^{\mathrm{box}}(\delta,B) the same argument applies with b j ​ k b_{jk} replaced by the effective half-width | δ j ​ k | + b j ​ k |\delta_{jk}|+b_{jk} , since admissible entries satisfy | Δ ​ W j ​ k | ≤ | δ j ​ k | + b j ​ k |\Delta W_{jk}|\leq|\delta_{jk}|+b_{jk} , giving ( 84 ). Taking the supremum over Δ ​ W \Delta W in each case yields ( 80 )-( 84 ). ■ \blacksquare
Appendix D Information monotonicity of the robust value
This section records the monotonicity and oracle-convergence properties of the robust transport value referenced in Remark 17 . Both are direct consequences of the minimax structure of ( 61 ).
Proposition 43 (Information monotonicity and ambiguity tightening) .
Let ( Ξ , d 𝒜 ) (\Xi,d_{\mathcal{A}}) denote the space of admissible target-side perturbation parameters, and for any nonempty 𝒜 ⊆ Ξ \mathcal{A}\subseteq\Xi let diam d 𝒜 ​ ( 𝒜 ) := sup ξ , ξ ′ ∈ 𝒜 d 𝒜 ​ ( ξ , ξ ′ ) \mathrm{diam}_{d_{\mathcal{A}}}(\mathcal{A}):=\sup_{\xi,\xi^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}}d_{\mathcal{A}}(\xi,\xi^{\prime}) . Define the robust transport value associated with an admissible target-side ambiguity set 𝒜 \mathcal{A} by
ℜ ⋆ ​ ( 𝒜 ) := inf T sup ξ ∈ 𝒜 1 | ℐ | ​ ∑ ι ∈ ℐ 𝖥 ι ​ ( T , ξ ) . \displaystyle\mathfrak{R}^{\star}(\mathcal{A}):=\inf_{\operatorname{T}}\sup_{\xi\in\mathcal{A}}\frac{1}{|\mathcal{I}|}\sum_{\iota\in\mathcal{I}}\mathsf{F}_{\iota}(\operatorname{T},\xi). (171)
Fix a reference target perturbation ξ ∘ ∈ Ξ \xi^{\circ}\in\Xi , interpreted as the true target-side perturbation, so that ℜ ⋆ ​ ( { ξ ∘ } ) \mathfrak{R}^{\star}(\{\xi^{\circ}\}) is the oracle value attained under full knowledge of the target. Then:
(i) (Monotonicity and certificate tightening.) If 𝒜 1 ⊆ 𝒜 2 \mathcal{A}_{1}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{2} , then ℜ ⋆ ​ ( 𝒜 1 ) ≤ ℜ ⋆ ​ ( 𝒜 2 ) \mathfrak{R}^{\star}(\mathcal{A}_{1})\leq\mathfrak{R}^{\star}(\mathcal{A}_{2}) . If, in addition, ξ ∘ ∈ 𝒜 1 \xi^{\circ}\in\mathcal{A}_{1} and T 1 ⋆ ∈ arg ​ min T sup ξ ∈ 𝒜 1 1 | ℐ | ∑ ι ∈ ℐ 𝖥 ι ( T , ξ ) \operatorname{T}_{1}^{\star}\in\displaystyle\argmin_{\operatorname{T}}\sup_{\xi\in\mathcal{A}_{1}}\tfrac{1}{|\mathcal{I}|}\sum_{\iota\in\mathcal{I}}\mathsf{F}_{\iota}(\operatorname{T},\xi) , then
1 | ℐ | ​ ∑ ι ∈ ℐ 𝖥 ι ​ ( T 1 ⋆ , ξ ∘ ) ≤ ℜ ⋆ ​ ( 𝒜 1 ) ≤ ℜ ⋆ ​ ( 𝒜 2 ) . \displaystyle\frac{1}{|\mathcal{I}|}\sum_{\iota\in\mathcal{I}}\mathsf{F}_{\iota}(\operatorname{T}_{1}^{\star},\xi^{\circ})\leq\mathfrak{R}^{\star}(\mathcal{A}_{1})\leq\mathfrak{R}^{\star}(\mathcal{A}_{2}). (172)
(ii) (Quantitative tightening.) Suppose each 𝖥 ι ​ ( T , ξ ) \mathsf{F}_{\iota}(\operatorname{T},\xi) is L ι L_{\iota} -Lipschitz in ξ \xi with respect to d 𝒜 d_{\mathcal{A}} , uniformly over T \operatorname{T} , and set L ¯ := 1 | ℐ | ​ ∑ ι ∈ ℐ L ι \bar{L}:=\tfrac{1}{|\mathcal{I}|}\sum_{\iota\in\mathcal{I}}L_{\iota} . Then for any nonempty bounded 𝒜 ⊆ Ξ \mathcal{A}\subseteq\Xi with ξ ∘ ∈ 𝒜 \xi^{\circ}\in\mathcal{A} ,
0 ≤ ℜ ⋆ ​ ( 𝒜 ) − ℜ ⋆ ​ ( { ξ ∘ } ) ≤ L ¯ ​ diam d 𝒜 ​ ( 𝒜 ) . \displaystyle 0\leq\mathfrak{R}^{\star}(\mathcal{A})-\mathfrak{R}^{\star}(\{\xi^{\circ}\})\leq\bar{L}\,\mathrm{diam}_{d_{\mathcal{A}}}(\mathcal{A}). (173)
If 𝒜 k + 1 ⊆ 𝒜 k \mathcal{A}_{k+1}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{k} , ξ ∘ ∈ 𝒜 k ​ ∀ k \xi^{\circ}\in\mathcal{A}_{k}~\forall k , and diam d 𝒜 ​ ( 𝒜 k ) → 0 \mathrm{diam}_{d_{\mathcal{A}}}(\mathcal{A}_{k})\to 0 , then ℜ ⋆ ​ ( 𝒜 k ) → ℜ ⋆ ​ ( { ξ ∘ } ) \mathfrak{R}^{\star}(\mathcal{A}_{k})\to\mathfrak{R}^{\star}(\{\xi^{\circ}\}) .
Proof.
(i) For every fixed T \operatorname{T} , 𝒜 1 ⊆ 𝒜 2 \mathcal{A}_{1}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{2} gives sup ξ ∈ 𝒜 1 1 | ℐ | ​ ∑ ι 𝖥 ι ​ ( T , ξ ) ≤ sup ξ ∈ 𝒜 2 1 | ℐ | ​ ∑ ι 𝖥 ι ​ ( T , ξ ) \sup_{\xi\in\mathcal{A}_{1}}\tfrac{1}{|\mathcal{I}|}\sum_{\iota}\mathsf{F}_{\iota}(\operatorname{T},\xi)\leq\sup_{\xi\in\mathcal{A}_{2}}\tfrac{1}{|\mathcal{I}|}\sum_{\iota}\mathsf{F}_{\iota}(\operatorname{T},\xi) ; taking inf T \inf_{\operatorname{T}} yields the first inequality. When ξ ∘ ∈ 𝒜 1 \xi^{\circ}\in\mathcal{A}_{1} , evaluating the supremum at ξ ∘ \xi^{\circ} gives
1 | ℐ | ​ ∑ ι 𝖥 ι ​ ( T 1 ⋆ , ξ ∘ ) ≤ sup ξ ∈ 𝒜 1 1 | ℐ | ​ ∑ ι 𝖥 ι ​ ( T 1 ⋆ , ξ ) = ℜ ⋆ ​ ( 𝒜 1 ) , \tfrac{1}{|\mathcal{I}|}\sum_{\iota}\mathsf{F}_{\iota}(\operatorname{T}_{1}^{\star},\xi^{\circ})\leq\sup_{\xi\in\mathcal{A}_{1}}\tfrac{1}{|\mathcal{I}|}\sum_{\iota}\mathsf{F}_{\iota}(\operatorname{T}_{1}^{\star},\xi)=\mathfrak{R}^{\star}(\mathcal{A}_{1}),
where the equality holds because T 1 ⋆ \operatorname{T}_{1}^{\star} attains the outer infimum; chaining with the first inequality completes the bound.
(ii) Fix T \operatorname{T} and ξ ∈ 𝒜 \xi\in\mathcal{A} . Since ξ ∘ ∈ 𝒜 \xi^{\circ}\in\mathcal{A} , we have d 𝒜 ​ ( ξ , ξ ∘ ) ≤ diam d 𝒜 ​ ( 𝒜 ) d_{\mathcal{A}}(\xi,\xi^{\circ})\leq\mathrm{diam}_{d_{\mathcal{A}}}(\mathcal{A}) , so Lipschitz continuity gives
1 | ℐ | ​ ∑ ι 𝖥 ι ​ ( T , ξ ) ≤ 1 | ℐ | ​ ∑ ι 𝖥 ι ​ ( T , ξ ∘ ) + L ¯ ​ diam d 𝒜 ​ ( 𝒜 ) . \tfrac{1}{|\mathcal{I}|}\sum_{\iota}\mathsf{F}_{\iota}(\operatorname{T},\xi)\leq\tfrac{1}{|\mathcal{I}|}\sum_{\iota}\mathsf{F}_{\iota}(\operatorname{T},\xi^{\circ})+\bar{L}\,\mathrm{diam}_{d_{\mathcal{A}}}(\mathcal{A}).
Taking sup ξ ∈ 𝒜 \sup_{\xi\in\mathcal{A}} and then inf T \inf_{\operatorname{T}} yields the upper bound; the lower bound is part (i) applied to { ξ ∘ } ⊆ 𝒜 \{\xi^{\circ}\}\subseteq\mathcal{A} . The convergence claim follows from the two-sided bound and diam d 𝒜 ​ ( 𝒜 k ) → 0 \mathrm{diam}_{d_{\mathcal{A}}}(\mathcal{A}_{k})\to 0 . ∎
The proposition formalises the scaling-with-information property of TraCA. Part (i) is qualitative: if additional target information shrinks the ambiguity set, then the robust objective can only decrease, and the resulting certificate on the true target loss can only improve. Part (ii) is quantitative: if the loss varies smoothly with the target parameter, then the gap to the oracle value is controlled by the size of the ambiguity set. Thus, whenever the ambiguity diameter vanishes, the robust problem approaches the full-information oracle problem.
Remark 44 .
A stronger oracle-convergence statement can be obtained without Lipschitz continuity, under additional regularity assumptions. Let { 𝒜 k } k ≥ 1 \{\mathcal{A}_{k}\}_{k\geq 1} be a nested sequence of compact ambiguity sets with ⋂ k ≥ 1 𝒜 k = { ξ ∘ } \bigcap_{k\geq 1}\mathcal{A}_{k}=\{\xi^{\circ}\} , let the admissible class of maps be compact, and let 𝖥 ι \mathsf{F}_{\iota} be jointly continuous in ( T , ξ ) (\operatorname{T},\xi) . Define
G k ​ ( T ) := sup ξ ∈ 𝒜 k 1 | ℐ | ​ ∑ ι ∈ ℐ 𝖥 ι ​ ( T , ξ ) , G ∞ ​ ( T ) := 1 | ℐ | ​ ∑ ι ∈ ℐ 𝖥 ι ​ ( T , ξ ∘ ) . G_{k}(\operatorname{T}):=\sup_{\xi\in\mathcal{A}_{k}}\tfrac{1}{|\mathcal{I}|}\sum_{\iota\in\mathcal{I}}\mathsf{F}_{\iota}(\operatorname{T},\xi),\qquad G_{\infty}(\operatorname{T}):=\tfrac{1}{|\mathcal{I}|}\sum_{\iota\in\mathcal{I}}\mathsf{F}_{\iota}(\operatorname{T},\xi^{\circ}).
For each fixed k k , G k G_{k} is continuous in T \operatorname{T} because it maximises a jointly continuous function of ( T , ξ ) (\operatorname{T},\xi) over the compact set 𝒜 k \mathcal{A}_{k} ; this is an instance of Berge’s maximum theorem ( Aliprantis and Border, 2006 , Thm. 17.31) . Since the 𝒜 k \mathcal{A}_{k} are nested and shrink to { ξ ∘ } \{\xi^{\circ}\} , continuity in ξ \xi implies G k ​ ( T ) ↓ G ∞ ​ ( T ) G_{k}(\operatorname{T})\downarrow G_{\infty}(\operatorname{T}) pointwise for every T \operatorname{T} , and G ∞ G_{\infty} is continuous in T \operatorname{T} . Because the admissible class of maps is compact, Dini’s theorem ( Rudin, 1976 , Thm. 7.13) upgrades this monotone pointwise convergence to uniform convergence. Hence
ℜ ⋆ ​ ( 𝒜 k ) = inf T G k ​ ( T ) ⟶ inf T G ∞ ​ ( T ) = ℜ ⋆ ​ ( { ξ ∘ } ) . \mathfrak{R}^{\star}(\mathcal{A}_{k})=\inf_{\operatorname{T}}G_{k}(\operatorname{T})\longrightarrow\inf_{\operatorname{T}}G_{\infty}(\operatorname{T})=\mathfrak{R}^{\star}(\{\xi^{\circ}\}).
Appendix E Proofs of the robustness certificates
This section contains the full analytical proofs of the Gaussian and empirical robustness certificates stated in Section 9 . In both cases, the proof strategy is to decompose the discrepancy into transport, mechanism, and environment terms, and to control each term separately.
Proof of Theorem 24 . Fix ( ι , O ι ) ∈ 𝒬 (\iota,O_{\iota})\in\mathcal{Q} and write S := S O ι S:=S_{O_{\iota}} . Our goal is to bound the Gaussian query-restricted loss
𝒲 2 2 ​ ( π O ι ​ # ​ ( T # ⁡ P s ( ι ) ) , π O ι ​ # ​ P t ( ι ) ​ ( Δ ​ W , μ t , Σ t ) ) . \displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}\!\Bigl(\pi_{O_{\iota}\#}(\operatorname{T}_{\#}P_{s}^{(\iota)}),\;\pi_{O_{\iota}\#}P_{t}^{(\iota)}(\Delta W,\mu_{t},\Sigma_{t})\Bigr). (174)
We first introduce the intermediate Gaussian measure
ν ~ ι , O ι ​ ( Δ ​ W ) := 𝒩 ⁡ ( S ​ A ι ′ ​ ( Δ ​ W ) ​ μ s , S ​ A ι ′ ​ ( Δ ​ W ) ​ Σ s ​ A ι ′ ​ ( Δ ​ W ) ⊤ ​ S ⊤ ) . \displaystyle\widetilde{\nu}_{\iota,O_{\iota}}(\Delta W):=\mathcal{N}\!\Bigl(SA^{\prime}_{\iota}(\Delta W)\mu_{s},\;SA^{\prime}_{\iota}(\Delta W)\Sigma_{s}A^{\prime}_{\iota}(\Delta W)^{\top}S^{\top}\Bigr). (175)
This is exactly the projected target law one would obtain if the mechanism had already changed from A ι A_{\iota} to A ι ′ ​ ( Δ ​ W ) A^{\prime}_{\iota}(\Delta W) , but the environment were still the source one. We now compare the true source and target laws by passing through this intermediate Gaussian. By the triangle inequality in 𝒲 2 \mathcal{W}_{2} ,
𝒲 2 ​ ( π O ι ​ # ​ ( T # ⁡ P s ( ι ) ) , π O ι ​ # ​ P t ( ι ) ) ≤ Ξ 1 , ι , O ι + Ξ 2 , ι , O ι , \displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{2}\bigl(\pi_{O_{\iota}\#}(\operatorname{T}_{\#}P_{s}^{(\iota)}),\;\pi_{O_{\iota}\#}P_{t}^{(\iota)}\bigr)\leq\Xi_{1,\iota,O_{\iota}}+\Xi_{2,\iota,O_{\iota}}, (176)
where
Ξ 1 , ι , O ι := 𝒲 2 ​ ( π O ι ​ # ​ ( T # ⁡ P s ( ι ) ) , ν ~ ι , O ι ​ ( Δ ​ W ) ) , \displaystyle\Xi_{1,\iota,O_{\iota}}:=\mathcal{W}_{2}\bigl(\pi_{O_{\iota}\#}(\operatorname{T}_{\#}P_{s}^{(\iota)}),\;\widetilde{\nu}_{\iota,O_{\iota}}(\Delta W)\bigr), (177)
Ξ 2 , ι , O ι := 𝒲 2 ​ ( ν ~ ι , O ι ​ ( Δ ​ W ) , π O ι ​ # ​ P t ( ι ) ​ ( Δ ​ W , μ t , Σ t ) ) . \displaystyle\Xi_{2,\iota,O_{\iota}}:=\mathcal{W}_{2}\bigl(\widetilde{\nu}_{\iota,O_{\iota}}(\Delta W),\;\pi_{O_{\iota}\#}P_{t}^{(\iota)}(\Delta W,\mu_{t},\Sigma_{t})\bigr). (178)
Hence
𝒲 2 2 ​ ( π O ι ​ # ​ ( T # ⁡ P s ( ι ) ) , π O ι ​ # ​ P t ( ι ) ) ≤ 2 ​ Ξ 1 , ι , O ι 2 + 2 ​ Ξ 2 , ι , O ι 2 . \displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}\bigl(\pi_{O_{\iota}\#}(\operatorname{T}_{\#}P_{s}^{(\iota)}),\;\pi_{O_{\iota}\#}P_{t}^{(\iota)}\bigr)\leq 2\Xi_{1,\iota,O_{\iota}}^{2}+2\Xi_{2,\iota,O_{\iota}}^{2}. (179)
We first bound Ξ 1 , ι , O ι \Xi_{1,\iota,O_{\iota}} . Since both measures are linear pushforwards of the same Gaussian U ∼ 𝒩 ⁡ ( μ s , Σ s ) U\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu_{s},\Sigma_{s}) , we couple them by taking
X := S ​ T ⁡ A ι ​ U , Y := S ​ A ι ′ ​ ( Δ ​ W ) ​ U . \displaystyle X:=S\operatorname{T}A_{\iota}U,\qquad Y:=SA^{\prime}_{\iota}(\Delta W)U. (180)
Then X X and Y Y have the required marginals, so ( X , Y ) (X,Y) is a coupling of π O ι ​ # ​ ( T # ⁡ P s ( ι ) ) \pi_{O_{\iota}\#}(\operatorname{T}_{\#}P_{s}^{(\iota)}) and ν ~ ι , O ι ​ ( Δ ​ W ) \widetilde{\nu}_{\iota,O_{\iota}}(\Delta W) . By the coupling characterisation of 𝒲 2 \mathcal{W}_{2} ,
𝒲 2 2 ​ ( μ , ν ) ≤ 𝔼 ​ ‖ X − Y ‖ 2 2 , \displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\mu,\nu)\leq\mathbb{E}\|X-Y\|_{2}^{2}, (181)
hence
Ξ 1 , ι , O ι 2 ≤ 𝔼 ​ ‖ S ​ T ⁡ A ι ​ U − S ​ A ι ′ ​ ( Δ ​ W ) ​ U ‖ 2 2 . \displaystyle\Xi_{1,\iota,O_{\iota}}^{2}\leq\mathbb{E}\Bigl\|S\operatorname{T}A_{\iota}U-SA^{\prime}_{\iota}(\Delta W)U\Bigr\|_{2}^{2}. (182)
Now factor the difference:
S ​ T ⁡ A ι ​ U − S ​ A ι ′ ​ ( Δ ​ W ) ​ U = S ⁡ ( T ⁡ A ι − A ι ′ ​ ( Δ ​ W ) ) ​ U . \displaystyle S\operatorname{T}A_{\iota}U-SA^{\prime}_{\iota}(\Delta W)U=S\bigl(\operatorname{T}A_{\iota}-A^{\prime}_{\iota}(\Delta W)\bigr)U. (183)
The key identity is
T ⁡ A ι − A ι ′ ​ ( Δ ​ W ) = ( T − I ) ​ A ι + ( A ι − A ι ′ ​ ( Δ ​ W ) ) . \displaystyle\operatorname{T}A_{\iota}-A^{\prime}_{\iota}(\Delta W)=(\operatorname{T}-I)A_{\iota}+(A_{\iota}-A^{\prime}_{\iota}(\Delta W)). (184)
This separates the error into a transport mismatch term ( T − I ) ​ A ι (\operatorname{T}-I)A_{\iota} , and a mechanism perturbation term A ι − A ι ′ ​ ( Δ ​ W ) A_{\iota}-A^{\prime}_{\iota}(\Delta W) . Substituting this and using ( x + y ) 2 ≤ 2 ​ x 2 + 2 ​ y 2 (x+y)^{2}\leq 2x^{2}+2y^{2} , we obtain
Ξ 1 , ι , O ι 2 ≤ 2 ​ 𝔼 ​ ‖ S ⁡ ( T − I ) ​ A ι ​ U ‖ 2 2 + 2 ​ 𝔼 ​ ‖ S ⁡ ( A ι − A ι ′ ​ ( Δ ​ W ) ) ​ U ‖ 2 2 . \displaystyle\Xi_{1,\iota,O_{\iota}}^{2}\leq\ 2\,\mathbb{E}\|S(\operatorname{T}-I)A_{\iota}U\|_{2}^{2}+2\,\mathbb{E}\|S(A_{\iota}-A^{\prime}_{\iota}(\Delta W))U\|_{2}^{2}.
For a Gaussian vector U ∼ 𝒩 ⁡ ( μ s , Σ s ) U\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu_{s},\Sigma_{s}) and any matrix M M , writing U = μ s + ( U − μ s ) U=\mu_{s}+(U-\mu_{s}) and using 𝔼 ⁡ [ U − μ s ] = 0 \mathbb{E}[U-\mu_{s}]=0 , we get 𝔼 ​ ‖ M ​ U ‖ 2 2 = ‖ M ​ μ s ‖ 2 2 + 𝔼 ​ ‖ M ⁡ ( U − μ s ) ‖ 2 2 = ‖ M ​ μ s ‖ 2 2 + Tr ⁡ ( M ​ Σ s ​ M ⊤ ) \mathbb{E}\|MU\|_{2}^{2}=\|M\mu_{s}\|_{2}^{2}+\mathbb{E}\|M(U-\mu_{s})\|_{2}^{2}=\|M\mu_{s}\|_{2}^{2}+\Tr(M\Sigma_{s}M^{\top}) . Applying this identity with M = S ⁡ ( T − I ) ​ A ι M=S(\operatorname{T}-I)A_{\iota} and M = S ⁡ ( A ι − A ι ′ ​ ( Δ ​ W ) ) M=S(A_{\iota}-A^{\prime}_{\iota}(\Delta W)) yields
Ξ 1 , ι , O ι 2 ≤ \displaystyle\Xi_{1,\iota,O_{\iota}}^{2}\leq\; 2 ​ ‖ S ⁡ ( T − I ) ​ A ι ​ μ s ‖ 2 2 + 2 ​ Tr ⁡ ( S ⁡ ( T − I ) ​ A ι ​ Σ s ​ A ι ⊤ ​ ( T − I ) ⊤ ​ S ⊤ ) \displaystyle 2\|S(\operatorname{T}-I)A_{\iota}\mu_{s}\|_{2}^{2}+2\Tr\!\Bigl(S(\operatorname{T}-I)A_{\iota}\Sigma_{s}A_{\iota}^{\top}(\operatorname{T}-I)^{\top}S^{\top}\Bigr)
+ 2 ​ ‖ S ⁡ ( A ι − A ι ′ ​ ( Δ ​ W ) ) ​ μ s ‖ 2 2 \displaystyle\quad+2\|S(A_{\iota}-A^{\prime}_{\iota}(\Delta W))\mu_{s}\|_{2}^{2}
+ 2 ​ Tr ⁡ ( S ⁡ ( A ι − A ι ′ ​ ( Δ ​ W ) ) ​ Σ s ​ ( A ι − A ι ′ ​ ( Δ ​ W ) ) ⊤ ​ S ⊤ ) . \displaystyle\quad+2\Tr\!\Bigl(S(A_{\iota}-A^{\prime}_{\iota}(\Delta W))\Sigma_{s}(A_{\iota}-A^{\prime}_{\iota}(\Delta W))^{\top}S^{\top}\Bigr).
Since Σ s \Sigma_{s} is symmetric positive semidefinite, we have Σ s ⪯ ‖ Σ s ‖ 2 ​ I \Sigma_{s}\preceq\|\Sigma_{s}\|_{2}I . Hence Tr ⁡ ( M ​ Σ s ​ M ⊤ ) = Tr ⁡ ( Σ s ​ M ⊤ ​ M ) ≤ ‖ Σ s ‖ 2 ​ Tr ⁡ ( M ⊤ ​ M ) = ‖ Σ s ‖ 2 ​ ‖ M ‖ F 2 \Tr(M\Sigma_{s}M^{\top})=\Tr(\Sigma_{s}M^{\top}M)\leq\|\Sigma_{s}\|_{2}\,\Tr(M^{\top}M)=\|\Sigma_{s}\|_{2}\,\|M\|_{F}^{2} . Thus,
Ξ 1 , ι , O ι 2 ≤ \displaystyle\Xi_{1,\iota,O_{\iota}}^{2}\leq\; 2 ​ ‖ S ⁡ ( T − I ) ​ A ι ​ μ s ‖ 2 2 + 2 ​ ‖ Σ s ‖ 2 ​ ‖ S ⁡ ( T − I ) ​ A ι ‖ F 2 \displaystyle 2\|S(\operatorname{T}-I)A_{\iota}\mu_{s}\|_{2}^{2}+2\|\Sigma_{s}\|_{2}\,\|S(\operatorname{T}-I)A_{\iota}\|_{F}^{2}
+ 2 ​ ‖ S ⁡ ( A ι − A ι ′ ​ ( Δ ​ W ) ) ‖ 2 2 ​ ‖ μ s ‖ 2 2 \displaystyle\quad+2\|S(A_{\iota}-A^{\prime}_{\iota}(\Delta W))\|_{2}^{2}\|\mu_{s}\|_{2}^{2}
+ 2 ​ ‖ Σ s ‖ 2 ​ ‖ S ⁡ ( A ι − A ι ′ ​ ( Δ ​ W ) ) ‖ F 2 . \displaystyle\quad+2\|\Sigma_{s}\|_{2}\,\|S(A_{\iota}-A^{\prime}_{\iota}(\Delta W))\|_{F}^{2}.
Now use the propagator perturbation control. Since ‖ A ι ′ ​ ( Δ ​ W ) − A ι ‖ 2 ≤ α ι \|A^{\prime}_{\iota}(\Delta W)-A_{\iota}\|_{2}\leq\alpha_{\iota} , and S S is a coordinate selector; i.e. ‖ S ⁡ ( A ι − A ι ′ ​ ( Δ ​ W ) ) ‖ 2 ≤ α ι \|S(A_{\iota}-A^{\prime}_{\iota}(\Delta W))\|_{2}\leq\alpha_{\iota} . Also, an | O ι | × d |O_{\iota}|\times d matrix has Frobenius norm bounded by | O ι | \sqrt{|O_{\iota}|} times its spectral norm, so
‖ S ⁡ ( A ι − A ι ′ ​ ( Δ ​ W ) ) ‖ F 2 ≤ | O ι | ​ α ι 2 . \displaystyle\|S(A_{\iota}-A^{\prime}_{\iota}(\Delta W))\|_{F}^{2}\leq|O_{\iota}|\,\alpha_{\iota}^{2}. (185)
Substituting these bounds gives
Ξ 1 , ι , O ι 2 ≤ 2 ​ ‖ S ⁡ ( T − I ) ​ A ι ​ μ s ‖ 2 2 + 2 ​ ‖ Σ s ‖ 2 ​ ‖ S ⁡ ( T − I ) ​ A ι ‖ F 2 + 2 ​ α ι 2 ​ ‖ μ s ‖ 2 2 + 2 ​ | O ι | ​ ‖ Σ s ‖ 2 ​ α ι 2 . \displaystyle\Xi_{1,\iota,O_{\iota}}^{2}\leq 2\|S(\operatorname{T}-I)A_{\iota}\mu_{s}\|_{2}^{2}+2\|\Sigma_{s}\|_{2}\,\|S(\operatorname{T}-I)A_{\iota}\|_{F}^{2}+2\alpha_{\iota}^{2}\|\mu_{s}\|_{2}^{2}+2|O_{\iota}|\,\|\Sigma_{s}\|_{2}\,\alpha_{\iota}^{2}. (186)
We next bound Ξ 2 , ι , O ι \Xi_{2,\iota,O_{\iota}} . Here the linear map S ​ A ι ′ ​ ( Δ ​ W ) SA^{\prime}_{\iota}(\Delta W) is the same in both distributions; only the input Gaussian changes from 𝒩 ⁡ ( μ s , Σ s ) \mathcal{N}(\mu_{s},\Sigma_{s}) to 𝒩 ⁡ ( μ t , Σ t ) \mathcal{N}(\mu_{t},\Sigma_{t}) . More generally, for a fixed linear map L L its pushforward is ‖ L ‖ 2 \|L\|_{2} -Lipschitz in 𝒲 2 \mathcal{W}_{2} : if π ∈ Π ⁡ ( μ , ν ) \pi\in\Pi(\mu,\nu) , then its image under ( x , y ) ↦ ( L ​ x , L ​ y ) (x,y)\mapsto(Lx,Ly) is a coupling of L # ​ μ L_{\#}\mu and L # ​ ν L_{\#}\nu , and ‖ L ​ x − L ​ y ‖ 2 ≤ ‖ L ‖ 2 ​ ‖ x − y ‖ 2 \|Lx-Ly\|_{2}\leq\|L\|_{2}\|x-y\|_{2} yields, after taking the infimum over π \pi ,
𝒲 2 ​ ( L # ​ μ , L # ​ ν ) ≤ ‖ L ‖ 2 ​ 𝒲 2 ​ ( μ , ν ) . \displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{2}(L_{\#}\mu,L_{\#}\nu)\leq\|L\|_{2}\,\mathcal{W}_{2}(\mu,\nu). (187)
Thus,
Ξ 2 , ι , O ι ≤ ‖ S ​ A ι ′ ​ ( Δ ​ W ) ‖ 2 ​ 𝒲 2 ​ ( 𝒩 ⁡ ( μ s , Σ s ) , 𝒩 ⁡ ( μ t , Σ t ) ) . \displaystyle\Xi_{2,\iota,O_{\iota}}\leq\|SA^{\prime}_{\iota}(\Delta W)\|_{2}\,\mathcal{W}_{2}\bigl(\mathcal{N}(\mu_{s},\Sigma_{s}),\,\mathcal{N}(\mu_{t},\Sigma_{t})\bigr). (188)
Since ( μ t , Σ t ) ∈ 𝒜 ρ ​ ( ε ) (\mu_{t},\Sigma_{t})\in\mathcal{A}_{\rho}(\varepsilon) ,
𝒲 2 ​ ( 𝒩 ⁡ ( μ s , Σ s ) , 𝒩 ⁡ ( μ t , Σ t ) ) ≤ ε . \displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{2}\bigl(\mathcal{N}(\mu_{s},\Sigma_{s}),\,\mathcal{N}(\mu_{t},\Sigma_{t})\bigr)\leq\varepsilon. (189)
Moreover,
S ​ A ι ′ ​ ( Δ ​ W ) = S ​ A ι + S ⁡ ( A ι ′ ​ ( Δ ​ W ) − A ι ) , \displaystyle SA^{\prime}_{\iota}(\Delta W)=SA_{\iota}+S(A^{\prime}_{\iota}(\Delta W)-A_{\iota}), (190)
so by the triangle inequality,
‖ S ​ A ι ′ ​ ( Δ ​ W ) ‖ 2 ≤ ‖ S ​ A ι ‖ 2 + ‖ S ⁡ ( A ι ′ ​ ( Δ ​ W ) − A ι ) ‖ 2 ≤ ‖ S ​ A ι ‖ 2 + α ι . \displaystyle\|SA^{\prime}_{\iota}(\Delta W)\|_{2}\leq\|SA_{\iota}\|_{2}+\|S(A^{\prime}_{\iota}(\Delta W)-A_{\iota})\|_{2}\leq\|SA_{\iota}\|_{2}+\alpha_{\iota}. (191)
Hence,
Ξ 2 , ι , O ι ≤ ( ‖ S ​ A ι ‖ 2 + α ι ) ​ ε . \displaystyle\Xi_{2,\iota,O_{\iota}}\leq(\|SA_{\iota}\|_{2}+\alpha_{\iota})\varepsilon. (192)
Finally, combine the bounds on Ξ 1 , ι , O ι \Xi_{1,\iota,O_{\iota}} and Ξ 2 , ι , O ι \Xi_{2,\iota,O_{\iota}} in
𝒲 2 2 ≤ 2 ​ Ξ 1 2 + 2 ​ Ξ 2 2 . \displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}\leq 2\Xi_{1}^{2}+2\Xi_{2}^{2}. (193)
This yields the pointwise estimate
sup Δ ​ W ∈ 𝒜 W ( μ t , Σ t ) ∈ 𝒜 ρ ​ ( ε ) 𝒲 2 2 ​ ( π O ι ​ # ​ ( T # ⁡ P s ( ι ) ) , π O ι ​ # ​ P t ( ι ) ) ≤ δ ι , O ι ρ ​ ( T ) 2 . \displaystyle\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\Delta W\in\mathcal{A}_{W}\\ (\mu_{t},\Sigma_{t})\in\mathcal{A}_{\rho}(\varepsilon)\end{subarray}}\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}\!\Bigl(\pi_{O_{\iota}\#}(\operatorname{T}_{\#}P_{s}^{(\iota)}),\;\pi_{O_{\iota}\#}P_{t}^{(\iota)}\Bigr)\leq\delta_{\iota,O_{\iota}}^{\rho}(\operatorname{T})^{2}. (194)
Averaging over 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} gives ( 115 ). ■ \blacksquare
Proof of Theorem 25 . Fix ( ι , O ι ) ∈ 𝒬 (\iota,O_{\iota})\in\mathcal{Q} and write S := S O ι S:=S_{O_{\iota}} . The projected empirical residual can be decomposed as
S ⁡ ( T ⁡ A ι ​ U s − A ι ′ ​ ( Δ ​ W ) ​ ( U s + Θ ) ) = S ⁡ ( T − I ) ​ A ι ​ U s + S ⁡ ( A ι − A ι ′ ​ ( Δ ​ W ) ) ​ U s − S ​ A ι ′ ​ ( Δ ​ W ) ​ Θ . \displaystyle S\bigl(\operatorname{T}A_{\iota}U^{s}-A^{\prime}_{\iota}(\Delta W)(U^{s}+\Theta)\bigr)=S(\operatorname{T}-I)A_{\iota}U^{s}+S(A_{\iota}-A^{\prime}_{\iota}(\Delta W))U^{s}-SA^{\prime}_{\iota}(\Delta W)\Theta. (195)
These are exactly the three contributions we need to control: transport-map error, mechanism error, and environment perturbation. Taking Frobenius norms and using the triangle inequality gives
‖ S ⁡ ( T ⁡ A ι ​ U s − A ι ′ ​ ( Δ ​ W ) ​ ( U s + Θ ) ) ‖ F \displaystyle\bigl\|S\bigl(\operatorname{T}A_{\iota}U^{s}-A^{\prime}_{\iota}(\Delta W)(U^{s}+\Theta)\bigr)\bigr\|_{F} ≤ ‖ S ⁡ ( T − I ) ​ A ι ​ U s ‖ F \displaystyle\leq\|S(\operatorname{T}-I)A_{\iota}U^{s}\|_{F}
+ ‖ S ⁡ ( A ι − A ι ′ ​ ( Δ ​ W ) ) ‖ 2 ​ ‖ U s ‖ F \displaystyle\quad+\|S(A_{\iota}-A^{\prime}_{\iota}(\Delta W))\|_{2}\,\|U^{s}\|_{F}
+ ‖ S ​ A ι ′ ​ ( Δ ​ W ) ‖ 2 ​ ‖ Θ ‖ F . \displaystyle\quad+\|SA^{\prime}_{\iota}(\Delta W)\|_{2}\,\|\Theta\|_{F}.
Now use the uniform bounds
‖ S ⁡ ( A ι − A ι ′ ​ ( Δ ​ W ) ) ‖ 2 ≤ α ι , ‖ S ​ A ι ′ ​ ( Δ ​ W ) ‖ 2 ≤ ‖ S ​ A ι ‖ 2 + α ι , ‖ Θ ‖ F ≤ ε ​ N . \displaystyle\|S(A_{\iota}-A^{\prime}_{\iota}(\Delta W))\|_{2}\leq\alpha_{\iota},\qquad\|SA^{\prime}_{\iota}(\Delta W)\|_{2}\leq\|SA_{\iota}\|_{2}+\alpha_{\iota},\qquad\|\Theta\|_{F}\leq\varepsilon\sqrt{N}. (196)
Substituting these into the previous display yields
‖ S ⁡ ( T ⁡ A ι ​ U s − A ι ′ ​ ( Δ ​ W ) ​ ( U s + Θ ) ) ‖ F ≤ δ ι , O ι U ​ ( T ) . \displaystyle\bigl\|S\bigl(\operatorname{T}A_{\iota}U^{s}-A^{\prime}_{\iota}(\Delta W)(U^{s}+\Theta)\bigr)\bigr\|_{F}\leq\delta_{\iota,O_{\iota}}^{U}(\operatorname{T}). (197)
This is a pointwise bound valid for every admissible ( Δ ​ W , Θ ) (\Delta W,\Theta) . Squaring, averaging over 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} , and then taking the supremum proves ( 117 ). ■ \blacksquare
E.1 Directional certificates for linear queries
This subsection proves Corollary 30 and shows how the directional mechanism modulus m ι , q ​ ( v ) m_{\iota,q}(v) is computed. Throughout, fix ( ι , O ι ) ∈ 𝒬 (\iota,O_{\iota})\in\mathcal{Q} , write q := S O ι ⊤ ​ c q:=S_{O_{\iota}}^{\top}c for the query direction, and abbreviate the perturbed propagator A ι ′ ​ ( Δ ​ W ) A_{\iota}^{\prime}(\Delta W) to A ι ′ A_{\iota}^{\prime} . The input signal is v = μ s ( ι ) v=\mu_{s}^{(\iota)} in the Gaussian case and v = U ¯ s , ( ι ) := 1 N ​ U s , ( ι ) ​ 𝟏 v=\bar{U}^{s,(\iota)}:=\tfrac{1}{N}U^{s,(\iota)}\mathbf{1} in the empirical one. Since ι \iota is fixed, we drop the superscript ( ι ) (\iota) and write U ¯ s \bar{U}^{s} and μ s \mu_{s} .
Proof of the directional certificate.
Take the empirical case and write Θ ¯ := 1 N ​ Θ ​ 𝟏 \bar{\Theta}:=\tfrac{1}{N}\Theta\mathbf{1} for the mean environment perturbation. The transported-source and target query values are Q τ ​ # ​ s ( ι , O ι ) = q ⊤ ​ T ⁡ A ι ​ U ¯ s Q_{\tau\#s}^{(\iota,O_{\iota})}=q^{\top}\operatorname{T}A_{\iota}\bar{U}^{s} and Q t ( ι , O ι ) = q ⊤ ​ A ι ′ ​ ( U ¯ s + Θ ¯ ) Q_{t}^{(\iota,O_{\iota})}=q^{\top}A_{\iota}^{\prime}(\bar{U}^{s}+\bar{\Theta}) . These differ in two ways: the mechanism and the environment, so we compare them using an intermediate quantity carrying the target mechanism but the source environment. Inserting q ⊤ ​ A ι ′ ​ U ¯ s q^{\top}A_{\iota}^{\prime}\bar{U}^{s} splits the discrepancy into three contributions:
Q t ( ι , O ι ) − Q τ ​ # ​ s ( ι , O ι ) = q ⊤ ​ ( I − T ) ​ A ι ​ U ¯ s ⏟ transport + q ⊤ ​ ( A ι ′ − A ι ) ​ U ¯ s ⏟ mechanism + q ⊤ ​ A ι ′ ​ Θ ¯ ⏟ environment . \displaystyle Q_{t}^{(\iota,O_{\iota})}-Q_{\tau\#s}^{(\iota,O_{\iota})}=\underbrace{q^{\top}(I-\operatorname{T})A_{\iota}\bar{U}^{s}}_{\text{transport}}+\underbrace{q^{\top}(A_{\iota}^{\prime}-A_{\iota})\bar{U}^{s}}_{\text{mechanism}}+\underbrace{q^{\top}A_{\iota}^{\prime}\bar{\Theta}}_{\text{environment}}. (198)
The identity is exact; the triangle inequality bounds the error by the sum of the three absolute values. The transport term is computable directly since T \operatorname{T} , A ι A_{\iota} , q q , and U ¯ s \bar{U}^{s} are all known. The mechanism term is bounded by m ι , q ​ ( U ¯ s ) m_{\iota,q}(\bar{U}^{s}) by Definition 18 , and the rest of this subsection is devoted to computing it.
For the environment term, recall that the perturbation is confined to the shifted coordinates, Θ = M 𝒦 ​ Θ \Theta=M_{\mathcal{K}}\Theta , hence Θ ¯ = M 𝒦 ​ Θ ¯ \bar{\Theta}=M_{\mathcal{K}}\bar{\Theta} . The inner product is therefore unchanged if we mask the other factor as well, which discards the coordinates that the environment cannot reach:
q ⊤ A ι ′ Θ ¯ = ( M 𝒦 A ι ′ q ⊤ ) ⊤ Θ ¯ . \displaystyle q^{\top}A_{\iota}^{\prime}\bar{\Theta}=\bigl(M_{\mathcal{K}}A_{\iota}^{\prime}{}^{\top}q\bigr)^{\top}\bar{\Theta}. (199)
The size of Θ ¯ \bar{\Theta} follows from the ambiguity set: since ‖ Θ ‖ F ≤ ε ​ N \|\Theta\|_{F}\leq\varepsilon\sqrt{N} and ‖ 𝟏 ‖ 2 = N \|\mathbf{1}\|_{2}=\sqrt{N} ,
‖ Θ ¯ ‖ 2 ≤ 1 N ​ ‖ Θ ‖ F ​ ‖ 𝟏 ‖ 2 = 1 N ​ ‖ Θ ‖ F ≤ ε , \displaystyle\|\bar{\Theta}\|_{2}\leq\tfrac{1}{N}\|\Theta\|_{F}\|\mathbf{1}\|_{2}=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\|\Theta\|_{F}\leq\varepsilon, (200)
so the N \sqrt{N} in the definition of 𝒜 U ​ ( ε ) \mathcal{A}_{U}(\varepsilon) cancels, and the mean perturbation has a size of at most ε \varepsilon regardless of sample size. Cauchy-Schwarz then gives | q ⊤ A ι ′ Θ ¯ | ≤ ε ∥ M 𝒦 A ι ′ ⊤ q ∥ 2 |q^{\top}A_{\iota}^{\prime}\bar{\Theta}|\leq\varepsilon\|M_{\mathcal{K}}A_{\iota}^{\prime}{}^{\top}q\|_{2} .
The Gaussian case is identical with U ¯ s \bar{U}^{s} replaced by μ s \mu_{s} and Θ ¯ \bar{\Theta} by h := μ t − μ s h:=\mu_{t}-\mu_{s} : the Wasserstein constraint gives ‖ h ‖ 2 ≤ ε \|h\|_{2}\leq\varepsilon , the mean pinning gives h = M 𝒦 ​ h h=M_{\mathcal{K}}h , and the same step applies. No covariance term appears because a mean query depends on the target law only through its mean. Taking the supremum over Δ ​ W ∈ 𝒜 W \Delta W\in\mathcal{A}_{W} yields ( 140 ).
The mechanism term reduces to first order.
The remaining task is to control m ι , q ​ ( v ) m_{\iota,q}(v) , the worst change that the perturbed mechanism can induce in the query direction. The obstacle is that Δ ​ W \Delta W sits inside a matrix inverse. The finite perturbation expansion ( 53 ):
A ι ′ ​ ( Δ ​ W ) − A ι = ∑ r = 1 d − 1 ( A ι ​ R ι ​ Δ ​ W ) r ​ A ι , \displaystyle A_{\iota}^{\prime}(\Delta W)-A_{\iota}=\sum_{r=1}^{d-1}(A_{\iota}R_{\iota}\Delta W)^{r}A_{\iota}, (201)
terminates because A ι ​ R ι ​ Δ ​ W A_{\iota}R_{\iota}\Delta W is strictly lower triangular under the fixed acyclic ordering, hence nilpotent. For the geometries used here, the sum collapses to a single term. Suppose the budget lies on the incoming edges of a single shifted node j j . Only row j j of W W can move, so Δ ​ W = e j ​ w ⊤ \Delta W=e_{j}w^{\top} , where w w collects that row’s entries and is supported on PA ⁡ ( X j ) \operatorname{PA}(X_{j}) . Hence, A ι ​ R ι ​ Δ ​ W = u ​ w ⊤ A_{\iota}R_{\iota}\Delta W=u\,w^{\top} with u := A ι ​ R ι ​ e j u:=A_{\iota}R_{\iota}e_{j} : an outer product, and therefore rank one. For any such matrix, ( u ​ w ⊤ ) 2 = ( w ⊤ ​ u ) ​ u ​ w ⊤ (uw^{\top})^{2}=(w^{\top}u)\,uw^{\top} , so it squares to zero exactly when w ⊤ ​ u = 0 w^{\top}u=0 . Here
w ⊤ ​ u = ∑ k w k ​ ( A ι ) k ​ j ​ ( R ι ) j ​ j = 0 , \displaystyle w^{\top}u=\textstyle\sum_{k}w_{k}\,(A_{\iota})_{kj}(R_{\iota})_{jj}=0, (202)
because w k w_{k} is nonzero only for parents of j j , which precede j j in the ordering, while ( A ι ) k ​ j = 0 (A_{\iota})_{kj}=0 for k < j k<j by lower triangularity. Structurally: the perturbed edges all point into j j , whereas j j propagates only forward, so the perturbation can never feed back into itself. Every term with r ≥ 2 r\geq 2 therefore vanishes.
The first-order term and what it means.
Projecting the surviving term onto q q and v v , and grouping the factors on either side of Δ ​ W \Delta W ,
q ⊤ ​ A ι ​ R ι ​ Δ ​ W ​ A ι ​ v = a ⊤ ​ Δ ​ W ​ b , a := R ι ​ A ι ⊤ ​ q , b := A ι ​ v , \displaystyle q^{\top}A_{\iota}R_{\iota}\Delta WA_{\iota}v=a^{\top}\Delta W\,b,\qquad a:=R_{\iota}A_{\iota}^{\top}q,\quad b:=A_{\iota}v, (203)
using R ι ⊤ = R ι R_{\iota}^{\top}=R_{\iota} , since R ι R_{\iota} is diagonal. Here a a and b b are ordinary vectors, fixed by the model and the query, and independent of Δ ​ W \Delta W , so they can be computed before the supremum over 𝒜 W \mathcal{A}_{W} is taken. They form a forward and a backward pass: b b propagates the source signal forward to the point where the mechanism acts, and a a propagates the query readout backward to the same point. Thus, b n b_{n} is the value arriving at node n n , and a m a_{m} is the total derivative of the query with respect to a perturbation at node m m . Their outer product a ​ b ⊤ ab^{\top} , with entries a m ​ b n a_{m}b_{n} , is the sensitivity matrix : entry ( m , n ) (m,n) is the derivative of the query with respect to the coefficient Δ ​ W m ​ n \Delta W_{mn} ; i.e., how much the query moves per unit change in the coefficient Δ ​ W m ​ n \Delta W_{mn} . Under the rank-one structure above, we therefore have m ι , q ​ ( v ) = β ι , q ​ ( v ) m_{\iota,q}(v)=\beta_{\iota,q}(v) exactly, where
β ι , q ​ ( v ) := sup Δ ​ W ∈ 𝒜 W | a ⊤ ​ Δ ​ W ​ b | . \displaystyle\beta_{\iota,q}(v):=\sup_{\Delta W\in\mathcal{A}_{W}}\bigl|a^{\top}\Delta W\,b\bigr|. (204)
The general case, where the budget spans several shifted nodes and higher-order terms survive, is treated at the end of this subsection.
Computing β ι , q ​ ( v ) \beta_{\iota,q}(v) .
Written out entrywise, the first-order term is
a ⊤ ​ Δ ​ W ​ b = ∑ m , n Δ ​ W m ​ n ​ a m ​ b n , \displaystyle a^{\top}\Delta W\,b=\sum_{m,n}\Delta W_{mn}\,a_{m}b_{n}, (205)
a sum of products; that is, the Frobenius inner product ⟨ Δ ​ W , a ​ b ⊤ ⟩ F \langle\Delta W,\,ab^{\top}\rangle_{F} . So β ι , q ​ ( v ) \beta_{\iota,q}(v) is the maximum of a linear functional over 𝒜 W \mathcal{A}_{W} , which is, by definition, the support function of 𝒜 W \mathcal{A}_{W} at the sensitivity matrix a ​ b ⊤ ab^{\top} . This makes the computation a budgeting problem. Each coefficient Δ ​ W m ​ n \Delta W_{mn} pays | a m ​ b n | |a_{m}b_{n}| per unit spent on it, and 𝒜 W \mathcal{A}_{W} specifies how much may be spent and where. The optimum spends the budget where the payoff is largest; the four geometries differ only in what spending is permitted, and each rule has a closed-form answer. Write P ∈ { 0 , 1 } d × d P\in\{0,1\}^{d\times d} for the support of 𝒜 W \mathcal{A}_{W} , the entries of Δ ​ W \Delta W permitted to vary. Every admissible Δ ​ W \Delta W vanishes off P P , so only P ⊙ ( a ​ b ⊤ ) P\odot(ab^{\top}) can contribute, and for the four geometries of Section 8.1 ,
𝒜 W F ​ ( η ) : β ι , q ​ ( v ) ≤ η ​ ‖ P ⊙ ( a ​ b ⊤ ) ‖ F , 𝒜 W box ​ ( δ , B ) : β ι , q ​ ( v ) ≤ ∑ m , n P m ​ n ​ ( | δ m ​ n | + B m ​ n ) ​ | a m | ​ | b n | , \displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{W}^{\mathrm{F}}(\eta):\;\beta_{\iota,q}(v)\leq\eta\|P\odot(ab^{\top})\|_{F},~~\mathcal{A}_{W}^{\mathrm{box}}(\delta,B):\;\beta_{\iota,q}(v)\leq\textstyle\sum_{m,n}P_{mn}\bigl(|\delta_{mn}|+B_{mn}\bigr)|a_{m}||b_{n}|,
𝒜 W row ( ρ ) : β ι , q ( v ) ≤ ∑ m ρ m | a m | ∥ P m , : ⊙ b ∥ ∞ , 𝒜 W col ( c ) : β ι , q ( v ) ≤ ∑ n c n | b n | ∥ P : , n ⊙ a ∥ ∞ . \displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{W}^{\mathrm{row}}(\rho):\;\beta_{\iota,q}(v)\leq\textstyle\sum_{m}\rho_{m}|a_{m}|\,\|P_{m,:}\odot b\|_{\infty},~~\mathcal{A}_{W}^{\mathrm{col}}(c):\;\beta_{\iota,q}(v)\leq\textstyle\sum_{n}c_{n}|b_{n}|\,\|P_{:,n}\odot a\|_{\infty}. (206)
Each line is the optimal spending rule for its budget. The Frobenius ball allows one shared allowance measured in Euclidean length, so the entries compete, and the best Δ ​ W \Delta W points along a ​ b ⊤ ab^{\top} ; Cauchy-Schwarz gives the value. The box gives every entry its own allowance, independent of the others, so each can be pushed to its limit with the sign chosen to make its contribution positive. The row and column budgets are ℓ 1 \ell_{1} within each row or column, and an ℓ 1 \ell_{1} allowance is always best spent entirely on the single largest entry, hence the ℓ 1 \ell_{1} - ℓ ∞ \ell_{\infty} pairing. For a shifted box, an entry ranges over [ δ m ​ n − B m ​ n , δ m ​ n + B m ​ n ] [\delta_{mn}-B_{mn},\,\delta_{mn}+B_{mn}] , whose largest magnitude is the effective half-width | δ m ​ n | + B m ​ n |\delta_{mn}|+B_{mn} ; at δ = 0 \delta=0 , this is B m ​ n B_{mn} and recovers the symmetric bound. It is slightly conservative relative to the exact shifted support function δ m ​ n ​ a m ​ b n + B m ​ n ​ | a m ​ b n | \delta_{mn}a_{m}b_{n}+B_{mn}|a_{m}b_{n}| , which keeps the sign of the center and is smaller whenever the declared offset points against the query direction. We use the effective half-width because it is sign-agnostic and needs no assumption relating δ \delta to q q .
Because the objective is linear in the free coefficients, its maximum over the box is attained at a vertex, and the expressions above give that maximum in closed form. The certificates we report are therefore exact rather than the output of a numerical search. We verified the rank-one identity numerically across all benchmarks, interventions, and admissible Δ ​ W \Delta W , with residual at machine precision.
The environment supremum.
The environment term of ( 140 ) carries its own supremum, sup Δ ​ W ∈ 𝒜 W ‖ M 𝒦 ​ A ι ′ ​ ( Δ ​ W ) ⊤ ​ q ‖ 2 \sup_{\Delta W\in\mathcal{A}_{W}}\|M_{\mathcal{K}}A_{\iota}^{\prime}(\Delta W)^{\top}q\|_{2} , because the perturbed propagator also determines how an environment shift reaches the query. Splitting A ι ′ = A ι + ( A ι ′ − A ι ) A_{\iota}^{\prime}=A_{\iota}+(A_{\iota}^{\prime}-A_{\iota}) , applying the triangle inequality, and using that M 𝒦 M_{\mathcal{K}} is a projection together with ‖ A ι ′ − A ι ‖ 2 ≤ α ι \|A_{\iota}^{\prime}-A_{\iota}\|_{2}\leq\alpha_{\iota} gives
sup Δ ​ W ∈ 𝒜 W ‖ M 𝒦 ​ A ι ′ ​ ( Δ ​ W ) ⊤ ​ q ‖ 2 ≤ ‖ M 𝒦 ​ A ι ⊤ ​ q ‖ 2 + α ι ​ ‖ q ‖ 2 . \displaystyle\sup_{\Delta W\in\mathcal{A}_{W}}\bigl\|M_{\mathcal{K}}A_{\iota}^{\prime}(\Delta W)^{\top}q\bigr\|_{2}\;\leq\;\bigl\|M_{\mathcal{K}}A_{\iota}^{\top}q\bigr\|_{2}+\alpha_{\iota}\|q\|_{2}. (207)
When the mechanism set is trivial (as on the Portland benchmark, where the shifted node is a root and 𝒜 W = { 0 } \mathcal{A}_{W}=\{0\} ), there is a single admissible Δ ​ W \Delta W : α ι = 0 \alpha_{\iota}=0 . The mechanism terms drop out of the certificate altogether, and the supremum reduces to the single evaluation ‖ M 𝒦 ​ A ι ⊤ ​ q ‖ 2 \|M_{\mathcal{K}}A_{\iota}^{\top}q\|_{2} . The directional certificate is then carried entirely by the environment term, which is why its tightness there comes from the direction-specificity of that term alone.
The general case.
The rank-one argument required the budget to sit on the incoming edges of a single node. When it spans the mechanisms of several shifted nodes, Δ ​ W \Delta W has more than one nonzero row, A ι ​ R ι ​ Δ ​ W A_{\iota}R_{\iota}\Delta W is no longer an outer product, and the higher-order terms of ( 201 ) survive. These terms describe the perturbation compounding on itself: a change at one shifted mechanism now propagates into another, which propagates further, and so on. The r r -th such term is q ⊤ ​ ( A ι ​ R ι ​ Δ ​ W ) r ​ A ι ​ v q^{\top}(A_{\iota}R_{\iota}\Delta W)^{r}A_{\iota}v . Peeling off one factor from each end,
| q ⊤ ​ ( A ι ​ R ι ​ Δ ​ W ) r ​ A ι ​ v | ≤ ‖ q ⊤ ​ A ι ​ R ι ​ Δ ​ W ‖ 2 ⏟ ≤ λ ι , q ​ ‖ A ι ​ R ι ​ Δ ​ W ‖ 2 r − 1 ⏟ ≤ γ ι r − 1 ​ ‖ A ι ​ v ‖ 2 ⏟ known , \displaystyle\bigl|q^{\top}(A_{\iota}R_{\iota}\Delta W)^{r}A_{\iota}v\bigr|\;\leq\;\underbrace{\|q^{\top}A_{\iota}R_{\iota}\Delta W\|_{2}}_{\leq\,\lambda_{\iota,q}}\;\underbrace{\|A_{\iota}R_{\iota}\Delta W\|_{2}^{\,r-1}}_{\leq\,\gamma_{\iota}^{\,r-1}}\;\underbrace{\|A_{\iota}v\|_{2}}_{\text{known}}, (208)
where γ ι \gamma_{\iota} is the amplification factor of ( 51 ) and
λ ι , q := sup Δ ​ W ∈ 𝒜 W ‖ a ⊤ ​ Δ ​ W ‖ 2 \displaystyle\lambda_{\iota,q}:=\sup_{\Delta W\in\mathcal{A}_{W}}\|a^{\top}\Delta W\|_{2} (209)
is the query-side modulus: the same object as β ι , q ​ ( v ) \beta_{\iota,q}(v) but with the input vector b b removed, so it measures the worst effect on the query direction irrespective of the signal arriving. Summing over r ≥ 2 r\geq 2 gives
m ι , q ​ ( v ) ≤ β ι , q ​ ( v ) ⏟ first order + λ ι , q ​ ‖ A ι ​ v ‖ 2 ​ ∑ r = 1 d − 2 γ ι r ⏟ higher-order remainder . \displaystyle m_{\iota,q}(v)\leq\underbrace{\beta_{\iota,q}(v)}_{\text{first order}}+\underbrace{\lambda_{\iota,q}\|A_{\iota}v\|_{2}\textstyle\sum_{r=1}^{d-2}\gamma_{\iota}^{r}}_{\text{higher-order remainder}}. (210)
The sum is finite by nilpotency; when γ ι < 1 \gamma_{\iota}<1 one may replace it with the geometric bound γ ι / ( 1 − γ ι ) \gamma_{\iota}/(1-\gamma_{\iota}) . This step is conservative: it bounds each factor by its own worst case, allowing a different Δ ​ W \Delta W for each, whereas the true supremum is over a single Δ ​ W \Delta W , making all of them large at once. No adversary can do that, so the bound overestimates the true supremum, but it is finite and computable, and the first-order term dominates whenever the perturbation is small.
The modulus λ ι , q \lambda_{\iota,q} is also a support function of the same set at the vector a a , rather than the matrix a ​ b ⊤ ab^{\top} , and admits bounds parallel to those mentioned above:
Frobenius: λ ι , q ≤ η ∥ a ∥ 2 , box: λ ι , q ≤ [ ∑ n ( ∑ m P m ​ n ( | δ m ​ n | + B m ​ n ) | a m | ) 2 ] 1 / 2 , \displaystyle\text{Frobenius:}\;\lambda_{\iota,q}\leq\eta\|a\|_{2},\quad\text{box:}\;\lambda_{\iota,q}\leq\Bigl[\textstyle\sum_{n}\bigl(\sum_{m}P_{mn}(|\delta_{mn}|+B_{mn})|a_{m}|\bigr)^{2}\Bigr]^{1/2},
row: λ ι , q ≤ ∑ m ρ m | a m | , col.: λ ι , q ≤ [ ∑ n c n 2 ∥ P : , n ⊙ a ∥ ∞ 2 ] 1 / 2 . \displaystyle\text{row:}\;\lambda_{\iota,q}\leq\textstyle\sum_{m}\rho_{m}|a_{m}|,\quad\text{col.:}\;\lambda_{\iota,q}\leq\Bigl[\textstyle\sum_{n}c_{n}^{2}\|P_{:,n}\odot a\|_{\infty}^{2}\Bigr]^{1/2}. (211)
Finally, even without the rank-one structure, the map Δ ​ W ↦ q ⊤ ​ ( A ι ′ ​ ( Δ ​ W ) − A ι ) ​ v \Delta W\mapsto q^{\top}(A^{\prime}_{\iota}(\Delta W)-A_{\iota})v remains a polynomial of degree at most d − 1 d-1 in the free coefficients since the expansion terminates. With one or two free coefficients, its supremum can therefore still be found exactly by enumerating the endpoints and the critical points of a low-degree polynomial.
Applicability to the semi-Markovian case. The certificate depends only on q q , T \operatorname{T} , and A ι , A ι ′ A_{\iota},A_{\iota}^{\prime} . The argument therefore applies whether T \operatorname{T} is diagonal (Markovian) or block-diagonal (semi-Markovian).
Appendix F Formulation-specific optimisation details
The two algorithms below instantiate the unified backbone of Algorithm 1 .
F.1 Gaussian specialisation
We first record the Gaussian specialisation, where the adversarial environment is parameterised by target moments, and the ascent step uses the smooth surrogate from Section 8.2 .
Algorithm 2 Gaussian TraCA
1: Initialise T ( 0 ) \operatorname{T}^{(0)} , Δ ​ W ( 0 ) = 0 \Delta W^{(0)}=0 , μ t ( 0 ) = μ s \mu_{t}^{(0)}=\mu_{s} , Σ t ( 0 ) = Σ s \Sigma_{t}^{(0)}=\Sigma_{s} .
2: repeat
3: // Projected gradient descent
4: for k τ k_{\tau} steps do

F.2 Empirical specialisation
We then record the empirical specialisation, where the adversarial environment is represented directly by sample perturbations, and both adversarial blocks are updated by projected ascent.
Algorithm 3 Empirical TraCA
1: Initialise T ( 0 ) \operatorname{T}^{(0)} , Δ ​ W ( 0 ) = 0 \Delta W^{(0)}=0 , Θ ( 0 ) = 0 \Theta^{(0)}=0 .
2: repeat
3: // Projected gradient descent
4: for k τ k_{\tau} steps do
5: T ( t + 1 ) ← T ( t ) − lr T ​ ∇ T F ​ ( T ( t ) , Δ ​ W ( t ) , Θ ( t ) ) \operatorname{T}^{(t+1)}\leftarrow\operatorname{T}^{(t)}-\mathrm{lr}_{\operatorname{T}}\,\nabla_{\operatorname{T}}F\bigl(\operatorname{T}^{(t)},\Delta W^{(t)},\Theta^{(t)}\bigr)
6: T ( t + 1 ) ← proj const ⁡ ( T ( t + 1 ) ) \operatorname{T}^{(t+1)}\leftarrow\proj_{\mathrm{const}}\!\bigl(\operatorname{T}^{(t+1)}\bigr) // projection to constructive class
7: end for
8: // Projected gradient ascent
9: for k adv k_{\mathrm{adv}} steps do
10: Δ ​ W ( t + 1 2 ) ← Δ ​ W ( t ) + lr adv ​ ∇ Δ ​ W F ​ ( T ( t + 1 ) , Δ ​ W ( t ) , Θ ( t ) ) \Delta W^{(\frac{t+1}{2})}\leftarrow\Delta W^{(t)}+\mathrm{lr}_{\mathrm{adv}}\,\nabla_{\Delta W}F\bigl(\operatorname{T}^{(t+1)},\Delta W^{(t)},\Theta^{(t)}\bigr)
11: Δ ​ W ( t + 1 ) ← proj 𝒜 W ⁡ ( M 𝒦 ​ Δ ​ W ( t + 1 2 ) ) \Delta W^{(t+1)}\leftarrow\proj_{\mathcal{A}_{W}}\!\bigl(M_{\mathcal{K}}\Delta W^{(\frac{t+1}{2})}\bigr) // mask + projection to ambiguity set
12: Θ ( t + 1 2 ) ← Θ ( t ) + lr adv ​ ∇ Θ F ​ ( T ( t + 1 ) , Δ ​ W ( t + 1 ) , Θ ( t ) ) \Theta^{(\frac{t+1}{2})}\leftarrow\Theta^{(t)}+\mathrm{lr}_{\mathrm{adv}}\,\nabla_{\Theta}F\bigl(\operatorname{T}^{(t+1)},\Delta W^{(t+1)},\Theta^{(t)}\bigr)
13: Θ ( t + 1 ) ← proj 𝒜 U ​ ( ε ) ⁡ ( M 𝒦 ​ Θ ( t + 1 2 ) ) \Theta^{(t+1)}\leftarrow\proj_{\mathcal{A}_{U}(\varepsilon)}\!\bigl(M_{\mathcal{K}}\Theta^{(\frac{t+1}{2})}\bigr) // mask + projection to ambiguity set
14: end for
15: until convergence
Appendix G Benchmark datasets
This appendix presents the structural causal models and intervention sets of the three synthetic benchmarks of Section 10 , together with the construction of the Portland real-world benchmark. Throughout, W W is the strictly lower triangular structural matrix in topological order, with W i ​ j W_{ij} as the weight on the edge j → i j\to i ; equivalently, row i i holds the incoming edges of node i i . Further, Table 4 summarises the configuration choices across all four benchmarks.
G.1 ATE
ATE is a two-variable semi-Markovian graph X → Y X\to Y with a bidirected edge X ↔ Y X\leftrightarrow Y (latent confounding) and a selection node on Y Y :
X = U X , Y = β ​ X + U Y , Cov ⁡ ( U X , U Y ) = ρ ≠ 0 , X=U_{X},\qquad Y=\beta X+U_{Y},\qquad\mathrm{Cov}(U_{X},U_{Y})=\rho\neq 0, (212)
with source coefficient β s = 1.0 \beta_{s}=1.0 , ρ = 0.6 \rho=0.6 , exogenous means μ U X = 0.5 \mu_{U_{X}}=0.5 and μ U Y = 0.3 \mu_{U_{Y}}=0.3 , and unit exogenous standard deviations σ U X = σ U Y = 1.0 \sigma_{U_{X}}=\sigma_{U_{Y}}=1.0 . The confounded pair { X , Y } \{X,Y\} forms a single district, so the constructive map acts as a free 2 × 2 2\times 2 block on that district rather than as a diagonal map. The designated shifted node is Y Y , whose mechanism is the incoming coefficient β = w X → Y \beta=w_{X\to Y} together with its exogenous noise. The target differs from the source in this coefficient alone, with β t = 1.5 \beta_{t}=1.5 ; all other parameters are shared. The two reported queries are therefore 𝔼 ⁡ [ Y ∣ d ​ o ​ ( X = 0 ) ] = μ U Y = 0.30 \mathbb{E}[Y\mid do(X{=}0)]=\mu_{U_{Y}}=0.30 , identical in both domains since the shifted coefficient multiplies zero, and 𝔼 ⁡ [ Y ∣ d ​ o ​ ( X = 1 ) ] = β t + μ U Y = 1.80 \mathbb{E}[Y\mid do(X{=}1)]=\beta_{t}+\mu_{U_{Y}}=1.80 on the target against 1.30 1.30 on the source. The intervention set is:
ℐ ATE = { ∅ , do ⁡ ( X = 0 ) , do ⁡ ( X = 1 ) } . \mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{ATE}}=\bigl\{\emptyset,\ \mathrm{do}(X{=}0),\ \mathrm{do}(X{=}1)\bigr\}. (213)
G.2 ATCE
ATCE is a three-variable confounded-treatment graph with Z Z an age-like covariate, X X a treatment, and Y Y an outcome:
Z = U Z , X = α ​ Z + U X , Y = β ​ X + γ ​ Z + U Y , Z=U_{Z},\qquad X=\alpha Z+U_{X},\qquad Y=\beta X+\gamma Z+U_{Y}, (214)
with α = 0.3 \alpha=0.3 , β = 1.0 \beta=1.0 , γ = 0.5 \gamma=0.5 and standard Gaussian exogenous noise ( μ = 0 \mu=0 , σ = 1 \sigma=1 for all three nodes). The intervention set is
ℐ ATCE = { ∅ , do ⁡ ( X = 0 ) , do ⁡ ( X = 1 ) } . \mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{ATCE}}=\bigl\{\emptyset,\ \mathrm{do}(X{=}0),\ \mathrm{do}(X{=}1)\bigr\}. (215)
The designated shifted nodes are the root Z Z and the outcome Y Y . The root Z Z is shifted only through its environment since it has no incoming structural mechanism. The non-root outcome Y Y is shifted through both its incoming structural coefficients w Z → Y , w X → Y w_{Z\to Y},w_{X\to Y} and its exogenous noise.
G.3 LiLUCAS
The LUng CAncer dataset (LUCAS) 2 2 2 http://www.causality.inf.ethz.ch/data/LUCAS.html was originally designed to simulate realistic causal relationships in lung cancer diagnosis. For our purposes, we leverage the linear variant of it: Linearised LUCAS (LiLUCAS) used in ( Felekis et al., 2026 ) . LiLUCAS is a six-variable Markovian DAG with roots Smoking , Genetics , and Allergy , and downstream variables LungCancer , Coughing , and Fatigue :
Smoking = U S , Genetics = U G , Allergy = U A , \displaystyle\text{Smoking}=U_{S},\qquad\text{Genetics}=U_{G},\qquad\text{Allergy}=U_{A}, (216)
LungCancer = 0.9 ​ Smoking + 0.8 ​ Genetics + U L , \displaystyle\text{LungCancer}=0.9\,\text{Smoking}+0.8\,\text{Genetics}+U_{L},
Coughing = 0.6 ​ LungCancer + 0.4 ​ Allergy + U C , \displaystyle\text{Coughing}=0.6\,\text{LungCancer}+0.4\,\text{Allergy}+U_{C},
Fatigue = 0.9 ​ LungCancer + 0.5 ​ Coughing + U F , \displaystyle\text{Fatigue}=0.9\,\text{LungCancer}+0.5\,\text{Coughing}+U_{F},
with Gaussian exogenous noise of means μ = [ 0 , 0 , 0.1 , 0.1 , 0.3 , 0.2 ] \mu=[0,0,0.1,0.1,0.3,0.2] and standard deviations σ = [ 0.5 , 2.0 , 1.5 , 1.0 , 0.8 , 1.2 ] \sigma=[0.5,2.0,1.5,1.0,0.8,1.2] in topological order. The designated shifted node is LungCancer , whose mechanism comprises its incoming edges from Smoking and Genetics ( w 𝑆𝑚 → 𝐿𝐶 w_{\mathit{Sm}\to\mathit{LC}} , w 𝐺𝑒 → 𝐿𝐶 w_{\mathit{Ge}\to\mathit{LC}} ) together with its noise. The intervention set consists of the observational regime, the four single-node interventions do ⁡ ( 𝑆𝑚 = v ) \mathrm{do}(\mathit{Sm}{=}v) and do ⁡ ( 𝐺𝑒 = v ) \mathrm{do}(\mathit{Ge}{=}v) for v ∈ { 0 , 1 } v\in\{0,1\} , and the four joint interventions do ⁡ ( 𝑆𝑚 = v 1 , 𝐺𝑒 = v 2 ) \mathrm{do}(\mathit{Sm}{=}v_{1},\mathit{Ge}{=}v_{2}) for v 1 , v 2 ∈ { 0 , 1 } v_{1},v_{2}\in\{0,1\} , giving | ℐ LiLUCAS | = 9 \lvert\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{LiLUCAS}}\rvert=9 .
G.4 Portland urban streams
The Portland benchmark ( Tabell et al., 2026 ) is a real-world domain-shift problem drawn from urban stream ecology. The scientific question is whether riparian tree-canopy cover ( X X ) causally affects dissolved oxygen ( Y Y ) in urban streams, and whether an effect estimated on observed source watersheds transports to a held-out target watershed, Fanno Creek, whose environmental context differs from that of the source. The causal mechanisms are assumed to be invariant across watersheds while the distribution of environmental covariates shifts, making this a natural test of transport under a real, unmanufactured covariate shift.
Data sources.
The benchmark is built by joining two public datasets. Seasonal water-quality field measurements from the Environmental Data Initiative (EDI) provide dissolved oxygen, water temperature, and site and season identifiers for urban stream sites in Portland, Oregon. Site-level landscape covariates from USGS ScienceBase provide elevation, mean annual precipitation, mean annual temperature, and riparian tree-canopy cover from the 2021 canopy layer. The two sources are merged by the shared site identifier, attaching the static landscape covariates to each site and season observation. The original study and its analysis code are publicly available. 3 3 3 https://github.com/ottotabell/transportability-ecology
Cleaning.
We reproduce the original study’s cleaning pipeline: rows missing key variables are removed ( − 1 -1 ); the Columbia Slough watershed is dropped as hydrologically distinct from the remaining sites ( − 60 -60 ); sites with zero canopy cover are removed ( − 3 -3 ); duplicate site season records are collapsed to the first observation ( − 2 -2 ); and a single extreme dissolved-oxygen outlier is removed ( − 1 -1 ). The final benchmark contains 347 observations across four seasonal rounds and 87 87 unique sites.
Source/target split.
Following the original study, Fanno Creek is the designated target domain ( N = 100 N=100 ) and all remaining watersheds form the source domain ( N = 247 N=247 ). The source provides the full joint distribution p ⁡ ( y , x , s , z ∣ source ) p(y,x,s,z\mid\text{source}) , while the target contributes only its observational covariate distribution p ∗ ​ ( z ) p^{\ast}(z) .
Causal structure.
The modelled variables are a one-dimensional environmental summary Z = PC 1 ​ ( elevation, precipitation, mean temperature ) Z=\mathrm{PC}_{1}(\text{elevation, precipitation, mean temperature}) , the season S S , the canopy cover X X , and the dissolved oxygen Y Y ; all are standardised using source statistics, with the PCA fitted on source data only. The back-door view is the linear-Gaussian SCM
Z = U Z , S = U S , X = w Z ​ X ​ Z + U X , Y = w Z ​ Y ​ Z + w S ​ Y ​ S + w X ​ Y ​ X + U Y , Z=U_{Z},\quad S=U_{S},\quad X=w_{ZX}Z+U_{X},\quad Y=w_{ZY}Z+w_{SY}S+w_{XY}X+U_{Y}, (217)
with edge weights fitted by ordinary least squares on source data. The designated shifted node is the root Z Z , carrying the environmental covariate shift between watersheds; as a root, it has no incoming edges, so its mechanism budget is structurally empty, and the shift is carried entirely by Z Z ’s exogenous distribution. The constructive map is Markovian with a single free entry τ Z \tau_{Z} . The intervention set consists of the observational regime together with four canopy-cover interventions.
ℐ Portland = { ∅ , do ⁡ ( X = 45 ) , do ⁡ ( X = 50 ) , do ⁡ ( X = 55 ) , do ⁡ ( X = 60 ) } , \mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{Portland}}=\bigl\{\emptyset,\ \mathrm{do}(X{=}45),\ \mathrm{do}(X{=}50),\ \mathrm{do}(X{=}55),\ \mathrm{do}(X{=}60)\bigr\}, (218)
with canopy values in percent, standardised to source units before injection into the SCM.
